infinite cardinals below $\kappa$.

On a subset of $A$ of cardinality $\kappa$, place $\lambda_i$ disjoint
copies of $G/H_i$ for each $i<\delta$.  Fix the remaining atoms.  The
selected type block $B_i$ has size $\lambda_i$, since each $G/H_i$ is
nonempty and countable, and the selected blocks together have size
$\kappa$.  The remainder has trivial type and cannot enlarge any $B_i$.
Thus the small-type union violates the criterion in
Theorem~\ref{nee:ca:thm:profile}.

Explicitly, a pure sequence of the selected type codes is an internal
parameter.  The formula sending $i$ to the set of all atoms having the
$i$th type has a unique internal value $B_i$ for every $i<\delta$.
Replacement for this function would produce a range set whose kernel
contains their union of size $\kappa$.  This is the required failed
instance, on the pure internal domain $\delta$.

At $\kappa=\omega$, a finite group has only finitely many transitive
types, all finite.  Its union of finite type blocks is finite, so the
finite-cutoff clause of Theorem~\ref{nee:ca:thm:profile} applies.  If $G$ is
infinite, place one regular $G$-orbit on a countable atom subset and fix
the remainder.  From an atom in that orbit, endpoints of finite words in
the generators and inverses are uniquely definable.  Every finite path
graph exists internally.  Replacement on the pure set of words would
collect the entire infinite orbit into an internal atom set, impossible
at the finite-kernel cutoff.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[The finite cutoff in the repository, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
The clause for $\kappa=\omega$ also follows from Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets}, written after this manuscript's pin: by its Proposition~62.1 (\texttt{hset:ns:prop:finitewords}) the generator names define the uniform action, and its Theorem~58.1 (\texttt{hset:ns:thm:full}) then requires finite orbits and a finite exceptional set, which every action has exactly when $G$ is finite. The clause for $\kappa>\omega$, the threshold $\cf(\kappa)>\tau(G)$, is new here.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{The three possible type counts}

\begin{proposition}[Cardinality trichotomy]\label{nee:ca:grp:spectrum}
For a countable group $G$, the cardinal $\tau(G)$ is finite,
$\aleph_0$, or $\cont$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Represent a subgroup by its characteristic function in $2^G$.  The
subgroup conditions are closed: failure to contain the identity or to
be closed under products and inverses has a finite witness.  Thus the
space of subgroups is a closed subspace of the compact metrizable space
$2^G$.  If uncountable, it has a nonempty perfect subset and therefore
cardinality $\cont$.  For clarity, the perfect-set step follows by
repeatedly splitting basic clopen neighborhoods around distinct
condensation points; the resulting binary tree embeds $2^{\NN}$.
Only countably many points can fail to be condensation points, since
the space has a countable base.  Every conjugacy class of subgroups is
countable, being an orbit of $G$.  Consequently an uncountable subgroup
space has $\cont$ conjugacy classes as well.  The remaining possibilities
are finite or countably infinite.
\end{proof}

For an infinite finitely generated abelian group the count is
$\aleph_0$: every subgroup is finitely generated, so a countable collection
of finite tuples lists them, and infinitely many finite-index subgroups
give distinct types.  The same upper-bound argument applies whenever
every subgroup of a countable group is finitely generated.  At the other
extreme, the free group $F(a,b)$ has $\cont$ types.  The elements
$a^n b a^{-n}$, $n\in\ZZ$, freely generate a subgroup with a countable
free basis.  Indeed, after combining adjacent equal basis indices, expansion of a nonempty
reduced word leaves nonzero powers of $a$ between consecutive nonzero powers of $b$,
so it cannot reduce to the identity.  Distinct subsets of this basis
generate distinct subgroups, giving $\cont$ subgroups; quotienting by
the countable conjugacy classes preserves that cardinality.

\begin{remark}[Infinite groups with finitely many types]\label{nee:ca:grp:exception}
The finite alternative cannot be identified with finiteness of $G$.
Smith records finitely generated infinite simple groups constructed by
Ivanov and Ol'shanskii in which all proper nontrivial subgroups are
conjugate~\cite[p.~69]{Smith1995}.  Such a group has $\tau(G)=3$.
We use this published existence statement and do not reproduce that
construction.  Theorem~\ref{nee:ca:grp:robust} then says that every named
generator action preserves Replacement at every uncountable cutoff,
while a regular orbit still defeats it at $\kappa=\omega$.  This is why
the finite-cutoff clause must be stated separately.
\end{remark}

\begin{writenote}[An imported existence theorem, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
This remark is not proved here: it imports the existence of a finitely generated infinite group all of whose proper nontrivial subgroups are conjugate, a construction of Ivanov and Ol'shanskii~\cite{IO1991} that the manuscript read only through Smith~\cite{Smith1995}. At the write, the first page of~\cite{Smith1995}, freely visible on the publisher's site, was checked: it attributes to Ivanov and Ol'shanskii finitely generated infinite simple groups with all proper nontrivial subgroups conjugate. The primary source (paywalled) was not consulted, by the manuscript or at the write. Given the existence statement, $\tau(G)=3$ is immediate: the classes are $\{1\}$, $G$, and the single class of proper nontrivial subgroups, which is nonempty because an infinite simple group is not cyclic. What depends on the import is only the claim that the two clauses of \cref{nee:ca:grp:robust} genuinely differ, that is, that an infinite finitely generated group can have finite $\tau(G)$; \cref{nee:ca:grp:robust} and \cref{nee:ca:grp:spectrum} are proved without it. \Cref{nee:ca:q:primary} records the missing primary check.
\end{writenote}

The topology of subgroup spaces has a much deeper established theory,
including a complete analysis for countable abelian groups in
\cite{CGP2010}. Only the elementary cardinality fact above is needed here.

These results translate group structure into preservation properties of
a set-theoretic scheme.  They do not classify groups with countably many
subgroups or claim to solve the existing classification problems about
subgroup spaces.  Their role is to identify exactly which invariant of
a fixed finitely generated action controls this family of kernel models.

\section{Uniformly evaluating an infinite family of automorphisms}
\label{nee:ca:uni:section}

There are two different ways to name an action of a countable group.
In the first, the language has a separate unary symbol $J_g$ for each
$g\in G$.  Every first-order formula still contains only finitely many
of these symbols.  In the second, one binary symbol $E$ uniformly
evaluates the action:
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ca:uni:eq:evaluation}
 E(g,x)=j_{\rho(g)}(x)\qquad(g\in G).
\end{equation}
Set $E(g,x)=x$ when $g\notin G$ to make it total, and allow the pure set
$G$ as an internal parameter.  Now a single formula can quantify over
all $g\in G$.  Although the individual maps in the two descriptions
are identical, their Replacement schemes need not agree.

\begin{proposition}[Separate names for a locally finite group]
\label{nee:ca:uni:separate}
If $G$ is locally finite, every action of $G$ preserves Replacement in
the language of separate unary names at every infinite cutoff.
For countable $G$, the converse holds for universal preservation at
$\kappa=\omega$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A formula uses finitely many names.  They generate a finite subgroup
when $G$ is locally finite, so Theorem~\ref{nee:ca:grp:robust} applies to that
formula.  Conversely, if a finite subset generates an infinite subgroup
$K$, a regular $G$-orbit on countably many atoms restricts to an infinite
$K$-orbit.  The finite-word endpoint instance using those finitely many
names fails at $\kappa=\omega$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform group profile]\label{nee:ca:uni:profile}
For a countable group acting with a uniform evaluation name, the
component-profile criterion of Theorem~\ref{nee:ca:thm:profile} holds with
components equal to full $G$-orbits and types equal to conjugacy classes
of stabilizers.  Consequently the universal robustness criterion
\eqref{nee:ca:grp:eq:robust} holds for every countable group in this language,
without a finite-generation assumption.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use pure codes for actions of the pure set $G$ on a positive finite
ordinal or $\NN$.  An internal enumeration graph $h$ recognizes a
component by requiring
$h(g\cdot d)=E(g,h(d))$ for every $g\in G$ and every code element $d$.
Transitivity of the code and inclusion of the chosen atom identify
the image with its whole orbit.  This is one formula precisely because
evaluation is uniform.  The graph is internal at uncountable cutoffs,
and for finite orbits at the finite cutoff.

The necessity argument therefore collects the small type blocks just
as before.  At $\kappa=\omega$, the uniformly defined function
$g\mapsto E(g,a)$ first forces every orbit to be finite.  For
sufficiency, saturate the kernels of the input set and parameters
under $G$, and adjoin the small-type part.  This protected union has
size below $\kappa$: countable saturation preserves that bound when
$\kappa>\omega$, while saturation of finitely many atoms is finite
when all orbits are finite.  A fresh equivariant component swap fixes
the protected union and changes any purported unique output using an
atom outside it.  Its lift preserves $E$, so uniqueness confines all
output kernels to the protected union.  External Replacement then
produces an internal range.  Finally the sharp type-block construction
in Theorem~\ref{nee:ca:grp:robust} uses only countability of $G$ and applies
unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[Prior in the repository at the finite cutoff, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
At $\kappa=\omega$ both propositions are in Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets}, written after this manuscript's pin: \cref{nee:ca:uni:separate} is its finite-symbol principle, Proposition~62.2 (\texttt{hset:ns:prop:language}), together with Corollary~58.2 (\texttt{hset:ns:cor:finitegroup}); \cref{nee:ca:uni:profile} is its Theorem~58.1 (\texttt{hset:ns:thm:full}), stated there for a uniformly named action of an arbitrary pure set-sized group, its exceptional set $X_{\mathsf{Act}}$ being $D_\omega$ once all orbits are finite. The proofs are independent. For $\kappa>\omega$ both propositions are new here; \cref{nee:ca:uni:profile} answers in part that Part's Question~65.2 (\texttt{hset:ns:q:kernelbounds}), whose dated note lists ``actions of arbitrary pure groups at uncountable $\kappa$'' as open: for countable groups and full Replacement the criterion is the profile criterion with full orbits, and the universal threshold is $\cf(\kappa)>\tau(G)$. Uncountable groups and the bounded levels $\lambda<\kappa$ of that question remain open.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{A separation using only two-atom orbits}

Take
\[
 G=\bigoplus_{n<\omega}C_2,
\]
coded as the pure set of finite subsets of $\NN$ with symmetric
difference.  This group is locally finite.  For each nonempty
$S\subseteq\NN$, its nonzero character
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ca:uni:eq:character}
 \chi_S(F)=|F\cap S|\pmod2
\end{equation}
defines a transitive action on $\{0,1\}$ by
$F\cdot\varepsilon=\varepsilon+\chi_S(F)\pmod2$.
Distinct characters have distinct kernels: an index-two subgroup has
a unique nonzero quotient character to $C_2$.  Because $G$ is abelian,
these kernels are never identified by conjugacy.  There are therefore
$\cont$ different two-point transitive types.

\begin{theorem}[An amenable uniform name can change Replacement]
\label{nee:ca:uni:two_point}
For every infinite $\kappa$ with $\cf(\kappa)\leq\cont$, there is an
action of $G=\bigoplus_{n<\omega}C_2$ on $A$, all of whose orbits have
size at most two, such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item naming every individual lift separately preserves Replacement;
\item naming the uniform evaluation function $E$ violates Replacement;
\item $E$ is amenable: its restriction graph on every internal set of
input pairs belongs to $\Mk$.
\end{enumerate}
For $\cf(\kappa)>\cont$, every uniformly named action of this group
preserves Replacement.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At $\kappa>\omega$, let $\delta=\cf(\kappa)$ and choose distinct
nonempty codes $S_i$, $i<\delta$.  Choose small infinite multiplicities
$\lambda_i$ with sum $\kappa$.  Put $\lambda_i$ copies of the two-point
action of $\chi_{S_i}$ in a block $B_i$, and fix the remaining atoms.
Then $|B_i|=\lambda_i<\kappa$, whereas the union of the $B_i$ has size
$\kappa$.  At $\kappa=\omega$, take one copy of each of countably many
distinct nonzero characters; each $B_i$ then has size two.

In the uniform language the formula
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ca:uni:eq:recognition}
 \At(a)\ \wedge\ \forall g\in G\,
 \bigl(E(g,a)=a\ \longleftrightarrow\ \chi_S(g)=0\bigr)
\end{equation}
recognizes the type of $\chi_S$.  Its arithmetic character computation
is pure and uniformly definable.  A pure sequence of the selected
$S_i$ therefore defines the unique internal block $B_i$ from $i$.
Any Replacement range for these values would have a kernel reaching
the cutoff.  This proves failure even though no orbit is infinite.
Preservation for the separate-name expansion follows from
Proposition~\ref{nee:ca:uni:separate}.

For amenability, let $w$ be an internal set of input pairs and put
$P=\kerat(w)$.  The kernel of any value $E(g,x)$ appearing on $w$ is
contained in the saturation $GP$.  Since every orbit has at most two
atoms, $|GP|\leq2|P|<\kappa$, with the evident finite interpretation.
The externally constructed restriction graph has kernel contained in
$GP$ and is therefore internal.  Finally $\tau(G)=\cont$ by the
two-point types and the general continuum upper bound, so
Proposition~\ref{nee:ca:uni:profile} gives the asserted positive threshold.
\end{proof}

The witness here does more than expose an unavailable infinite orbit.
All individual named maps are commuting involutions, every full orbit
has at most two atoms, and the uniform evaluation class is itself
amenable.  The failed instance uses the ability of one formula to test
all group indices and distinguish their stabilizers.  Replacing
infinitely many separate names by a uniform evaluator is thus a
substantive change to an axiom scheme.

\begin{writenote}[Relation to Part~VIII of the surreal report, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
Theorem~62.3 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:thm:evaluator}), written after this manuscript's pin, makes the same separation for the same group at $\kappa=\omega$: countably many separately named involutions keep Replacement, and one uniform evaluator $T(i,a,b)$ destroys it. Its witness is the regular action of $\bigoplus_\omega C_2$ on atoms labelled by the group, whose orbit is infinite; the evaluator with one atom parameter then collects infinitely many single atoms, so already $\mathsf R_1$ (Replacement for outputs with at most one atom) fails. \Cref{nee:ca:uni:two_point} differs in four ways: every orbit has at most two atoms, so the failure comes from distinguishing stabilizers rather than from an infinite orbit; it holds at every $\kappa$ with $\cf(\kappa)\leq\cont$, not only at $\omega$; the evaluator is amenable; and the converse for $\cf(\kappa)>\cont$ is included. At $\kappa=\omega$ the two witnesses are also separated by the graded levels of that Part: for the witness above, every orbit is finite, and at most one atom (a fixed atom, when it is the only fixed one) has an orbit of size at most one under the centralizer of the action, because the transposition of a two-atom orbit commutes with the action; so by its Theorem~59.1 (\texttt{hset:ns:thm:graded}; its language is interdefinable with the evaluator $E$ by its Proposition~62.4) $\mathsf R_1$ holds, while $\mathsf R_2$ fails, since each of the infinitely many two-atom orbits is the only orbit of its type and so has centralizer orbits of size two. This last observation is the write's, by that theorem, and is not in either manuscript.
\end{writenote}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform evaluation for a finitely generated group]
\label{nee:ca:uni:fg}
If $G$ is finitely generated, its uniform evaluation function is definable
from the finitely many generator names and a pure parameter.  Adjoining
that evaluator therefore does not change whether Replacement holds.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose a pure function assigning to every $g\in G$ a finite word in the
generators and their inverses representing $g$.  To define $E(g,x)$,
ask for the endpoint of a finite path starting at $x$ and following
that word.  A positive letter applies its named lift; a negative
letter requires the named lift of the next value to equal the previous
value.  These steps are unique because the lifts are automorphisms.

Every such finite path graph exists internally.  Its finitely many
values have kernels which are images of $\kerat(x)$ under group elements,
so their union has size below $\kappa$, even when $\kappa$ is singular
or equals $\omega$.  The defining formula thus uses only the finite
generator language and the pure word-selector parameter; it assumes
no expanded Replacement.  Substituting this definition into an
evaluation-language Replacement instance translates it to an instance
in the generator language.  Conversely each generator is an evaluation
at one fixed pure group element.
\end{proof}

This proposition specifies the boundary of the preceding counterexample.
The issue is not merely that an index variable occurs in the notation.
For finitely generated actions a finite path can evaluate every index
using fixed named operations, and its existence is already guaranteed
by the kernel bound.  For $\bigoplus_{n<\omega}C_2$, no finite family of
group elements generates the full action; a formula in separate names
has access only to a finite subgroup, while the uniform predicate
\eqref{nee:ca:uni:eq:recognition} compares all its indices at once.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~93]
At $\kappa=\omega$, \cref{nee:ca:uni:fg} is Proposition~62.1 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:prop:finitewords}), stated there for the words of a pure free group on finitely many named permutations and written after this manuscript's pin; here it holds at every cutoff.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Finite orbits and the profinite completion}

There is an exact version of this phenomenon for any countable group.
Define
\[
 \tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)=
 \bigl|\{H\leq G:[G:H]<\omega\}/\text{conjugacy}\bigr|.
\]

\begin{corollary}[Universal preservation for finite-orbit actions]
\label{nee:ca:uni:finite_orbit}
Restrict to actions of a countable group $G$ whose every orbit is
finite, and name their uniform evaluation function.  Every such action
preserves Replacement exactly under the conditions
\[
 \begin{cases}
 \tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)\text{ is finite},&\kappa=\omega,\\
 \cf(\kappa)>\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G),&\kappa>\omega.
 \end{cases}
\]
At the finite cutoff the condition is equivalent to finiteness of the
profinite completion $\widehat G$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The uniform profile proof now uses only finite transitive types.
At uncountable cutoffs its upper bound and sharp block construction
apply with $\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)$.  At the finite cutoff, finitely many
types give a finite union of finite blocks.  Infinitely many types
allow one copy of each of countably many distinct nontrivial types,
whose finite blocks have infinite union.

A finite-index subgroup has finitely many conjugates, since its
normalizer contains it.  Hence finite $\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)$ is
equivalent to having finitely many finite-index subgroups.  Intersect
their normal cores to obtain a finite-index normal subgroup $R$
contained in every such subgroup.  Every finite quotient factors
through $G/R$, so $\widehat G=G/R$ is finite.  Conversely, each
finite-index subgroup is the inverse image of an open subgroup of
$\widehat G$: its finite coset action extends to the profinite
completion, and one takes the stabilizer of its distinguished coset.
If $\widehat G$ is finite there are only finitely many such subgroups.
\end{proof}

For a finitely generated group there are only finitely many actions on
each fixed finite labelled set: the images of finitely many generators
determine the action.  Thus $\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)$ is at most countable.
The continuum-sized family of two-point types above relies on infinitely
many generators and their uniform evaluation.  This explains precisely
why its language distinction has no counterpart for a fixed finite
generating tuple.

\section{Collection and reflection in the commuting cases}\label{nee:ca:sec:reflection}

The preceding results concern Replacement. The relation to Glazer and Yao's research becomes clearer when Collection and reflection are kept distinct. In the present external-choice models, the general Collection and full-reflection criteria were already established in \cite[Theorems 7.5 and 7.7]{RepoNaming}. We record and prove the consequence for the countable-type commuting signatures.

\begin{theorem}[Collection and reflection for at most one injection]\label{nee:ca:thm:commuting-collection}
Fix a finite nonempty commuting tuple of injections in which all but at most one map are permutations. In its canonical expansion of $\Mk$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item full expanded Collection;
\item for every finite family $\Phi$ of expanded formulas and every internal set $w$, there is an internal transitive set $t$ with $w\subseteq t$, closed under the named functions, such that every formula in $\Phi$ is absolute between $t$ and the full model for all parameter tuples from $t$;
\item $\kappa$ is an uncountable successor cardinal.
\end{enumerate}
This is a consequence of the prior Collection/reflection criterion and the type classification proved here.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Failure at a limit cutoff.}
Suppose $\kappa$ is a limit cardinal, with $\omega$ included. Choose a pure set $w$ of ordinals below $\kappa$ whose cardinalities are unbounded below $\kappa$. For $\omega$, take $w=\omega$. For each $\alpha\in w$ there is an internal graph of an injection from $\alpha$ into the atoms: it uses only $|\alpha|<\kappa$ atoms. A set collecting such graphs for all $\alpha\in w$ would have a kernel of size at least $|\alpha|$ for every $\alpha\in w$, contrary to the cutoff. This is a failed base-language Collection instance, so adding names cannot repair it.

\emph{A common reservoir at a successor cutoff.}
Write $\kappa=\nu^+$, with $\nu$ infinite. There are at most countably many component types, and every component is countable. Close the kernels of an internal input domain $w$ and its parameters under components; this uses at most $\nu$ atoms. Add every entire type block of size at most $\nu$. There are only countably many such blocks, so the resulting component-closed set $P^*$ still has size at most $\nu$.

For each remaining realized type, choose $\nu$ components disjoint from $P^*$. Such a type has at least $\nu^+$ components. Their union with $P^*$ is a fixed component-closed reservoir $H$ of size at most $\nu$. If $y$ is any internal witness, its kernel meets at most $\nu$ components outside $P^*$. For each type, match those components injectively into its reservoir. Because the full component family has size at least $\nu^+$, the partial matching extends to a permutation of that family; choose the corresponding component isomorphisms. The resulting atom permutation commutes with all names and fixes $P^*$ pointwise. It carries $y$ to a witness whose kernel lies in $H$ while fixing every input and parameter.

For a fixed total relation on $w$, external Collection and Choice now supply witnesses all supported on the same $H$. Their collected set is internal. This proves full expanded Collection independently of the cardinality of the pure part of $w$.

\emph{Reflection.}
For a finite family of formulas, include its subformulas, expressing universal quantifiers through negation and existence. Construct externally increasing sets
\[
X_n=V_{\alpha_n}(H_n)\qquad(n<\omega),
\]
where $H_n$ is component-closed of size at most $\nu$, the $\alpha_n$ increase, and the initial stage contains the desired parameters and elements of the prescribed internal set. Given $X_n$, the reservoir construction over $H_n$ compresses all needed witnesses while fixing their parameter tuples. There are only set-many tuples in $X_n$ and finitely many existential subformulas. External Collection gives a common rank bound, producing $H_{n+1},\alpha_{n+1}$ such that each true existential instance over $X_n$ has a witness in $X_{n+1}$.

The union $t=\bigcup_n X_n$ is transitive, is closed under each named function, and has kernel of size at most $\nu$, so it is internal. Induction on the finite collection of formulas proves absoluteness between the full model and $t$. The existential step is precisely the witness property; the atomic step uses transitivity and closure under the named functions.

Conversely full reflection implies Collection by reflecting a totality assertion and its matrix. For a domain $w$ and parameter tuple $p$, prescribe an internal set containing $w$ itself and all the entries of $p$, so that these objects belong to the reflecting set $t$. Transitivity then puts every element of $w$ in $t$. The witnesses supplied in $t$ are genuine witnesses by absoluteness, and $t$ itself is an internal collecting set. This completes the equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The reservoir must grow]
Reflection uses increasing atom reservoirs as well as increasing rank bounds. A single $V_\alpha(H)$ can contain its whole atom supply $H$ as one of its elements, whereas the full kernel model has no internal set of all atoms. The growing-reservoir proof avoids incorrectly treating one fixed atom supply as a reflecting universe.
\end{remark}

These results give several distinct possibilities. At an uncountable successor cutoff, every commuting-permutation expansion has full Replacement, Collection, and the specified full reflection. At a limit cardinal of uncountable cofinality, every such expansion still has Replacement, but Collection and full reflection fail. At a limit cardinal of countable cofinality, Replacement depends on the specific action's small-block profile, and Collection always fails.

This does not recover or strengthen the choiceless separation theorem of Glazer and Yao. Their work varies choice and reflection principles in a broader collection of models. Here the ambient construction uses Choice, and the new classification varies the named action and the atom cutoff. The connection is the common role of atom symmetries in deciding whether witnesses can be confined to one internal set.

\section{Forcing stability for a fixed finite action}\label{nee:ca:sec:forcing}

The universal thresholds quantify over all available actions, so they can change when a forcing extension supplies new actions. For an action already present in the ground universe, the profile theorem yields a stronger stability statement. This answers the fixed-action part of the forcing question proposed in the repository \cite[Question 10.10]{RepoNaming} under an explicit hypothesis on the cutoff.

\begin{theorem}[Preserving the cutoff preserves the verdict]\label{nee:ca:thm:forcing}
Let $V\subseteq W=V[\mathcal G]$ be a set-forcing extension of the ambient universe. Suppose $A$, an infinite cardinal $\kappa$, and a fixed finite tuple $\boldsymbol f$ of injections on $A$ belong to $V$, with $|A|^V\geq\kappa$. Assume that the ground ordinal $\kappa$ remains a cardinal in $W$. Form the kernel model and the canonical lifts separately in $V$ and in $W$. Then the ground expansion satisfies expanded Replacement if and only if the rebuilt expansion in $W$ does.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The atom set and the named maps have the same elements and values in both universes. A component is determined by finite paths through these values, so each component is the same ground set in $V$ and $W$. It is countable in $V$.

We next show that the partition into component types does not change. Fix two ground components and ground enumerations of them. Their labelled isomorphisms correspond to infinite branches through a ground tree of finite partial isomorphisms, requiring at the $n$th stage that the first $n$ enumerated elements on both sides be covered. A ground isomorphism remains one in $W$. If there is no ground isomorphism, this tree is well-founded in $V$ and has an ordinal-valued rank function there. The same rank function remains valid in $W$ and precludes a new infinite branch. Thus no two previously different component types become isomorphic. Indexing types by the actual partition of components, rather than by all possible pure codes, gives the same blocks $B_t$ in both universes.

For any ground set $X$, the assertion $|X|<\kappa$ is absolute under the stated cardinal hypothesis. A ground injection of $X$ into an ordinal below $\kappa$ persists and proves the forward direction. If $|X|^V\geq\kappa$, a ground injection of $\kappa$ into $X$ persists and, because $\kappa$ is still a cardinal, prevents $|X|^W<\kappa$.

Consequently the family of small blocks $B_t$ is unchanged, its union $D_\kappa$ is the same ground set, and the smallness of that union is unchanged. At $\kappa=\omega$, the finiteness of every component and of $D_\omega$ is also absolute. Apply \cref{nee:ca:thm:profile} in the two universes. The models have different pure sets in general, but the criterion applies to all formulas and all internal parameters in each rebuilt model, which is exactly the required equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[Question~10.10, fixed-action part, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
``Question~10.10'' of~\cite{RepoNaming} is \cref{nee:q:forcing} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}; a dated note there records this answer and what remains open (\cref{nee:ca:q:forcing}).
\end{writenote}

\begin{remark}[What can still change]
The theorem permits a change of $\cf(\kappa)$ as long as $\kappa$ remains a cardinal. If such a change crosses a universal threshold, counterexample actions available in the extension need not be ground actions. The theorem does not assert that the old proper-class model is unchanged, that each old formula has the same truth value, or that Collection is absolute under arbitrary cardinal collapses below $\kappa$. For the countable-type commuting signatures of \cref{nee:ca:thm:commuting-collection}, preserving whether $\kappa$ is a successor cardinal additionally preserves Collection and the stated reflection scheme; forcing that preserves all cardinals is sufficient.
\end{remark}

\section{Formalization, verification, and proof dependencies}\label{nee:ca:sec:verification}

\subsection{How the argument fits the existing ProveIt formalization}

The inspected repository contains a formula-indexed ZF development at
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/ZF/Lean/ZF/Zf.lean}. Its declarations include
\texttt{Sep\_form}, \texttt{Func\_form}, \texttt{Image\_form},
\texttt{Repl\_form}, \texttt{ZFax}, and \texttt{ZFprov} \cite{RepoZFca}.
They provide a natural starting point for distinguishing a base formula language from the enlarged language used by a scheme. They do not already formalize the urelement class models, component profiles, or theorems of this article.

A useful formalization should make the signature an explicit input. A proof of Replacement for formulas in $\{\in,\At\}$ must not automatically become a proof for formulas in $\{\in,\At,J_1,\ldots,J_r\}$. Similarly, an interface for amenability should assert the existence of individual restriction graphs and should not quietly include expanded Replacement.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{0.26\linewidth}L{0.65\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Milestone & Mathematical content to certify \\
\midrule
Syntax and interpretation & Separate atom and set predicates; finite function signatures; relativization of each schema instance. \\[5pt]
The kernel model & Tagged universe, transitive closure, kernel operations, closure under subsets, and the fresh-atom proof of base Replacement. \\[5pt]
Finite paths and lifts & Recursive lift, preservation of kernels, extension of a small partial injection to an atom permutation, and internal finite path graphs. \\[5pt]
Component types & Pure countable codes, labelled isomorphisms, uniform recognition, and the fresh-component swap. \\[5pt]
Commuting normal forms & Subgroups of $\ZZ^r$, the fiber quotient for one injection, holonomy on cycles, and rigidity of the coded staircases. \\[5pt]
Schema certificates & An explicit input domain, functional formula, and cutoff-violating kernel for each negative example. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The core mathematical proof is amenable to modular formalization: finite combinatorics establishes the type families, elementary cardinal arithmetic gives their possible unions, and the kernel model converts those unions into schema instances. The hard formal boundary is semantic bookkeeping for a definable proper-class structure. An eventual formalization may instead work over externally quantified set models with explicit satisfaction and transport each fixed instance. Either route must state its metatheory rather than conceal a truth predicate.

\subsection{The supplied computational checks}

The file \texttt{verify\_examples.py} performs deterministic finite checks using integer arithmetic and finite sets only. Its recorded run passes 347,278 explicit checks. These cover all 256 eight-bit staircase prefixes continued by zeros, boundary decoding, coordinate closure and commutation, translated finite boundary shadows, all 15 subgroups of $(\ZZ/4\ZZ)^2$, quotient-action stabilizers and equivariant maps, and all 31 nonzero characters of $(\FF_2)^5$.

The file \texttt{make\_figure.py} regenerates \cref{nee:ca:fig:staircases}. The picture is an exact integer-lattice visualization of the defining inequalities; it is not generated imagery. Both the vector figure and a high-resolution preview are included.

The role of these checks is limited and useful. They test the explicit finite formulas underlying the constructions and make arithmetic or indexing errors reproducible. They do not prove that all infinite staircase codes are distinct, do not verify any infinite-cardinal argument, and do not certify any instance of set-theoretic Replacement. The mathematical proofs of those claims are supplied in the text. No Lean, Coq, or other proof-assistant verification is claimed.

\begin{writenote}[Shipped layout and replay, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
In ProveIt the two programs are \nolinkurl{code/02-commuting-actions-verify_examples.py} and \nolinkurl{code/02-commuting-actions-make_figure.py}, the recorded run is \nolinkurl{data/02-commuting-actions-verification_output.txt}, and the figure is \nolinkurl{figures/02-commuting-actions-staircases.pdf} with the preview \nolinkurl{figures/02-commuting-actions-staircases.png}. Both programs were rerun on scratch copies at the write (Section~\ref{nee:ca:sec:provenance}); the report's README gives the commands, which never write into this directory.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Dependency audit}

\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.28\linewidth}L{0.34\linewidth}L{0.29\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Conclusion & Essential input & Boundary to preserve \\
\midrule
\endhead
Commuting threshold & Finite descriptions of subgroups of $\ZZ^r$; type profile & Countability of types is used with cofinality, not mere size of $\kappa$. \\[5pt]
One-injection threshold & Injective map on permutation orbits; untwisting of fiber maps & Induced fiber maps are bijections even though the global map may not be. \\[5pt]
Two-injection lower bound & Rigid staircase boundary; block multiplicities & Complete infinite labelled types are distinct; finite shadows are only checks. \\[5pt]
Exact definable kernel & Parameter-free subgroup definitions and transitive translations & Kernels of definable objects differ from individually definable atoms. \\[5pt]
General group threshold & Coset-action classification and uniformly recognized codes & Subgroups are counted up to conjugacy; the finite-cutoff case is separate. \\[5pt]
Uniformity separation & Infinitely many index-two stabilizers; finite reducts & A binary evaluator permits index quantification unavailable to a fixed finite list of unary names. \\[5pt]
Collection and reflection & Compression into growing component reservoirs & Arbitrary witnesses require a reservoir argument; uniqueness alone gives only Replacement. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\section{Further research questions and topics}\label{nee:ca:sec:future}

The questions below specify where the proved classifications end. They are proposed research tasks, not a claim that every formulation is a longstanding published open problem. The commuting-permutation question itself is no longer included as an open task in the setting of this article.

\begin{question}[Atom-valued definable closure for nonabelian actions]\label{nee:ca:q:nonabelian}
For a finitely generated nonabelian group $G$, determine the atoms that are individually definable from specified parameters, as distinct from the kernel of definable objects. The equivariant automorphism group of $G/H$ is controlled by $N_G(H)/H$. How does that normalizer quotient interact with the internal availability of complete orbit blocks? A useful first target is the infinite dihedral group, where orbit and stabilizer data remain explicit.
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[Answered with pure real parameters; re-scoped, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
Part~\ref{nee:aa:part} (source~03 there) answers this question with pure real parameters. For a finitely generated group $G$ named by generators, an atom of type $G/H$ with multiplicity $\mu_{[H]}$ is definable from pure real parameters alone exactly when $\mu_{[H]}=1$ and $N_G(H)=H$; from finitely many atom parameters $F$ and pure reals, exactly when it lies in $GF$ or its residual multiplicity outside $GF$ is~$1$ and $N_G(H)=H$ (\cref{nee:aa:cor:normalizer,nee:aa:thm:atoms}); for arbitrary set parameters the same criterion is exact outside the saturation of their kernels. The interaction with complete orbit blocks is the formula $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa=\bigcup\{A_{[H]}:\mu_{[H]}[N_G(H):H]<\kappa\}$ for the atoms occurring in objects definable with pure reals. The infinite dihedral first target is worked out in Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:dihedral}. At $\kappa=\omega$, \cref{nee:aa:thm:fixedobjects} characterizes all objects definable from arbitrary parameters and arbitrary pure parameters as those fixed by every centralizer element fixing the parameters. Open: ordinary definability, without pure parameters (\cref{nee:aa:q:ordinary}), and arbitrary set parameters inside the saturation of their kernels at $\kappa>\omega$ (\cref{nee:aa:q:fixedobjects}).
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[Exact finite-kernel formulas for several injections]\label{nee:ca:q:injections}
Develop the analogue of \cref{nee:ca:thm:dcl} when two or more commuting injections are named and $\kappa=\omega$. The ray in \cref{nee:ca:ex:ray-dcl} shows that boundary points create definable atoms absent from the small-block term. Which finite layers or distinguished boundary configurations account for all such atoms in the staircase families? Can the result be stated through a finite-boundary invariant of a cancellative monoid action?
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[Answered with pure real parameters; open for ordinary definability, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
Part~\ref{nee:aa:part} (source~03 there) proves the analogue of \cref{nee:ca:thm:dcl} for every finite tuple of injections, commuting or not, at every infinite cutoff including $\kappa=\omega$, \emph{with pure real parameters}: the atom kernel of the objects definable from $\bar a$ and finitely many pure reals is $\Sat(B)\cup D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa$ (\cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel}). At $\kappa=\omega$ a component type contributes to $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega$ exactly when it has finitely many copies and a finite automorphism group, even if the component is infinite (\cref{nee:aa:cor:finitekernel}). For a cancellative commutative monoid action this is the finite-boundary invariant asked for: the automorphism group of a component $S\subseteq G/H$ is its period group $\Per(S)$ (\cref{nee:aa:cor:periods}); for $f^m=g^n$ it is $C_\delta$, read off the boundary word (\cref{nee:aa:thm:boundaryperiods}); the staircases of \cref{nee:ca:lem:staircase} are rigid, so in the staircase families every atom of a type with finitely many copies is in the kernel, not only boundary points, and an atom is individually definable with pure reals exactly when its type occurs once (\cref{nee:aa:thm:atoms}). For \emph{ordinary} definability, as in \cref{nee:ca:thm:dcl}, \cref{nee:aa:prop:ordinary} reduces the question at $\kappa=\omega$ to the definability of rooted codes, which is open (\cref{nee:aa:q:ordinary}). The question stays open in that form.
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[Relations among noninvertible generators]\label{nee:ca:q:monoids}
Classify the connected-type cardinality for injective actions of specified finitely presented commutative monoids. The free case $\ZZ^s\times\NN^t$ is settled here. Relations such as $f^m=g^n$ can collapse two apparent directions. Determine when such relations force countably many types and when a staircase or another real-coded family survives.
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[Answered, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
Part~\ref{nee:aa:part} answers this question in full, by two independent proofs (its sources~03 and~04). For a finitely generated commutative monoid $M$ acting by injections, with group completion $G$, cancellative image $P$, units $U=P\cap(-P)$ and $d=\rank(G/U)$, the number of connected labelled types is $|\Sub(G)|$ when $G$ is finite, $\aleph_0$ when $G$ is infinite and $d\leq1$, and $\cont$ when $d\geq2$, in which case a rigid real-coded family survives (\cref{nee:aa:thm:types,nee:ci:thm:cantor}). The integer $d$ is computable from a finite presentation (\cref{nee:aa:thm:effective}). A relation $f^m=g^n$ always gives $d=1$, hence countably many types, which are enumerated exactly by divisor and necklace sums (\cref{nee:aa:thm:power}); relations can also collapse directions outright ($f^2g=f$ forces $fg=\id$, and $fh=gh$ forces $f=g$; \cref{nee:aa:tab:presentations}). The universal Replacement thresholds are: none for $G$ finite, $\cf(\kappa)>\aleph_0$ for $G$ infinite and $d\leq1$, $\cf(\kappa)>\cont$ for $d\geq2$ (\cref{nee:aa:thm:universal}); Collection and reflection in \cref{nee:aa:cor:univcollection}. AI-assisted and unrefereed, like the rest of the report.
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[Using finer subgroup-space invariants]\label{nee:ca:q:subgroupspaces}
The theorem uses only $\tau(G)$, but subgroup spaces have much finer topology and descriptive complexity; for countable abelian groups see \cite{CGP2010}. Can Cantor--Bendixson rank or a natural complexity bound on stabilizer codes control the syntactic complexity of failed Replacement instances? The first goal should be a precise theorem for a class of groups with already classified subgroup spaces.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Decidability from a finite presentation]\label{nee:ca:q:decidability}
For a finitely presented group $G$, what is the complexity of determining which of the finite, countable, and continuum type-count cases occurs? More realistically, find effective sufficient certificates for a countable bound, a perfect family of subgroup types, or a selected bad multiplicity profile. Any undecidability claim should be proved by an explicit reduction rather than inferred from the general difficulty of group theory.
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[Not answered; the monoid analogue is decided, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
\Cref{nee:aa:thm:effective} of Part~\ref{nee:aa:part} (both of its sources) decides, from a finite commutative \emph{monoid} presentation, which of the three cases holds for the number of connected injective action types: the commutative-monoid analogue of this question. Source~04 there calls this ``a commutative answer'' to the question. It is not the commutative case of the question as posed: for a finitely generated abelian group given by a commutative presentation, $\tau(G)=|\Sub(G)|$ is finite when $G$ is finite and $\aleph_0$ otherwise, which a Smith normal form decides, and this was immediate before (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}). The question for finitely presented groups beyond the abelian ones stays open; \cref{nee:ci:q:presentationcomplexity} asks for the bit complexity of the monoid test.
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[Separate names for groups that are not locally finite]\label{nee:ca:q:separate}
Classify universal Replacement preservation for countable groups in the language of separate names. Local finiteness gives unconditional preservation, and finite generation is settled by \cref{nee:ca:grp:robust}. Between these cases, every formula sees a finitely generated subgroup, but only actions that extend to the whole group are available. Determine whether an invariant of extendible subgroup actions gives a sharp general criterion.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Infinitary formulas]\label{nee:ca:q:infinitary}
Replace ordinary first-order syntax by a precisely specified infinitary logic and re-evaluate the separate-name/uniform-evaluation distinction. For example, can a countable conjunction uniformly distinguish the index-two stabilizers used in \cref{nee:ca:uni:two_point}? The treatment must separately specify formula codes, satisfaction, the allowed parameters, and which Replacement or Collection scheme is being asserted.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[How much ambient Choice is necessary?]\label{nee:ca:q:choice}
The present proofs use external Choice for cardinal arithmetic, families of isomorphisms, and witness selection after external Collection. Isolate these uses in the commuting case. Rooted finitely generated orbits have explicit word enumerations, but selecting and matching components still needs analysis. Establish the weakest natural choice hypotheses under which the exact threshold and the definable-kernel formula survive.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Atom ideals beyond cardinal cutoffs]\label{nee:ca:q:ideals}
Replace the condition $|\kerat(x)|<\kappa$ by membership in an atom ideal $I$. Seek conditions on $I$ and the action which are both necessary and sufficient for expanded Replacement. Closure under orbit saturation, closure under unions of recognizable small blocks, and the ability to transport witnesses into fresh copies are distinct requirements and should not be merged into one unexamined symmetry assumption.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Complexity of a failed instance]\label{nee:ca:q:complexity}
Find the least formula complexity needed to witness the negative examples. The type selector quantifies over whole blocks, while the staircase construction has a directly readable boundary. Can a lower-complexity formula uniformly recognize each coded block? The answer should distinguish a fixed code as parameter, a pure sequence of codes, and a parameter-free formula.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Forcing beyond preservation of the cutoff]\label{nee:ca:q:forcing}
\Cref{nee:ca:thm:forcing} settles the fixed-action question when the original cutoff remains a cardinal. Analyze what happens when that ordinal is collapsed and the model is rebuilt using its new cardinality as the cutoff. Find exact examples in which Replacement changes, and determine which changes of block sizes cause the transition. Separately, when cofinality changes but the cutoff remains a cardinal, identify how new actions witness any change in a universal threshold.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Formal schema independence certificates]\label{nee:ca:q:formal}
Build a reusable ProveIt interface certifying a definable model, its valid scheme instances, and one explicit failed instance in an expanded signature. Begin with the distinct-period example, then certify the two-point uniformity separation. This would provide a concrete test that the formal infrastructure keeps elementary lifts, amenability, base Replacement, and expanded Replacement separate.
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[A question added at the write, 4 October 2026, batch~93]
ProveIt's standing rule for unproved claims (\nolinkurl{docs/incoming/README.md}, Section~4, item~11) moves the one imported, unproved premise of this Part here.
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[A primary check of the Ivanov--Ol'shanskii groups]\label{nee:ca:q:primary}
\emph{Source:} \cref{nee:ca:grp:exception}, which credits Ivanov and Ol'shanskii~\cite{IO1991} through Smith~\cite[p.~69]{Smith1995}. \emph{Claim:} there is a finitely generated infinite group $G$ all of whose proper nontrivial subgroups are conjugate; then $\tau(G)=3$, so the finite-cutoff clause ``$G$ finite'' of \cref{nee:ca:grp:robust} and the clause ``$\cf(\kappa)>\tau(G)$'' read at $\kappa=\omega$ (that is, $\tau(G)$ finite) differ. \emph{Argument available:} none beyond Smith's attribution (checked on the first page of~\cite{Smith1995}); the title of~\cite{IO1991} names its method, graded diagrams in combinatorial group theory. \emph{Missing:} a check of the construction in the primary source, or in a textbook account of it, confirming finite generation, infinitude and conjugacy of all proper nontrivial subgroups; neither the manuscript nor the write consulted~\cite{IO1991}. Short of that, is there a more accessible infinite finitely generated group with finitely many conjugacy classes of subgroups?
\end{question}

\section{Conventions and construction data}\label{nee:ca:app:conventions}

\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.24\linewidth}L{0.66\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Notation & Meaning and location \\
\midrule
\endhead
$A$ & An external set of atoms with $|A|\geq\kappa$; it is not an internal set of $\Mk$. \\[4pt]
$\kerat(x)$ & Atoms occurring in $\TC(\{x\})$, including $x$ itself when $x$ is an atom. \\[4pt]
$\Mk$ & All objects over $A$ whose atom kernels have cardinality below $\kappa$. Pure ranks are unrestricted. \\[4pt]
$j_f$ & The recursively lifted atom injection $f$, fixing every pure object. \\[4pt]
$B_t$ & All atoms in all components of type $t$, not a single component. \\[4pt]
$D_\kappa$ & Union of the entire type blocks that are internal atom sets. It is always an external set but need not be internal. \\[4pt]
$\tau(G)$ & Number of conjugacy classes of all subgroups of $G$. \\[4pt]
$\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}(G)$ & Number of conjugacy classes of finite-index subgroups of $G$. \\[4pt]
$E(g,x)$ & One binary evaluator for the whole group action, totalized by the identity outside the pure set $G$. \\[4pt]
$\mathcal K(\dcl(\bar a))$ & Scheme notation for kernels of uniquely definable objects, as explained before \cref{nee:ca:thm:dcl}; not an added object-language truth predicate. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\subsection{An explicit singular-cutoff witness}

At $\kappa=\aleph_\omega$, choose disjoint atom blocks $B_n$ of size $\aleph_n$. Partition $B_n$ into cycles of length $n+2$. Let $p$ rotate each cycle and fix any unused atoms. The single formula
\[
\text{``$y$ is the set of all atoms of exact $p$-period $n+2$''}
\]
has the unique internal value $B_n$ at each $n\in\omega$. The statement of exact period uses finite path graphs and the requirement that no shorter positive iterate returns to the atom. Its range cannot be an internal set because its kernel would contain $\bigcup_n B_n$, of size $\aleph_\omega$. Adding finitely many identity names gives commuting counterexamples of every finite positive arity.

\subsection{An explicit regular-cutoff witness}

At $\kappa=\aleph_1$, choose distinct staircase codes $S_\alpha$ for $\alpha<\omega_1$ and use countably many copies of each $U_{S_\alpha}$. Such a family of codes exists by Choice and $\aleph_1\leq\cont$. Each entire type block is countable. Their union has size $\aleph_1$.

The pure sequence $\langle S_\alpha:\alpha<\omega_1\rangle$ is an internal parameter, even though it has uncountable size. Its type-block selector is functional on the internal pure domain $\omega_1$ and has no internal range. Each unary coordinate map has just the ray type and preserves Replacement. This explicitly illustrates why large pure input sets cannot be ignored in a model that restricts only atom kernels.

\subsection{The finite-cutoff uniformity witness}

Take $G=\bigoplus_\omega C_2$ and nonzero characters $\chi_{\{n\}}$ for $n<\omega$. Give each one exactly one two-atom orbit. Separate unary names preserve Replacement, since any finite set of them acts through a finite subgroup. With uniform evaluation, the formula comparing an atom's stabilizer with $\ker\chi_{\{n\}}$ defines the complete two-atom block. Collecting those blocks would require infinitely many atoms, which is forbidden at $\kappa=\omega$. The evaluator's restriction to any internal set still uses only finitely many atoms, so it remains amenable.

\fancyhead[L]{\small\textsc{Naming Elementary Embeddings}}
\fancyhead[R]{\small Part~III: relations among injections}
\part{Relations among injections: definable atoms, connected types, and Replacement}
\label{nee:aa:part}

% Part III continues the section numbers of Part II after its appendices.
\setcounter{section}{23}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{aa.\arabic{section}}
\makeatletter\gdef\Hy@chapapp{\Hy@chapterstring}\makeatother

{\Large A finite/countable/continuum classification, exact kernels from rooted components, exact enumeration, and Replacement\par}
\smallskip
{\large An investigation of ProveIt Questions 23.1--23.3 at the intersection with Elliot Glazer's research\par}
\smallskip
{Two research manuscripts, merged: \emph{Relations, Definable Atoms, and Replacement} (source~03) and \emph{Relations among Commuting Injections: Connected Actions, Exact Enumeration, and Replacement} (source~04, subtitled \emph{An effective rank classification at the Glazer--ProveIt research intersection}), both 4 October 2026\par}

\status{\textbf{Status} (source~03). Complete mathematical proofs are supplied for the stated results. The component-count classification and the definability refinements are developed here; their standard localization, group-action, and polyhedral ingredients are credited. The results answer the specified repository questions with the parameter conventions stated in the article. Historical publication priority has not been established. The accompanying computations check finite examples; these proofs have not been checked by a proof assistant. Elliot Glazer and Bokai Yao are cited researchers, not authors or endorsers of this manuscript.}

\paragraph{Abstract (source~03).}
Let a finitely generated commutative monoid act by injective maps. Write $G$ for
its Grothendieck group, $P$ for its image in $G$, $U=P\cap(-P)$ for the group of
units, and $d=\rank(G/U)$. We prove that the number of nonempty connected labelled
action types is finite when $G$ is finite, countably infinite when $G$ is infinite
and $d\leq1$, and exactly $2^{\aleph_0}$ when $d\geq2$. For a finite presentation,
$d$ is the dimension of the span of an explicitly given nonnegative rational
solution cone, so the trichotomy is decidable by exact linear algebra. In
particular, commuting injections satisfying $f^m=g^n$ have only countably many
types. If $m,n$ are coprime, precisely
$1+\binom{m+n}{m}/(m+n)$ of those types are infinite. A necklace formula
classifies and counts all finite and infinite types without a coprimality
assumption, and determines the automorphism group of every proper component.

We apply this classification to $M_\kappa(A)$, the universe of sets involving
fewer than $\kappa$ atoms. It gives sharp universal thresholds for preserving
Replacement after the canonical lifts of the generators are named. A second
theorem, valid without commutativity, identifies the exact atom kernel of
definable closure with pure real parameters: it is the component saturation of
the parameter kernels together with the atoms whose orbits under the commuting
automorphism group have size below $\kappa$. Finite path codes make this result
valid even at $\kappa=\omega$ and for infinite components. For group actions the
criterion is expressed through subgroup normalizers; for commutative actions it
is expressed through translation periods. We distinguish kernels of definable
objects from individually definable atoms, give exact parameter-sensitive
formulas, explain the relation to Glazer--Yao small-kernel methods, and propose
further research directions.

\paragraph{Abstract (source~04).}
We classify the number of connected injective actions of every finitely
generated commutative monoid. After cancellativization, let $P$ be the
monoid inside its finitely generated abelian group completion $G$, let
$U=P\cap(-P)$, and put $d=\rank(G/U)$. There are finitely many connected
types when $G$ is finite, countably infinitely many when $G$ is infinite
and $d\leq1$, and continuum many when $d\geq2$. The last case admits an
explicit Cantor family of rigid components. For a fixed completion
stabilizer the alternatives sharpen to finite or continuum.

The invariant is computable by exact rational linear algebra from a
finite commutative presentation. For two distinguished commuting
injections satisfying $f^m=g^n$, we give a complete enumeration: finite
connected types are counted by a divisor sum, and infinite connected
types by a finite sum of binary-necklace numbers. The coprime boundary
case recovers the classical rational Catalan number.

In cardinal-kernel models with urelements, the type classification yields
sharp universal thresholds for expanded Replacement: no restriction on
the infinite cutoff for finite effective monoids, cofinality above
$\aleph_0$ for infinite monoids of positive rank at most one, and
cofinality above $2^{\aleph_0}$ in higher positive rank. Explicit
counterexamples witness every negative case. These results answer
ProveIt's Question 23.3 in the inspected revision and give a commutative
answer to its related decidability question.

{\footnotesize\noindent\textbf{[write]} Part~III of this report since 4 October 2026: batch-96 manuscripts~05 and~06 of ProveIt's incoming-reports intake, two independent answers to one assignment, \emph{merged} (every result, proof, example, remark, question and non-claim of both is printed; a result proved by both is printed once, with the other proof as a marked second route). In this report they are \emph{source~03} (manuscript~05, \emph{Relations, Definable Atoms, and Replacement}, labels \texttt{nee:aa:}, the base of the merge) and \emph{source~04} (manuscript~06, \emph{Relations among Commuting Injections}, labels \texttt{nee:ci:}); Parts~I and~II are sources~01 and~02. Both answer Question~\ref{nee:ca:q:monoids} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}; source~03 also answers Questions~\ref{nee:ca:q:nonabelian} and~\ref{nee:ca:q:injections} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} and Question~\ref{nee:q:dcl} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} \emph{with pure real parameters}, which the write re-scopes rather than closes (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}). The sections are renumbered from~24 and the appendices from~F; a line ``Sources'' after each numbered item names the item of each manuscript that it prints. The second sentence of source~04's abstract uses source~03's letters (source~04 swaps $M$ and $P$; Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:notation}); its last sentence overstates what is decided about Question~\ref{nee:ca:q:decidability} (the note in Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}). Provenance, the merge choices and the notation are in Sections~\ref{nee:aa:sec:provenance} and~\ref{nee:aa:sec:notation}.\par}

\section{The research intersection and the results}\label{nee:aa:sec:intro}
\label{nee:ci:sec:introduction}

\subsection{Why this is a Glazer--ProveIt topic}

Glazer and Yao study the interaction of Collection, reflection, and choice in
set theory with urelements, including models in which the atoms form an internal
proper class \cite{GY2026}. Their small-kernel constructions use permutations to
control which atoms can occur in a uniquely specified output. Glazer's thesis
places these questions in a broader program involving choice, forcing, and
measure theory \cite{GlazerThesis}. These primary mathematical sources provide
the research-interest match; the LinkedIn address supplied with the research
request did not provide readable contents during this investigation.

The inspected ProveIt report \cite{RepoNamingIII} develops canonical elementary
lifts of injections on atoms and asks what changes when such lifts become named
operations. Its Part II ends with explicit questions about nonabelian definable
closure, finite kernels for several injections, and relations among commuting
noninvertible generators. We use the repository snapshot
\begin{center}
\texttt{715a716a3002b1e9f28daa2d81c009a392851b94}.
\end{center}
The mathematical source is
\begin{quote}\small\raggedright
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/naming-elementary-embeddings/article.tex}.
\end{quote}
The connection is precise: equations among named injections constrain their
connected components, which constrain available automorphisms, which in turn
control definable atom sets and the Replacement scheme.

\emph{Source~04's account of the same intersection.}
Glazer and Yao's \emph{Reflection Principles in ZFU} studies how
Collection and reflection diverge in set theory with urelements
\cite{GY2026}. Its relevance here is the role of atom symmetries and
of the availability of sets collecting witnesses. ProveIt's report
\emph{Naming Elementary Embeddings} develops cardinal-kernel models and
asks how naming atom maps changes the full Replacement scheme
\cite{RepoNamingIII}. Its Part II settles free commuting signatures and explicitly
asks, in Question~23.3, what relations among noninvertible generators do
to the number of connected action types.

We answer that repository question for all finitely generated
commutative monoids, including those initially presented without
cancellation. The answer is controlled by the rank of the group
completion after removing the directions already invertible in the
effective monoid. We then compute considerably more than cardinality
for the two-generator relation $f^m=g^n$.

The external ambient theory throughout is ordinary set theory with
Choice; for the kernel-model application we use its usual version with
an external set of urelements. The conclusions concern explicit
expansions of those models. They do not strengthen the choiceless
separation theorem of Glazer and Yao.

\subsection{Questions answered and conventions}

\begin{longtable}{L{0.14\textwidth}L{0.34\textwidth}L{0.43\textwidth}}
\toprule
Repository question & Mathematical issue & Result in this Part (source~03) \\
\midrule
23.1 & Individually definable atoms for nonabelian actions & A normalizer formula
with residual component multiplicity for finite atom parameters; an exact
formula outside the support saturation for arbitrary parameters. At the finite
cutoff, a general orbit-definability theorem also treats arbitrary parameters. \\[5pt]
23.2 & Finite definable kernels for several injections & An exact rooted-component
formula for every infinite cutoff, with pure real parameters. At the finite
cutoff, finite multiplicity and a finite local automorphism group are decisive. \\[5pt]
23.3 & Relations in finitely presented commutative monoids & A complete
finite/countable/continuum classification, a decision procedure, sharp axiom
thresholds, and an explicit power-relation classification. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

Pure-parameter definability means that each definition uses only finitely many
pure parameters; it does not add a constant symbol for every pure object to a
single formula. The distinction from parameter-free definability is essential.
An infinite union of rigid components can have many more atoms than there are
parameter-free formulas. We give the corresponding counterexample below.

\emph{The inspected repository question} (source~04).
The audit used commit
\[
\texttt{715a716a3002b1e9f28daa2d81c009a392851b94}
\]
of \texttt{VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt}. The relevant file is
\begin{quote}\small
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/}\\
\nolinkurl{naming-elementary-embeddings/article.tex}.
\end{quote}
Part II's Question 23.3 asks for a classification for finitely presented
commutative monoids, with $f^m=g^n$ as a motivating example. Its
Question 23.5 asks about decidability, primarily for general finitely
presented groups. Our algorithm settles the commutative presentation
case, without making a decidability claim for arbitrary groups.

The source is a research report, not a blanket certificate of formal
verification. We use the mathematical arguments explicitly stated
below; no unexamined repository claim is a premise of the main
classification. \Cref{nee:aa:sec:verification} records the proof boundaries
and the supplied reproducibility material.

\subsubsection*{What is answered, as re-scoped at the write}\label{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~96]
``Question~23.$k$'' above is Question~23.$k$ of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}: 23.1 is \cref{nee:ca:q:nonabelian}, 23.2 is \cref{nee:ca:q:injections}, 23.3 is \cref{nee:ca:q:monoids} and 23.5 is \cref{nee:ca:q:decidability}; the sources' numbers are this report's. ProveIt's standing rule for unproved claims (\nolinkurl{docs/incoming/README.md}, Section~4, item~11) asks that a question only partly answered be re-scoped, not closed. Read against the questions as posed, the two sources settle the following.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Question~23.3 is answered} in full, by both sources independently: the type count is finite, $\aleph_0$ or $2^{\aleph_0}$ according to $G$ and $d=\rank(G/U)$ (\cref{nee:aa:thm:types}), decidably from a finite presentation (\cref{nee:aa:thm:effective}); relations $f^m=g^n$ are enumerated exactly (\cref{nee:aa:thm:power}), and the universal Replacement, Collection and reflection thresholds follow (\cref{nee:aa:thm:universal,nee:aa:cor:univcollection}). A dated note after the question records this.
\item \emph{Question~23.2 is answered with pure real parameters only.} \Cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel} gives, at every infinite cutoff and for every finite tuple of injections (commuting or not), the exact atom kernel of objects definable from the parameters \emph{together with finitely many pure reals}. The question asks for the analogue of \cref{nee:ca:thm:dcl}, which is a statement about ordinary definability; for ordinary definability source~03 reduces the problem, at $\kappa=\omega$, to the definability of rooted codes (\cref{nee:aa:prop:ordinary}) and leaves that open (\cref{nee:aa:q:ordinary}). The question stays open in its ordinary form.
\item \emph{Question~10.1 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}} (\cref{nee:q:dcl}) is answered in the same pure-real form, for every finite tuple of injections: at $\kappa>\omega$ the bound ``component closure plus $D_\kappa(\boldsymbol f)$'' of \cref{nee:thm:profile}'s proof is exactly the atom kernel of objects definable with pure real parameters (\cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel,nee:aa:cor:finitekernel}); at $\kappa=\omega$ the small-type part must be replaced by $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega$, the atoms whose centralizer orbits are finite. Ordinary definability stays open.
\item \emph{Question~23.1 is answered with pure real parameters for atom parameters, and at $\kappa=\omega$ for arbitrary parameters with arbitrary pure parameters.} \Cref{nee:aa:thm:atoms,nee:aa:cor:normalizer} give the individually definable atoms from finitely many named atoms and pure reals, through $N_G(H)/H$ and the residual multiplicity, including the infinite dihedral ``first target'' (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:dihedral}); for arbitrary set parameters the criterion is exact only outside the saturation $\Sat(B)$ of their kernels; at $\kappa=\omega$, \cref{nee:aa:thm:fixedobjects} characterizes all objects definable from arbitrary parameters and arbitrary pure parameters. Open: ordinary definability, and arbitrary set parameters inside $\Sat(B)$ at $\kappa>\omega$ (\cref{nee:aa:q:ordinary,nee:aa:q:fixedobjects}).
\item \emph{Question~23.5 is not answered.} Source~04's abstract says that its results ``give a commutative answer to'' it, and its README that they give ``an effective answer to the related commutative case''. What \cref{nee:aa:thm:effective} decides is the \emph{commutative-monoid analogue}: from a finite commutative monoid presentation, which of the three cases holds for the number $\tau(M)$ of connected injective action types. Question~23.5 asks about $\tau(G)$, the number of conjugacy classes of subgroups, for finitely presented \emph{groups}. Its commutative case, finitely generated abelian groups given by commutative presentations, was immediate before source~04: every subgroup of such a group is finitely generated, so $\tau(G)=|\Sub(G)|$ is finite when $G$ is finite and $\aleph_0$ otherwise (an infinite one has infinitely many finite-index subgroups), and finiteness of $G$ is read off a Smith normal form. (Presenting such a group as a commutative monoid with inverse generators, \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} returns the same answer: $P=G$, so $d=0$.) Question~23.5 stays open beyond abelian groups; source~04's own text in Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:intro} says as much (``without making a decidability claim for arbitrary groups''). A dated note after the question records this.
\end{itemize}
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Main conclusions of source~04}

Source~04 opens by stating its two main theorems in summary form, its Theorem~1.1 (the type classification) and Theorem~1.2 (universal Replacement). Each is proved by both sources and is printed once in this Part: Theorem~1.1 as \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} with source~04's Cantor-family clause, Theorem~1.2 as \cref{nee:aa:thm:universal}. In source~04's words:
the theorem is proved in Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:trichotomy}, after the normal form in
\cref{nee:aa:sec:localization}. Its effective version in \cref{nee:aa:sec:effective} determines
the answer directly from a finite commutative presentation. The complete
two-generator enumeration is \cref{nee:aa:thm:power}. The main
foundational consequence, proved in \cref{nee:aa:sec:replacement}, is the
universal Replacement table of \cref{nee:aa:thm:universal}.

Here a statement about \emph{every} action is essential. At a cutoff
below the threshold, particular actions can still preserve Replacement.
We give the exact criterion for each individual action in
\cref{nee:aa:thm:profile}.

\subsection{What is inherited, and the contribution}

The small-kernel universe itself is established groundwork \cite{Yao2023,GY2026}.
The fixed-action Replacement and Collection criteria are proved in the inspected
repository report \cite{RepoNamingIII}. A related report already classifies
Replacement with a fixed finite bound on output kernels for uniformly named
group actions \cite[Part VIII, Theorem 59.1]{RepoBirthday}. We do not claim those
classifications as new. The passage from a cancellative action to an action of
its group of fractions is standard \cite[Section 6]{BaileyRenshaw}; so are the
normalizer formula for automorphisms of a transitive group action \cite{Boardman}
and the geometry of units in affine monoids \cite{Koch}.

The contributions here are the complete type-count trichotomy for finitely
generated commutative actions, its explicit pure-code construction and effective
form, and the rooted-path definability refinements that remain valid for infinite
components with finite atom kernels. Some proofs recombine standard ingredients.
The claim of progress is tied to the precise statements proved, rather than to an
assertion of a field-wide breakthrough or of settled publication priority.

\status{\textbf{Contribution and status} (source~04).
The new extension relative to the inspected ProveIt report is the
general monoid rank classification, its effective presentation test,
the explicit two-generator enumeration, and their resulting uniform
foundational classification. Localization of commutative monoid
actions, rational-cone facts, necklaces, and rational Catalan
enumeration are classical ingredients. The component-block criterion
is prior repository work and is reproved with attribution.
The proofs have received multiple internal reviews during AI-assisted
preparation and exact finite checks, but are not proof-assistant
certified or externally refereed. A claim of historical priority or
of an established mathematical breakthrough is not made.}

The source numbers continue those of the report's README (Part~\ref{nee:part:one} is source~01, Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} source~02) and match the file prefixes. Both Parts' sources are AI-assisted; this Part is unrefereed and not formalized. The two manuscripts answer one assignment independently (their shared 8-grams, about 1\% of each, are mostly bibliography) and both answer Question~\ref{nee:ca:q:monoids} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}, so the placement made them one addition to this report, which they continue, rather than a new report or an addition to Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/docs/foundations-and-computation/birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets}.

\emph{How they were merged.} Union, not selection: every statement, proof, example, remark, figure, table, question and non-claim of both manuscripts is printed. Source~03 is the base: its kernel theorems need no commutativity, and it answers three repository questions to source~04's one. Its section order is kept, and source~04's sections are inserted where their subject is: its Section~1 is merged into Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:intro}, its Sections~2--3 (localization, cones, the trichotomy) are merged into Sections~\ref{nee:aa:sec:localization}--\ref{nee:aa:sec:trichotomy}, its Section~4 is Section~\ref{nee:ci:sec:fixed}, its Section~5 is merged into Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:effective}, its Section~6 into Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:power}, its Sections~7--10 into Sections~\ref{nee:aa:sec:ambient} and~\ref{nee:aa:sec:replacement}, its Section~11.1 is Section~\ref{nee:ci:sec:boundaries}, its Section~11.2 is merged into Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:finitegeneration}, its Sections~12--13 into Sections~\ref{nee:aa:sec:future} and~\ref{nee:aa:sec:verification}. After each numbered item a line ``Sources'' names the manuscript items it prints. Where both sources prove a result, it is printed once, in a statement containing every clause of either, with source~03's proof first and source~04's as ``Second proof (source~04)'' whenever the arguments differ: \cref{nee:aa:prop:lifts}, \cref{nee:aa:lem:cancellation}, \cref{nee:aa:thm:localization}, \cref{nee:aa:lem:cone}, \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}, \cref{nee:aa:thm:effective}, \cref{nee:aa:thm:power}~(i) and~(iii), \cref{nee:aa:thm:profile}, \cref{nee:aa:thm:universal}, \cref{nee:aa:prop:collection} and \cref{nee:aa:cor:univcollection}. Section, theorem and equation numbers of both manuscripts change; the ``Sources'' lines give the correspondence. Each source's equations, numbered consecutively in source~03 and within sections in source~04, are numbered within sections here.

\emph{Pin and repository claims.} Both manuscripts are pinned to \texttt{715a716a3002b1e9f28daa2d81c009a392851b94} (``Construct a non-dyadic outer family with actual compiler constants fixed'', 4 October 2026), an ancestor of the placement. This report's \texttt{article.tex} at the pin is the blob \texttt{af39ad7c6}, identical to Parts~\ref{nee:part:one}--\ref{nee:ca:part} as printed here before this Part was added, so every number the sources quote (Questions~23.1--23.3 and~23.5; Theorems~5.1, 7.5 and~7.7 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}) is this report's, and \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} is also unchanged since the pin. The sources' repository statements are true at the pin and at this write: Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} ends with the questions they quote, under the labels source~03 names (\texttt{nee:ca:q:nonabelian}, \texttt{nee:ca:q:injections}, \texttt{nee:ca:q:monoids}); it ``settles free commuting signatures'' (\cref{nee:ca:thm:mixed}, $\ZZ^s\times\NN^t$); the fixed-action Replacement and Collection criteria are \cref{nee:thm:profile,nee:ca:thm:profile,nee:thm:collection,nee:thm:reflection}, the reservoir argument is Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s Section~\ref{nee:sec:collection} (\cref{nee:lem:reservoir}); Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets}, Theorem~59.1 (\texttt{hset:ns:thm:graded}), classifies Replacement with a finite bound on output kernels for uniformly named group actions, at $\kappa=\omega$; and the repository's root README says that generated data and exploratory computations enter the trusted boundary only through proved checkers. No repository statement is contradicted, and nothing needed retraction.
