\section{The ambient universe and named injections}\label{nee:aa:sec:ambient}
\label{nee:ci:sec:models}

\subsection{Sets with a restricted atom kernel}

Work externally in a well-founded universe of sets with Choice and a set $A$ of
atoms. Let $\kappa$ be an infinite cardinal and assume $|A|\geq\kappa$.
For any object $x$, define its \emph{atom kernel}
\[
\kerat(x)=A\cap\TC(\{x\}).
\]
In particular $\kerat(a)=\{a\}$ for an atom $a$. Let
\[
\Mk=\{x:|\kerat(x)|<\kappa\}.
\]
There is no bound on pure rank. Every pure set belongs to $\Mk$, but $A$ itself
does not. Thus $A$ is an external set and an internal proper class. The predicates
``set'' and ``atom'' are distinguished; Extensionality applies to sets.

\begin{lemma}[Kernel facts]\label{nee:aa:lem:kernel}
If $y\in x$, then $\kerat(y)\subseteq\kerat(x)$. Moreover
\[
\kerat(\{x,y\})=\kerat(x)\cup\kerat(y),\qquad
\kerat(\mathcal P(x))\subseteq\kerat(x),\qquad
\kerat(Y)=\bigcup_{y\in Y}\kerat(y)
\]
for every external set $Y$ of objects. Consequently $\Mk$ is transitive and is
closed under subsets of internal sets, Pairing, Union, and Powerset.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~03, Lemma~2.1. It is \cref{nee:lem:kernel} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}.}
\begin{proof}
An atom in a transitive closure is witnessed by a finite membership path.
Concatenating such paths proves the inclusions and identities. A finite union of
cardinals below an infinite $\kappa$ remains below $\kappa$, including when
$\kappa$ is singular. The closure assertions follow.
\end{proof}

Every permutation $\pi$ of $A$ has a canonical recursive lift to all objects,
with $\widehat\pi(x)=\{\widehat\pi(y):y\in x\}$ on sets. It is an automorphism
of the membership structure on $\Mk$ and fixes every pure object. If it fixes
$\kerat(x)$ pointwise, it fixes $x$.

\begin{proposition}[The classical reduct]\label{nee:aa:prop:base}
The structure $(\Mk,\in,\At)$ satisfies ZF with atoms, Replacement, and Choice.
Its internal cardinalities of internal sets agree with their external
cardinalities.
\end{proposition}
\srcs{source~03, Proposition~2.2; source~04, Section~7.1 (second route below). It is \cref{nee:thm:base} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} and \cref{nee:ca:prop:base} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}.}
\begin{proof}
Only Replacement needs a substantive argument. Suppose a base-language formula
uniquely specifies $y$ from internal parameters $x,p$. If
$b\in\kerat(y)\setminus(\kerat(x)\cup\kerat(p))$, choose
$c\in A\setminus(\kerat(x)\cup\kerat(p)\cup\kerat(y))$. Such $c$ exists by the
kernel bound. Swapping $b,c$ fixes $x,p$ and changes $y$, contradicting uniqueness.
Thus a unique output introduces no atoms beyond the input and parameter kernels.
The externally constructed range on an internal domain $w$ has kernel contained
in $\kerat(w)\cup\kerat(p)$, so it is internal. This proves each Replacement instance.
The remaining structural axioms follow from \cref{nee:aa:lem:kernel}; Separation uses
external Separation. An external well-order of an internal set $x$, and the
graph of its enumeration by an ordinal, use only atoms from $\kerat(x)$. They are
internal, proving Choice and cardinality agreement.
\end{proof}

\emph{Source~04's framework} (its Section~7.1). Work in a well-founded ambient universe with urelements and Choice.
Fix an external set $A$ of atoms and an infinite cardinal
$\kappa\leq|A|$. Let $U(A)$ be the cumulative universe over $A$.
For an object $x$, let $\kerat(x)$ be the set of atoms in
$\TC(\{x\})$, and define the class
\[
\Mk=\{x\in U(A):|\kerat(x)|<\kappa\}.
\]
This is a definable class structure. No set of all its objects
or internal set of all its atoms is asserted.
Every pure set lies in $\Mk$; an atom set is internal exactly
when it has size below $\kappa$.

Subsets of an internal set are internal. Pairing, Union, and
Powerset preserve the kernel bound, as do the usual other
ZFU axioms. An external well-order of an internal set has
its graph supported on the same atom kernel, so is internal.
The base-language Replacement assertion follows, for example,
from the component argument below with no named maps:
there is one singleton component type with a large atom block.
Thus the underlying model satisfies ZFU with Choice and
Replacement. This does not assert Collection at every cutoff.
(That is, \cref{nee:aa:thm:profile} below, applied to the empty tuple of named maps, or to the identity, gives a second proof of base Replacement.)

\subsection{Lifts are elementary before they are named}

For an injection $f:A\to A$, let $j_f$ be the recursively defined map
\[
j_f(a)=f(a),\qquad j_f(x)=\{j_f(y):y\in x\}\quad(x\text{ a set}).
\]
It satisfies $\kerat(j_f(x))=f[\kerat(x)]$.

\begin{proposition}[Canonical lifts]\label{nee:aa:prop:lifts}
\label{nee:ci:prop:elementary}
Every $j_f$ is an elementary self-embedding of the classical reduct and is
amenable: the graph of its restriction to any internal set is internal.
Naming several such maps need not preserve expanded Replacement.
\end{proposition}
\srcs{source~03, Proposition~2.3; source~04, Proposition~7.1 (elementarity). It is part of \cref{nee:thm:lifts} and \cref{nee:ca:prop:lifts}.}
\begin{proof}
For a finite tuple of parameters, the union $B$ of their kernels has size below
$\kappa$. The partial bijection $f|B$ extends to a permutation of $A$, since both
complements have size $|A|$. Its lift agrees with $j_f$ on the tuple, proving
elementarity one formula at a time. The restriction graph on an internal $x$
has kernel contained in $\kerat(x)\cup f[\kerat(x)]$, proving amenability.
The last assertion will follow from the explicit failed instances in
\cref{nee:aa:sec:replacement}; elementarity of a map in the original language is a
different assertion from Replacement for formulas that can mention that map.
\end{proof}

\emph{Source~04's setting for the lifts.}
Let the effective monoid $P$ act on $A$ by injections.
For a chosen finite generating tuple $p_1,\ldots,p_r$, let
$f_i(a)=p_i\cdot a$, and define its recursive lift by
\[
J_i(a)=f_i(a)\quad(a\in A),\qquad
J_i(x)=\{J_i(y):y\in x\}\quad(x\text{ a set}).
\]
These class maps preserve $\Mk$, since
$\kerat(J_i(x))=f_i[\kerat(x)]$.
The expanded language is
\[
\{\in,\At,J_1,\ldots,J_r\}.
\]
Expanded Replacement concerns \emph{every formula} in this
language, not only the existence of the individual images
$J_i[w]$.

\begin{proof}[Second proof of elementarity (source~04)]
For any finite parameter tuple, its combined atom kernel $B$
has size below $\kappa$. The injection $f_i|B$ extends to
a permutation of $A$, because both complements have
cardinality $|A|$. The recursive lift of that permutation
is an automorphism of the base structure and agrees with
$J_i$ on the chosen parameters. Every base-language
formula therefore has the same truth value before and
after applying $J_i$.
\end{proof}

Thus the new scheme obstruction can coexist with elementary
self-embeddings of the reduct. Introducing a symbol permits
formulas to use the component structure of that map, which
was unavailable in the base language. This is the specific
foundational issue studied by the repository report.
(The two proofs are the same argument, that of \cref{nee:thm:lifts}, written independently.)

Fix a finite tuple of injections $\boldsymbol f=(f_1,\ldots,f_r)$ and let
$\N=(\Mk,\in,\At,j_{f_1},\ldots,j_{f_r})$. Unless explicitly
stated, they need not commute. The relevant atom automorphism group is
\[
\mathcal Z=\Aut(A;\boldsymbol f)
=\{\pi\in\Sym(A):\pi f_i=f_i\pi\text{ for all }i\}.
\]
Its lifts are automorphisms of $\N$.

\begin{remark}[Schemes and external semantics]\label{nee:aa:rem:semantics}
Assertions about a formula in $\N$ refer to the external relativization
of that fixed formula to the definable class $\Mk$. Replacement, reflection, and
definable closure are interpreted formula by formula. No internal satisfaction
predicate for a proper-class structure is assumed. All collection of external
witnesses occurs in the ambient theory and is followed by a separate atom-kernel
bound. This convention prevents a concealed use of the very expanded
Replacement scheme being investigated.
\end{remark}
\srcs{source~03, Remark~2.4; source~04, Section~7.2 (below). Compare \cref{nee:rem:schema,nee:ca:rem:semantics}.}

\emph{External arguments versus internal assertions} (source~04, Section~7.2).
All uses of satisfaction below concern one fixed formula,
whose quantifiers can be restricted to the definable class
$\Mk$ in the ambient theory. There is no uniform truth
predicate for the universe. Type codes and sets of type
codes are pure sets, hence internal; an isomorphism from a
countable pure code to an atom component need not be
internal at the finite cutoff. We treat that cutoff
separately.

\section{Rooted components encoded by finite paths}\label{nee:aa:sec:codes}

Give $A$ the labelled directed edges $a\xrightarrow{i}f_i(a)$. A component is
connected after directions are forgotten. Since each label has at most one
incoming and one outgoing edge at a vertex, every component is countable.
Let $W$ be the pure countable set of finite words in
$1^+,1^-,\ldots,r^+,r^-$. A positive letter follows $f_i$; a negative letter
follows its partial inverse.

\begin{lemma}[Internal finite evaluation]\label{nee:aa:lem:eval}
There is a fixed first-order formula $\Eval(w,a,b)$ saying that $w$ has a feasible
path from atom $a$ to atom $b$. It has its intended external meaning in
$\N$ at every infinite $\kappa$. For fixed $w,a$, the endpoint is unique
when it exists.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~03, Lemma~3.1. It is \cref{nee:lem:paths} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}.}
\begin{proof}
The formula asks for a finite sequence of atoms of length $|w|+1$ with the
specified endpoints. For $i^+$ require $j_{f_i}(a_k)=a_{k+1}$; for $i^-$ require
$j_{f_i}(a_{k+1})=a_k$. Every actual finite path has a finite kernel and therefore
has an internal sequence graph. Conversely an internal such graph is a genuine
path. Injectivity provides uniqueness for negative steps; positive steps are
already functional.
\end{proof}

For an atom $a$ define
\[
E_a=\{(u,v)\in W^2:\exists b\ (\Eval(u,a,b)\land\Eval(v,a,b))\}.
\]
Using a fixed injective natural-number coding of $W^2$, regard $E_a$ as a pure
real $c_a\subseteq\NN$; numbers outside the coding image are excluded.
The diagonal records feasible words; all pairs record equality of endpoints.

\begin{theorem}[Exact rooted codes]\label{nee:aa:thm:rootcode}
There is one first-order formula $\Code(c,a)$ defining $c=c_a$. For atoms $a,b$,
the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $c_a=c_b$;
\item their rooted labelled components are isomorphic;
\item $b\in\mathcal Z a$.
\end{enumerate}
The result includes infinite components when $\kappa=\omega$.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~3.2.}
\begin{proof}
The defining formula for $\Code$ requires $c\subseteq\NN$, excludes numbers
outside the coding image, and states, for every coded word pair, exactly the
displayed condition defining $E_a$. The pure real $c_a$ exists externally and is
internal because it contains no atoms. This does not require an internal graph
of all endpoints.

If the codes agree, send the endpoint of $w$ at $a$ to the endpoint of $w$ at $b$.
Equality data makes this well defined and injective, and feasibility gives
surjectivity. Appending a letter shows preservation of each labelled edge.
Every point of a component is reached by a finite signed word, so this is a
rooted isomorphism. The converse follows by transporting paths.

A rooted isomorphism between distinct components extends to a permutation of
$A$ by interchanging the components through the isomorphism and its inverse.
Within a single component, extend the corresponding automorphism by the
identity on other components. These extensions commute with every $f_i$.
Finally a commuting permutation preserves every path code. This proves the
three-way equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[Rooted codes and Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s recognition lemma, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
\Cref{nee:lem:recognize} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} recognizes a whole component type by an enumeration graph of the component, which is internal only when $\kappa>\omega$ or the component is finite. The rooted code $c_a$ uses only finite path graphs, one formula at a time, so \cref{nee:aa:thm:rootcode} holds at $\kappa=\omega$ for infinite components too; this is what lets \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel} cover the finite cutoff.
\end{writenote}

\begin{lemma}[Multiplicity and local symmetry]\label{nee:aa:lem:mult}
Let $X_t$ represent a nonempty connected labelled type $t$, let $\mu_t$ be its
multiplicity in $A$, and put $\delta_t=|\Aut(X_t)|$. Its local automorphism group
acts freely on $X_t$, has cardinality at most $\aleph_0$, and for every atom $a$
of that type,
\[
|\mathcal Z a|=\mu_t\delta_t.
\]
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~03, Lemma~3.3.}
\begin{proof}
An automorphism fixing one vertex fixes all finite signed paths from it and
hence the entire component. It is determined by the image of one vertex, so
there are at most countably many such automorphisms. In each target copy of the
type, the possible images of $a$ form a free orbit of cardinality $\delta_t$.
Every component isomorphism extends globally as in \cref{nee:aa:thm:rootcode}. Counting
these disjoint target-copy contributions gives the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[A generalization of a lemma of Part~VIII of the surreal report, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
For a uniformly named action of a pure group $\Gamma$ at the finite cutoff, Lemma~56.3 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/docs/foundations-and-computation/birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:lem:centralizer}) proves that $\mathcal Z a$ is infinite when the type $t$ has infinitely many copies, has $m_td_t$ elements when it has finitely many ($d_t=|\Aut_\Gamma(O_t)|$), and that $\Aut_\Gamma(O_t)$ acts freely on the orbit $O_t$; there every orbit is a transitive $\Gamma$-set and, in the applications, finite. \Cref{nee:aa:lem:mult} is the same statement for a finite tuple of \emph{injections} (not necessarily permutations, so a component need not be an orbit of a group) and for \emph{infinite} components, as one cardinal identity $|\mathcal Z a|=\mu_t\delta_t$ in which both factors may be infinite (with $\delta_t\leq\aleph_0$). The proofs are the same argument (an automorphism fixing one point of a connected structure is trivial; component isomorphisms extend to commuting permutations), written independently; source~03 cites Part~VIII only for its Theorem~59.1. Part~VIII's $m_t$, $d_t$ are $\mu_t$, $\delta_t$ here; its divisibility $d_t\mid n_t$ for a finite orbit has no counterpart for infinite components. A reciprocal note for Part~VIII is proposed separately.
\end{writenote}

\section{The exact atom kernel of definable closure}\label{nee:aa:sec:kernels}

Let $\mathscr R=\mathcal P(\NN)$ denote the pure reals. For a finite tuple of
internal parameters $\bar a$, write
\[
B=\bigcup_i\kerat(a_i),\qquad
\Sat(B)=\text{the union of components meeting }B,
\qquad D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa=\{b\in A:|\mathcal Z b|<\kappa\}.
\]
Use $\mathcal K(\dcl(\bar a,\mathscr R))$ for the external union of atom kernels
of objects uniquely definable using $\bar a$ and finitely many pure real
parameters. This is scheme notation as in \cref{nee:aa:rem:semantics}.

\begin{theorem}[Exact kernel formula]\label{nee:aa:thm:kernel}
For every infinite $\kappa$, every finite tuple of injective maps, and every
finite internal parameter tuple,
\begin{equation}\label{nee:aa:eq:kernel}
\boxed{\quad
\mathcal K(\dcl(\bar a,\mathscr R))=\Sat(B)\cup D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa.
\quad}
\end{equation}
Allowing arbitrary pure parameters instead of pure reals does not enlarge this
atom kernel.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~4.1 and its equation~(1).}
\begin{proof}
The set $B$ is definable from the parameters using the ordinary kernel operation.
Each fixed signed word $w$ induces a partial bijection on atoms. Its image
\[
B_w=\{b:\exists a\in B\ \Eval(w,a,b)\}
\]
has size at most $|B|<\kappa$ and is an internal atom set. Its exact defining
formula gives a unique object from $\bar a$. The sets $B_w$ cover $\Sat(B)$.
This uses neither individually definable elements of $B$ nor expanded
Replacement to collect the family of all $B_w$.

For $b\in D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa$, its rooted code $c_b$ defines exactly the
fiber $\mathcal Z b$ by \cref{nee:aa:thm:rootcode}. This fiber is an internal atom set,
since it has size below $\kappa$. It is uniquely specified from the pure real
$c_b$, and its kernel contains $b$. This proves the lower inclusion.

For the upper inclusion, a unique output $y$ from the given parameters and any
pure parameters is fixed by every member of $\mathcal Z$ fixing $B$ pointwise.
Its kernel is consequently invariant under $\mathcal Z_{(B)}$.
Suppose $b$ lies outside the right side of \eqref{nee:aa:eq:kernel}. Its component does
not meet $B$. Write $\mu,\delta$ for the multiplicity and local automorphism
cardinality of its type. Then $\mu\delta\geq\kappa$. Fewer than $\kappa$
components meet $B$. If $\delta\geq\kappa$, the automorphisms within the untouched
component already give at least $\kappa$ images of $b$. Otherwise cardinal
arithmetic gives $\mu\geq\kappa$; deleting fewer than $\kappa$ touched copies
leaves at least $\kappa$ target copies. In either case
$|\mathcal Z_{(B)}b|\geq\kappa$. An invariant kernel of size below $\kappa$ cannot
contain $b$. This proves the upper inclusion, and also proves that additional
pure parameters provide no larger kernel.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The finite-cutoff correction]\label{nee:aa:cor:finitekernel}
Let $A_t$ be the union of all copies of component type $t$. Then
\[
D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa
=\bigcup\{A_t:\mu_t\delta_t<\kappa\}.
\]
At $\kappa=\omega$, a type contributes exactly when it has finite multiplicity
and a finite local automorphism group. Its component may still be infinite.
At $\kappa>\omega$,
\[
D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa=\bigcup\{A_t:|A_t|<\kappa\}.
\]
\end{corollary}
\srcs{source~03, Corollary~4.2.}
\begin{proof}
Use \cref{nee:aa:lem:mult}. At an uncountable cutoff both $|X_t|$ and $\delta_t$ are at
most countable, and both are nonzero. Thus
$\mu_t\delta_t<\kappa$ if and only if $\mu_t|X_t|<\kappa$. At the finite
cutoff the two conditions differ when $X_t$ is infinite and $\delta_t$ is finite.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[Relation to Parts~\ref{nee:part:one} and~\ref{nee:ca:part}, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
$A_t$ is Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s type block $B_t$, so for $\kappa>\omega$ the corollary says $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa=D_\kappa(\boldsymbol f)$, and \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel} says that the bound $\operatorname{cl}_{\boldsymbol f}(P)\cup D_\kappa(\boldsymbol f)$ of \eqref{nee:eq:H}, for parameters with kernel union $P$, is exactly the atom kernel of the objects definable from them \emph{with pure real parameters}: the pure-real form of \cref{nee:q:dcl}, for every finite tuple of injections. \Cref{nee:ca:thm:dcl} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} proves, for commuting permutations, the same formula for \emph{ordinary} definability, at every cutoff. The two agree where both apply; the write checked this. For commuting permutations an infinite component is a transitive $\ZZ^r$-set $\ZZ^r/K$, whose translations by $\ZZ^r/K$ are automorphisms, so $\delta_t$ is infinite and the type does not contribute to $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega$; a finite component has $\delta_t=|\ZZ^r/K|$ finite, so it contributes exactly when $\mu_t$ is finite, that is, when its block is finite. Hence $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega=D_\omega(\boldsymbol p)$ for commuting permutations, matching \eqref{nee:ca:eq:dcl-exact}. For a single ray at $\kappa=\omega$ (\cref{nee:ca:ex:ray-dcl}), $\mu_t=\delta_t=1$, so $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega=A$ although $D_\omega=\varnothing$, which is the correction that example calls for. Whether ordinary definability gives the same kernel for injections is \cref{nee:aa:q:ordinary}.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Individually definable atoms and parameters}

\begin{theorem}[Exact atom closure for atom parameters]\label{nee:aa:thm:atoms}
If the impure parameters are a finite tuple of individually named atoms
$F=\{a_1,\ldots,a_n\}$, let $\mu_t^F$ count the components of type $t$ disjoint
from $\Sat(F)$. Then
\[
A\cap\dcl(F,\mathscr R)
=\Sat(F)\ \cup\!
\bigcup_{\mu_t^F\delta_t=1}
\{\text{atoms in untouched copies of type }t\}.
\]
In particular, without impure parameters an atom is definable from a pure real
exactly when its type occurs once and its component is rigid.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~4.3.}
\begin{proof}
Every atom in $\Sat(F)$ is the unique endpoint of a fixed feasible word from a
named atom. Every member of $\mathcal Z_{(F)}$ fixes these components pointwise.
On untouched components, all component swaps and local automorphisms remain
available. Thus an untouched atom has stabilizer-orbit size $\mu_t^F\delta_t$.
This orbit is defined by its rooted code together with the requirement of being
outside $\Sat(F)$; saturation is expressible by existence of a finite path.
A singleton orbit supplies a definition. A larger orbit supplies an automorphism
fixing all parameters and moving the atom, precluding a definition.
\end{proof}

For arbitrary set parameters $\bar a$, the same singleton criterion is exact
\emph{outside} $\Sat(B)$, with $\mu_t^B$ counting untouched components. Inside
$\Sat(B)$, an unordered set parameter need not individually name its members.
For example, two interchangeable atoms form a definable two-element set without
either being individually definable. The full kernel formula
\eqref{nee:aa:eq:kernel} intentionally retains this distinction.

\subsection{Why pure parameters cannot be omitted}\label{nee:aa:sec:pureparams}

Consider a family of $\cont$ pairwise nonisomorphic rigid countable components,
one copy of each, for two commuting injections. Such a family is constructed
explicitly in \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}. Its global automorphism group is trivial, so
$D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega=A$ has cardinality $\cont$. Every atom is definable from
its pure real rooted code. Yet there are only countably many parameter-free
formulas. Each uniquely defined internal object has a finite atom kernel, so
the parameter-free definable kernel is at most countable. This counting
observation is external semantic reasoning, for example after placing the
construction in a set-sized ambient model whose atom set is uncountable in
a larger metatheory with access to its satisfaction relation; it is not an
internal enumeration of definitions.
It shows that the
pure-parameter qualification is mathematically necessary, even with a rigid
underlying atom structure.

\begin{writenote}[A metatheoretic argument, kept as a remark, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
The preceding paragraph is not a theorem of the framework of \cref{nee:aa:rem:semantics}, and source~03 does not state it as one. There, ``uniquely definable'' is read one fixed formula at a time; the union over \emph{all} formulas of the kernels of parameter-free definable objects is not an object that the scheme reading defines, so ``that union is at most countable'' is not one assertion of the ambient theory. What the framework does prove, for each fixed finite set of formulas, is that the kernels of the objects they define without parameters have a finite union. The counting argument becomes a proof once the ambient universe is a set-sized model $W$ inside a larger universe that has its satisfaction relation: then the parameter-free definable objects of the expanded model built in $W$ are countably many, each with a finite kernel. The intended contrast with $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega=A$, ``of cardinality $\cont$'', needs in addition that $A$ be uncountable in the larger universe (for a countable $W$ it is countable there), and it needs $W$ to satisfy enough of the ambient theory for \cref{nee:aa:thm:types,nee:aa:thm:rootcode} to hold inside it. Neither source states or proves such a version, and the write does not supply one. The claim is recorded, with what is missing, as \cref{nee:aa:q:parameterfree}. Nothing else in this Part depends on it: \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel,nee:aa:thm:atoms} are stated with pure real parameters precisely so as not to need it.
\end{writenote}

An infinite component still violates atom-valued Replacement at the finite
cutoff when an atom parameter is allowed: send each feasible signed word to its
endpoint and every infeasible word to the starting atom. The domain $W$ is pure,
the outputs are single atoms, and the range is the infinite component. Definable
finite objects inside a component do not imply that their total family can be
collected internally.

\subsection{An exact reduction for ordinary finite-kernel definability}

The pure-parameter theorem also yields an exact statement when no extra
parameters are automatically supplied. This statement identifies the remaining
issue as definability of pure codes.

\begin{proposition}[Ordinary definability at the finite cutoff]\label{nee:aa:prop:ordinary}
Assume $\kappa=\omega$, and put
$\mathscr D_{\bar a}=\mathscr R\cap\dcl(\bar a)$, with ordinary definable closure.
For a code $c$, let $F_c=\{b\in A:\Code(c,b)\}$. Then
\[
\mathcal K(\dcl(\bar a))
=\Sat(B)\cup\bigcup\{F_c:c\in\mathscr D_{\bar a},\ |F_c|<\omega\}.
\]
\end{proposition}
\srcs{source~03, Proposition~4.4.}
\begin{proof}
The saturation term is obtained by fixed-word images of $B$, as before. If $c$
is definable from $\bar a$ and $F_c$ is finite, the exact fiber is an internal
set definable from $\bar a$.

Conversely, let $y$ be definable from $\bar a$ and let $K=\kerat(y)$, a definable
finite atom set. The set of rooted codes of members of $K$ is a finite pure
set, exists internally, and is uniquely specified from $\bar a$. Order its
distinct reals lexicographically, using the least natural number at which two
codes differ. Each member of this finite ordered set is individually definable
by its finite position. Thus each code appearing in $K$ belongs to
$\mathscr D_{\bar a}$. For an atom of $K$ outside $\Sat(B)$, the invariance
argument in \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel} forces its global fiber to be finite. This proves
the upper inclusion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{All objects at the finite cutoff}

\begin{theorem}[Fixed objects and pure parameters]\label{nee:aa:thm:fixedobjects}
At $\kappa=\omega$, for every finite tuple $\bar a$ of arbitrary internal
parameters,
\[
\dcl(\bar a,\Pure)
=\{x:\widehat\pi(x)=x\text{ for all }\pi\in\mathcal Z
\text{ satisfying }\widehat\pi(\bar a)=\bar a\}.
\]
This concerns all internal objects, not just atoms or their kernels.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~4.5.}
\begin{proof}
Automorphism invariance gives the forward inclusion. For the reverse, fix such
an $x$ and enumerate the finite set
$\kerat(x)\cup\bigcup_i\kerat(a_i)$ as $b_1,\ldots,b_s$.
Code the complete signed-word equality diagram of this tuple by a pure real:
for all $i,j,u,v$, record whether paths $u$ from $b_i$ and $v$ from $b_j$
exist and have the same endpoint. Its diagonal includes feasibility. A tuple
$b'_1,\ldots,b'_s$ realizes the same diagram exactly when the word map gives
an isomorphism between the two saturated unions of components, carrying each
$b_i$ to $b'_i$.

This isomorphism extends to a member of $\mathcal Z$. It matches equal finite
numbers of touched components of every type. Removing equal finite numbers
from the same component multiplicity leaves equal complementary multiplicities,
whether finite or infinite. Choose component isomorphisms on the remaining
copies and extend the given partial isomorphism. Infinite touched components
cause no difficulty; the matching is external.

Take also a pure code for the well-founded membership structure on
$\TC(\{x,a_1,\ldots,a_n\})$. Concretely, enumerate this external set by an
ordinal, record its membership relation, mark its atom nodes by
$1,\ldots,s$, and mark the distinguished roots. All this information is pure.
Given atom labels $b'_1,\ldots,b'_s$, a realization is a function on the pure
index set satisfying the recorded membership equations, with these labels at
the atom nodes and a set at every unmarked node. Its graph has a finite atom
kernel and belongs to the model.
Existence follows by well-founded recursion and uniqueness by well-founded
induction on the coded relation.

Define $y$ by requiring such a labelled realization whose parameter roots equal
the actual $\bar a$ and whose distinguished $x$-root equals $y$, with the labels
realizing the signed-word diagram. Every realization is obtained from the
original one by a commuting atom permutation. Equality of the parameter roots
forces that permutation to fix $\bar a$, and the hypothesis then forces
$y=x$. The original realization proves existence. The definition uses only
$\bar a$ and finitely many pure codes.
\end{proof}

The membership code in this theorem can be large and need not be a real.
Accordingly the theorem states $\Pure$, not $\mathscr R$. It is an explicit
coding theorem in this particular well-founded setting; it does not assume
that automorphism-invariance implies definability in arbitrary structures.

\section{Nonabelian actions and subgroup normalizers}\label{nee:aa:sec:groups}

Suppose the named permutations generate an action of a finitely generated
group $G$. A transitive component is $G/H$, where $H$ is a point stabilizer.
The type depends on the conjugacy class $[H]$. The standard formula
\cite[Theorem 8]{Boardman} is
\[
\Aut_G(G/H)\cong N_G(H)/H,
\]
with the harmless opposite-group convention if a different choice of left or
right action is used. In particular its cardinality is $[N_G(H):H]$.

\begin{corollary}[Normalizer formulas]\label{nee:aa:cor:normalizer}
If $\mu_{[H]}$ is the multiplicity of type $G/H$, then
\[
D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\kappa
=\bigcup_{\mu_{[H]}[N_G(H):H]<\kappa} A_{[H]}.
\]
An atom is definable from pure real parameters alone exactly when
\[
\mu_{[H]}=1\quad\text{and}\quad N_G(H)=H.
\]
For finite atom parameters $F$, replace $\mu_{[H]}$ by the residual multiplicity
$\mu_{[H]}^F$ outside $GF$, and include every atom of $GF$.
For arbitrary parameter tuples the same residual criterion is exact outside
the saturation of their kernels; \cref{nee:aa:thm:fixedobjects} gives the full
fixed-point characterization at $\kappa=\omega$.
\end{corollary}
\srcs{source~03, Corollary~5.1.}
\begin{proof}
An equivariant map of $G/H$ is determined by the image $gH$ of $H$. It is an
automorphism exactly when the image has stabilizer $H$, equivalently
$gHg^{-1}=H$. The possible images are therefore the cosets of $H$ in its
normalizer. Apply \cref{nee:aa:lem:mult,nee:aa:thm:kernel,nee:aa:thm:atoms}.
\end{proof}

The normalizer index cannot be replaced by the size $[G:H]$ of the component.
Nor can the residual multiplicity be replaced by the original multiplicity:
if there are exactly two fixed atoms and one is individually named, the other
is the unique remaining fixed atom and becomes definable.

\subsection{Infinite dihedral layers}\label{nee:aa:sec:dihedral}

Let $D_\infty=\langle r,t:t^2=1,\ trt=r^{-1}\rangle$, and act by
$D_\infty\times C_m$ on $A=\ZZ\times C_m$ through
\[
r(n,j)=(n+1,j),\qquad t(n,j)=(-n,j),\qquad z(n,j)=(n,j+1).
\]
The stabilizer of $(0,0)$ is $H=\langle t\rangle\times\{0\}$, and direct
calculation gives
\[
N_G(H)=\langle t\rangle\times C_m,\qquad
[N_G(H):H]=m,\qquad [G:H]=\aleph_0.
\]
There is one infinite component, but its commuting-automorphism orbits are the
finite layers $L_n=\{n\}\times C_m$. The layer $L_0$ is the set of atoms fixed
by $t$, and $L_n=r^nL_0$. Thus every atom belongs to the kernel of a
parameter-free definable finite set in $M_\omega(A)$. For $m>1$ no individual
atom is definable from pure parameters, because cyclic rotation moves it.
For $m=1$ every atom is individually parameter-free definable.

With $q$ identical copies, each pointed fiber has size $qm$. Finite nonzero
$q$ preserves the finite-kernel phenomenon; infinite $q$ removes every atom
from the pure-parameter definable kernel. This gives explicit instances of all
three factors: component infinitude, internal symmetry, and multiplicity.

\begin{writenote}[Question~23.1, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
This is the ``useful first target'' of \cref{nee:ca:q:nonabelian}, the infinite dihedral group, worked out (here as $D_\infty\times C_m$, with $m=1$ giving $D_\infty$ itself). With \cref{nee:aa:thm:atoms,nee:aa:cor:normalizer} it answers that question for finitely many atom parameters with pure real parameters, and with \cref{nee:aa:thm:fixedobjects} at $\kappa=\omega$ for arbitrary parameters with arbitrary pure parameters; the dated note after the question records what stays open (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}).
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Two involutions can jointly change Replacement}\label{nee:aa:sec:involutions}

On atoms indexed by $\ZZ$, put $s(n)=1-n$ and $t(n)=-n$. The expansion of
$M_\omega(A)$ by either single involution preserves Replacement: each has only
finite cycles and finitely many types; for $t$ include its unique fixed atom
in the finite kernel reservoir. Yet jointly, $t$ defines atom $0$ and
$(s\circ t)(n)=n+1$. The parameter-free formula
\[
k\longmapsto (s\circ t)^k(0),\qquad k\in\NN,
\]
defined by finite paths, has an infinite atom range. It is a failed
Replacement instance. The symbols $0,k$ in the formula mean, respectively,
the uniquely specified $t$-fixed atom and a pure natural number; they are
different sorts of objects.

\begin{writenote}[Relation to earlier joint failures, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
The phenomenon is that of \cref{nee:cor:jointunsafe} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} (two permutations each safe alone, jointly failing Replacement), here at the finite cutoff and with involutions; there the cutoff is $\aleph_1$ and the joint failure comes from continuum many component types. Theorem~61.3 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} gives, at $\kappa=\omega$, two involutions each harmless alone \emph{with all joint orbits finite} whose joint expansion keeps exactly the levels $\mathsf R_k$, $k<d$. In the example above the joint orbit is infinite ($s$ and $t$ generate an infinite dihedral group acting transitively on $\ZZ$), so Replacement fails already for single-atom outputs; the example is not one of the finite-orbit pairs asked about in Part~VIII's Question~65.7 (\texttt{hset:ns:q:joining}), and it answers no question.
\end{writenote}

\section{Commutative relations and localization}\label{nee:aa:sec:localization}
\label{nee:ci:sec:normal}

From this point let $M$ be a finitely generated \emph{commutative} monoid with
fixed labelled generators $x_1,\ldots,x_r$. An action by injective maps means
that each generator acts injectively. Surjectivity is allowed; it is not
required. Isomorphisms preserve each operation label. We avoid the phrase
``injective act,'' which in the monoid literature usually denotes a different,
categorical extension property.

Let $G=M^{\mathrm{gp}}$ be the Grothendieck group and $P\subseteq G$ the image
of $M$. Thus $P$ is the universal cancellative image of the original monoid.
The original presentation need not be cancellative.

\emph{Source~04's conventions} (its Section~2.1).
We write monoids additively, with identity $0$. An $M$-action on a
set $X$ is \emph{injective} if every translation $x\mapsto p\cdot x$
is injective. It need not be faithful, and an injective translation need
not be surjective. Choose a finite generating list for $M$. The
action is \emph{connected} if the undirected graph joining $x$ to
$p_i\cdot x$ is connected. This property is independent of the chosen
finite generating list.

The Grothendieck group is constructed from pairs of monoid elements;
the images of $p,q\in M$ agree precisely when
\[
\exists r\in M\quad p+r=q+r.
\]
Consequently $P=\im(M\to G)$ is a cancellative commutative monoid
embedded in $G$, and $G=P-P$.

\begin{lemma}[Cancellation forced by injectivity]\label{nee:aa:lem:cancellation}
\label{nee:ci:lem:cancellation}
Actions of $M$ by injective maps are exactly actions of $P$ by injective maps,
pulled back along $M\to P$. This identification includes their equivariant maps
and connected components.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~03, Lemma~6.1; source~04, Lemma~2.1 (``Injective actions see only cancellativization''; the second sentence is its addition).}
\begin{proof}
Two elements $a,b\in M$ have the same group-completion image exactly when
$a+c=b+c$ for some $c\in M$. Their action maps satisfy
$T_c\circ T_a=T_c\circ T_b$. Since $T_c$ is injective, $T_a=T_b$.
The action therefore factors through $P$. The converse is immediate.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
If $p+r=q+r$, then
\[
r\cdot(p\cdot x)=r\cdot(q\cdot x).
\]
Injectivity of the action of $r$ gives $p\cdot x=q\cdot x$.
Thus the action factors uniquely through $P$. The factored maps
remain injective, and their images contain the original generators.
Equivariance and the component relation are unchanged.
\end{proof}

For example, a relation $f^2g=f$ between commuting injections forces
$fg=\id$. Both maps are then bijections. The number of generators that look
noninvertible in a presentation is consequently an unreliable invariant.

Source~04's example: this elementary observation prevents a significant ambiguity.
The relation $f^2=f$ does not make $f$ the identity
in the original idempotent monoid, but it forces $f=\mathrm{id}$
in every injective action. All unit groups in our statements refer
to the effective monoid $P$.

\emph{A direct construction of the localization} (source~04, Section~2.2).
For an injective $P$-set $X$, form pairs $(x,p)\in X\times P$ and put
\[
(x,p)\sim(y,q)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad q\cdot x=p\cdot y.
\]
Transitivity uses cancellation of an acting element: from
$q\cdot x=p\cdot y$ and $r\cdot y=q\cdot z$, applying $r$ and $p$
and cancelling $q$ gives $r\cdot x=p\cdot z$. Denote the quotient
by $X^{\mathrm{loc}}$, and write $[x,p]$ for a class.
For $a,b\in P$, define
\[
(a-b)\cdot[x,p]=[a\cdot x,p+b].
\]
The displayed equivalence and injectivity verify independence of all
representatives. The resulting action is a $G$-action.
The map $x\mapsto[x,0]$ is an injective equivariant map.

This construction is the usual localization mechanism for injective
commutative monoid actions; \cite[\S2.1]{ChanLevine} provides related
background. We give the construction because its uniqueness properties
are needed in the counting argument.

\begin{theorem}[Localized component classification]\label{nee:aa:thm:localization}
\label{nee:ci:thm:normal}
Every nonempty connected action of $P$ by injective maps is isomorphic to
translation on a nonempty subset $S\subseteq G/H$, for some subgroup $H\leq G$, such that
$S+P\subseteq S$. Conversely every such subset is connected under the labelled
generator maps. Two descriptions $(H,S)$ and $(H',S')$ give isomorphic labelled
actions exactly when $H=H'$ and $S'=S+q$ for some $q\in G/H$.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~6.2; source~04, Theorem~2.2 (``Translation normal form'').}
\begin{proof}
For an action on $X$, form pairs $(p,x)\in P\times X$ and set
\[
(p,x)\sim(q,y)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad T_qx=T_py.
\]
This is an equivalence relation. For transitivity, apply $T_s$ and $T_p$ to
$T_qx=T_py$ and $T_sy=T_qz$, then cancel the injective $T_q$. The map
$x\mapsto[0,x]$ is injective. On the quotient, $T_m[p,x]=[p,T_mx]$ becomes
invertible, with inverse $[p,x]\mapsto[p+m,x]$. Hence the action extends to $G$.

Two original points are in the same connected component exactly when they
have a common forward image $T_ax=T_by$: this relation is an equivalence
relation containing the generator edges, and every such equality supplies
an undirected path. Therefore a connected component localizes to one
transitive $G$-set, necessarily $G/H$. Its embedded image is $P$-stable.

Conversely, for $s,t\in S$ write $t-s=a-b$ modulo $H$, with $a,b\in P$.
Then $s+a=t+b$ is a common forward point in $S$, proving connectedness.
Translation gives injective maps.

An isomorphism extends to the localized transitive actions. As $G$ is abelian,
its stabilizer is independent of the basepoint, so the stabilizers agree.
An equivariant isomorphism between these coset actions is translation.
Conversely translation taking $S$ onto $S'$ preserves all labels.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
Localizing a connected action makes all generator steps invertible,
so its localization is a transitive $G$-set. Choose a base point;
its stabilizer $H$ identifies the localization with $G/H$.
The image of $X$ is the required $P$-invariant subset.

For the converse, take $s,t\in S$. Since $G=P-P$, there are
$a,b\in P$ with $t-s=a-b$ modulo $H$. Then
\[
s+a=t+b.
\]
Both paths to this common upper point stay in $S$, establishing
connectedness.

An equivariant isomorphism $S\to S'$ extends uniquely to their
localizations by $[x,p]\mapsto[\varphi(x),p]$.
Equivariant isomorphisms between transitive abelian $G$-sets preserve
stabilizers exactly, so an isomorphism requires $H=H'$.
After this identification it is translation by the image of one
base point. Restricting such a translation proves the reverse direction.
\end{proof}

This explicit construction is the commutative specialization of the standard
act-of-quotients construction \cite[Section 6]{BaileyRenshaw}. We need both
the embedding and the isomorphism criterion, since merely bounding component
cardinalities would not count their types.

\begin{remark}[Labels and point stabilizers]\label{nee:ci:rem:labels}
The isomorphisms preserve the action of each monoid element. Permuting
generators or applying an automorphism of $G$ is not allowed.
The subgroup $H$ is the stabilizer of a \emph{point} in the
localization. It is different from the group of translations preserving
the subset $S$ as a whole, which is the automorphism group of the
component.
\end{remark}
\srcs{source~04, Remark~2.3.}

\begin{corollary}[Periods are local automorphisms]\label{nee:aa:cor:periods}
For a component $S\subseteq G/H$,
\[
\Aut_P(S)\cong\Per(S):=\{q\in G/H:S+q=S\}.
\]
At the finite cutoff, its type contributes to the pure-parameter definable
kernel exactly when its multiplicity and $\Per(S)$ are both finite.
For a torsion-free $G/H$, a finite period group is necessarily trivial.
\end{corollary}
\srcs{source~03, Corollary~6.3 (stated without a separate proof: the first assertion is the isomorphism criterion of \cref{nee:aa:thm:localization} with $S'=S$, the second is \cref{nee:aa:cor:finitekernel}).}

\emph{Removing unit directions} (source~04, Section~2.3).
Let
\[
U=P\cap(-P).
\]
This is a subgroup of $G$, equal to the unit group of $P$.
Every $P$-invariant subset of $G/H$ is invariant under the image
of $U$, in both directions. It is therefore the full inverse image
of a subset of
\[
G/(H+U).
\]
This simple quotient accounts for the effective number of one-way
directions. A finitely generated abelian group has countably many
subgroups: each subgroup is finitely generated, and there are only
countably many finite tuples.
The quotient $G/U$ may have torsion (\cref{nee:aa:rem:torsion}).
Throughout, $\rank$ means the
dimension after tensoring with $\QQ$; we never silently discard
torsion from the action classification.

\section{The finite/countable/continuum theorem}\label{nee:aa:sec:trichotomy}
\label{nee:ci:sec:types}

\subsection{Units and the dimension that matters}

Put
\[
U=P\cap(-P),\qquad Q=G/U,\qquad \overline P=\operatorname{im}(P\to Q),
\qquad d=\rank(Q).
\]
The elements of $U$ are precisely those monoid motions with monoid inverses.
The integer $d$ counts independent directions remaining after these units
are removed. It can differ from both $r$ and $\rank(G)$.

\begin{lemma}[Cone, units, and positive height]\label{nee:aa:lem:cone}
\label{nee:ci:lem:cone}
Let $V=G\otimes_\ZZ\RR$, let $C\subseteq V$ be the cone generated by the images
of the monoid generators, and let $L=C\cap(-C)$. Then
\[
\spanR(U)=L,\qquad d=\dim V-\dim L.
\]
The monoid $\overline P$ has no nonzero units and no nonzero torsion elements.
If $d>0$, there is a homomorphism $\ell:Q\to\ZZ$ with
\[
\ell(p)>0\quad(p\in\overline P\setminus\{0\}).
\]
When $\overline P=\{0\}$, take $\ell=0$.
If $d=0$, then $P=G$.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~03, Lemma~7.1; source~04, Lemma~3.1 (``Unit directions are exactly the lineality directions'', which states $U\otimes_\ZZ\RR=L$ and the positive height, including the convention $\ell=0$).}
\begin{proof}
Write $p_i$ for generator images in $G$ and $v_i$ for their images in $V$.
A generator is a unit exactly when $-v_i\in C$. The nontrivial implication
uses a nonnegative rational expression for $-v_i$. Clear denominators and
then multiply by the exponent of the finite torsion subgroup of $G$ to obtain
\[
Np_i+\sum_j c_jp_j=0,\qquad N\geq1,\ c_j\geq0.
\]
It follows that $-p_i=(N-1)p_i+\sum_jc_jp_j\in P$.

Every unit is a sum of unit generators: if $u$ and $-u$ are expressed using
generators, their sum is a nonnegative relation equalling zero; each generator
occurring in it has its inverse obtained by rearrangement. Also $L$ is spanned
by the $v_i$ lying in $L$: express $v\in L$ and $-v$ in $C$ and add the
expressions. Every generator with positive coefficient in the resulting zero
relation has its negative in $C$. These observations prove the span identity.

If $p,q\in P$ and $p+q\in U$, then $-p=q-(p+q)\in P$. Hence any unit in
$\overline P$ is zero. A torsion element of a monoid inside a group is a unit,
so $\overline P$ contains no nonzero torsion. Applying the span identity to
$\overline P$ shows that its rational cone is pointed. Choose a rational
functional strictly positive on this cone away from zero and clear
denominators on the free quotient of $Q$. This gives $\ell$; finite torsion
is killed by it. These standard cone facts are also developed in
\cite[Lemmas 3.4.4--3.4.5]{Koch}.

If $d=0$, the finitely generated $Q$ is finite. Its submonoid $\overline P$
is then a group, but has no nonzero units, so is zero. It generates $Q$,
forcing $Q=0$ and $P=U=G$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof of the span identity and the positive height (source~04)]
Units clearly map into $L$. If a generator $p$ maps into $L$,
then $-p$, in $V$, is a nonnegative rational combination of
generator images. Clear denominators, and then multiply by the
order of the remaining torsion discrepancy in $G$. This gives
$-Np\in P$ for a positive integer $N$. Hence
\[
-p=(N-1)p+(-Np)\in P,
\]
so $p$ is a unit.

Every vector of $L$ is a nonnegative combination of generator
images lying in $L$. Indeed, if $v=\sum a_i p_i\in L$ with
$a_i>0$, then
$-a_i p_i=-v+\sum_{j\ne i}a_jp_j\in C$, whence $p_i\in L$.
Thus the unit generators span $L$.

The quotient cone in $V/L$ is pointed and rational. A rational
linear functional can be chosen strictly positive on its nonzero
generating rays. For completeness, normalize those finitely many
nonzero rational generators to points on a sphere. Their convex hull
does not contain zero, because a nontrivial nonnegative dependence
summing to zero would give a line in the cone. Separation from zero
gives a strictly positive functional; a rational approximation
preserves the finitely many strict inequalities. Clearing denominators
gives an integral homomorphism on the lattice $G/U$ modulo torsion.

If an element $a\in\overline P$ had zero height, a representation as
a sum of generators could use only unit generators. Hence $a=0$.
This establishes the stated positivity including the possible
torsion in $G/U$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Torsion must be retained]\label{nee:aa:rem:torsion}
$Q$ need not be torsion-free. For example, the submonoid generated by
$(1,0),(1,1)$ in $\ZZ\oplus C_2$ has $U=0$. Its positive height is the first
coordinate. The rank-one argument below uses finitely many cyclic cosets,
so it includes this case.
\end{remark}
\srcs{source~03, Remark~7.2; source~04, Section~2.3 (the same example: ``one has $U=0$, so the torsion survives'').}

\begin{lemma}[Finite height levels]\label{nee:ci:lem:levels}
For the positive height above, each set
$\{a\in\overline P:\ell(a)=h\}$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~04, Lemma~3.2.}
\begin{proof}
Discard zero generators of $\overline P$. All remaining generators
have positive integer heights. At a fixed nonnegative height, each
coefficient in a nonnegative generator expression is bounded.
There are only finitely many such expressions. Negative levels
are empty.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact type count}

Let $\tau(M)$ be the number of isomorphism types of nonempty connected labelled
actions by injective maps.

\begin{theorem}[Type-count trichotomy]\label{nee:aa:thm:types}
\label{nee:ci:thm:summary}
For every finitely generated commutative monoid,
\begin{equation}\label{nee:aa:eq:trichotomy}
\boxed{\quad
\tau(M)=
\begin{cases}
|\{H:H\leq G\}|=|\Sub(G)|,&G\text{ finite},\\
\aleph_0,&G\text{ infinite and }d\leq1,\\
\cont,&d\geq2.
\end{cases}\quad}
\end{equation}
Isomorphisms preserve the named generators.
In the last case, already the rigid connected components have exactly
$\cont$ types, all with the same localization stabilizer $U$, and there is a
continuously coded Cantor family of pairwise nonisomorphic rigid components
(\cref{nee:ci:thm:cantor}).
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~7.3 and its equation~(2); source~04, Theorem~1.1 (``The type classification, summarized'', with the Cantor-family clause), proved in its Section~3.4 (second proof below).}
\begin{proof}
Every subgroup of a finitely generated abelian group is finitely generated.
Thus $G$ has at most countably many subgroups. The localization theorem reduces
the problem to invariant subsets of the countable coset groups, up to
translations. This also supplies the general upper bound $\cont$.

If $G$ is finite, its submonoid $P$ is all of $G$. A nonempty invariant subset
of a transitive coset action is the whole action, giving exactly one type
for each subgroup. If $d=0$ with $G$ infinite, the same argument gives at most
countably many types.

Suppose $d=1$. Pull back a $P$-invariant subset of $G/H$ to $G$. It is invariant
under both $u$ and $-u$ for $u\in U$, and so is the inverse image of a
$\overline P$-invariant subset of $Q$. Choose nonzero $p\in\overline P$.
It has infinite order, and $\ZZ p$ has finite index in $Q$. On each of the
finitely many cyclic cosets $q_i+\ZZ p$, a subset invariant under $+p$ is
empty, the whole coset, or
\[
\{q_i+np:n\geq b_i\}\quad(b_i\in\ZZ).
\]
It is therefore encoded by finitely many cutoffs in
$\ZZ\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\}$. There are only countably many choices. This
bounds the possibilities for every $H$, and hence the total type count.
An infinite finitely generated abelian $G$ has a quotient $\ZZ$, and therefore
transitive actions of every finite size through $\ZZ/k\ZZ$. The image of $P$
generates each finite quotient also as a monoid. These different cardinalities
give infinitely many types, proving the countably infinite case.

Finally assume $d\geq2$. Use \cref{nee:aa:lem:cone} to choose $\ell$, and choose an
infinite-order $v\in\ker\ell$, whose rank is $d-1\geq1$. For each
$R\subseteq\NN$ set
\begin{equation}\label{nee:aa:eq:antichain}
A_R=\{0\}\cup\{(n+1)v:n\in R\},\qquad
S_R=A_R+\overline P\subseteq Q.
\end{equation}
It is a nonempty stable subset and hence a connected action. Positivity gives
\[
S_R\cap\ker\ell=A_R,\qquad\min\ell[S_R]=0.
\]
An isomorphism $S_R\to S_T$ is translation by $q\in Q$. Equality of minimum
heights gives $\ell(q)=0$, so $A_T=A_R+q$. Since $0$ lies in both sets,
$q\in A_T$ and $-q\in A_R$. Both are nonnegative multiples of the same
infinite-order $v$, forcing $q=0$. Thus $R=T$. The same argument for $R=T$
shows rigidity. This is an explicit injection of $\mathcal P(\NN)$ into the
type set; no choice of representatives of translation classes is needed.
The stabilizer of the localized $G$-action on $Q$ is $U$. The upper bound
already proved completes the trichotomy.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/03-atom-actions-antichain_upsets.pdf}
\caption{Finite windows of the construction in $\ZZ^2$, with
$\overline P=\NN^2$, $\ell(x,y)=x+y$, and $v=(1,-1)$. The orange zero-height
points recover the chosen subset of positive multiples of $v$; adding the
quadrant gives the blue points. In the theorem the chosen subset may be
infinite. The drawing illustrates the construction, rather than serving as
evidence for its infinite cardinality. (Source~03, Figure~1.)}
\label{nee:aa:fig:antichain}
\end{figure}

\emph{Source~04's route to the trichotomy} (its Sections~3.2--3.4).

\begin{proposition}[Countability in rank at most one]\label{nee:ci:prop:countable}
If $d=\rank(G/U)\leq1$, then there are at most countably many
connected injective $P$-action types.
\end{proposition}
\srcs{source~04, Proposition~3.3; the same argument as the case $d=1$ in source~03's proof of \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}.}
\begin{proof}
Fix $H\leq G$. By the normal form and unit invariance, it suffices
to count invariant subsets of $Q=G/(H+U)$. This group is finite
or has rank one.

In the rank-one case the image of some $p\in P$ has infinite
order, since the monoid image generates $Q$ as a group.
The cyclic subgroup $\ZZ p$ has finite index in $Q$.
On each coset $r+\ZZ p$, closure under $+p$ means that the
subset is empty, the whole coset, or a tail
\[
\{r+np:n\geq k\}\qquad(k\in\ZZ).
\]
Thus finitely many values in $\ZZ\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\}$
determine the subset. There are at most countably many choices.
The finite case is immediate. Finally, $G$ has countably many
subgroups $H$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A Cantor family of rigid components]\label{nee:ci:thm:cantor}
If $d\geq2$, there is a continuous map from $2^\NN$ to the
space of subsets of $G/U$ whose images are connected injective
action carriers, are pairwise nonisomorphic, and have trivial
equivariant automorphism groups.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~04, Theorem~3.4; its family is source~03's \eqref{nee:aa:eq:antichain}, with continuity added.}
\begin{proof}
Put $Q=G/U$. Choose the positive integral
height $\ell$. Its kernel has positive rank, so choose an element
$v\in\ker\ell$ of infinite order. For $R\in2^\NN$, put
\[
A_R=\{0\}\cup\{(n+1)v:n\in R\},\qquad S_R=A_R+\overline P.
\]
Then $S_R$ is nonempty and $\overline P$-invariant, and hence connected.
Positivity gives
\[
\min\ell[S_R]=0,\qquad S_R\cap\ker\ell=A_R.
\]
If $S_R+t=S_T$, their minimum heights force $\ell(t)=0$.
Their zero-height traces satisfy $A_R+t=A_T$.
Since both traces lie in $\NN v$ and contain zero, first
$t\in\ZZ v$, and then the minimum integer coefficient in each
trace forces $t=0$. It follows that $R=T$. The same argument
with $R=T$ proves rigidity, using \cref{nee:aa:thm:localization}.

For a fixed $a\in Q$, membership in $S_R$ asks whether
$a=b+n$ for $b\in A_R$ and $n\in\overline P$.
Necessarily $\ell(n)=\ell(a)$, and there are only finitely many
such $n$ by \cref{nee:ci:lem:levels}. Membership therefore depends on
finitely many bits of $R$. This is precisely continuity into
the product space $2^Q$.
\end{proof}

\centering

    ((\x>=3)&&(\y>=-3))||((\x>=4)&&(\y>=-4))}
   \ifnum\inside=1
    \fill[accent!65] (\x,\y) circle (2.3pt);
   \else
    \fill[black!15] (\x,\y) circle (1.4pt);
   \fi
  }
 }
 \draw[->,black!50] (-3.6,0)--(6.8,0) node[right] {$x$};
 \draw[->,black!50] (0,-5.6)--(0,5.8) node[above] {$y$};
 \draw[dashed,accent] (-3,3)--(5,-5);
 \foreach \x/\y in {0/0,1/-1,3/-3,4/-4}{
  \fill[ink] (\x,\y) circle (4pt);
 }
 \node[anchor=west] at (7,3.3) {$\overline P=\NN^2$};
 \node[anchor=west] at (7,2.1) {$\ell(x,y)=x+y$};
 \node[anchor=west] at (7,.9) {$v=(1,-1)$};
 \node[anchor=west] at (7,-.5) {$A_R=\{0,v,3v,4v\}$};

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A finite window of a component from the higher-rank construction.
The dark points on height zero encode the chosen bits; each generates
its northeast quadrant. The proof concerns the entire infinite subset,
not this finite window. (Source~04, Figure~1, drawn in TikZ; its labels $M$, $B$, $S$ are printed $\overline P$, $A_R$, $S_R$.)}
\label{nee:ci:fig:upset}
\end{figure}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} (source~04)]
If $G$ is finite, its submonoid $P$ is a group: positive powers
of an element include its inverse. Since $P$ generates $G$,
one has $P=G$. A nonempty invariant subset of $G/H$ is the
whole quotient, so there is exactly one type for each subgroup.

If $G$ is infinite, it has a quotient $\ZZ$. Reducing this
quotient modulo each positive integer $n$ supplies connected
actions of cardinality $n$, and hence infinitely many types.
\Cref{nee:ci:prop:countable} therefore yields exactly $\aleph_0$ when
$d\leq1$.

When $d\geq2$, \cref{nee:ci:thm:cantor} is the continuum lower bound.
For the upper bound, there are countably many subgroups and at
most continuum many subsets of each countable group $G/H$.
This completes all cases.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Why finite generation matters}\label{nee:aa:sec:finitegeneration}

\begin{example}[Rank one without finite generation]\label{nee:aa:ex:rationalcuts}
\label{nee:ci:ex:rationals}
The additive monoid of all nonnegative rational numbers has group completion
$\QQ$ and rational rank one, but the invariant cuts
$S_\alpha=\{q\in\QQ:q>\alpha\}$, for $\alpha\in\RR$, have continuum many
types modulo rational translation. Each translation class contains only
countably many cuts. Thus rational rank alone does not extend
\cref{nee:aa:thm:types} to infinitely generated monoids. The finite-index cyclic
subgroup used in the countable case is indispensable.

In source~04's words: let $P=\QQ_{\geq0}$ inside $G=\QQ$. Its unit
group is zero and its rational rank is one.
For $\alpha\in\RR$, let
$S_\alpha=\{q\in\QQ:q>\alpha\}$.
These are nonempty connected invariant subsets.
They are translation-isomorphic exactly when
$\alpha-\beta\in\QQ$.
Each isomorphism class contains at most countably
many parameters, so there are continuum many
types, all rigid. This violates both low-rank
finiteness conclusions once finite generation
is omitted.
\end{example}
\srcs{source~03, Section~7.3; source~04, Example~11.2 (its rigidity clause, and ``both low-rank finiteness conclusions'', those of \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} and \cref{nee:ci:cor:rigid-general}, are its additions).}

The failed step is precise: a positive cyclic
subgroup of $\QQ$ has infinite index, so finitely
many integer thresholds no longer suffice.
The example is only an algebraic limitation.
It makes no claim that a language with infinitely
many separate function symbols has the same
Replacement behavior as a finite signature or
as one uniform action evaluator. (Source~04, Section~11.2.)

\section{A finer classification at a fixed stabilizer}\label{nee:ci:sec:fixed}

The global countably infinite case hides a useful finiteness theorem.
For a fixed subgroup $H\leq G$, let $Q_H=G/H$, let $N_H$ be the
image of $P$ in $Q_H$, and let $V_H=N_H\cap(-N_H)$. Define
\[
d_H=\rank(Q_H/V_H).
\]
The new unit group $V_H$ can be larger than the image of $U$:
passing to a quotient can make additional translations invertible.

\begin{theorem}[Finite or continuum at fixed isotropy]\label{nee:ci:thm:fixed}
Among connected action types whose localization stabilizer is $H$,
the number is one if $d_H=0$, finite if $d_H=1$, and continuum
if $d_H\geq2$.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~04, Theorem~4.1.}
\begin{proof}
Every invariant subset is the inverse image of one in
$Q=Q_H/V_H$, invariant under the sharp monoid $N$ induced by
$N_H$. Translation classes correspond exactly under this quotient.
If $d_H=0$, $Q$ is finite. Its generating submonoid is a group,
and sharpness forces $Q=0$. There is only the full carrier.
The construction of \cref{nee:ci:thm:cantor} in the quotient gives the
continuum alternative when $d_H\geq2$.

Suppose $d_H=1$. Choose a positive height $\ell:Q\to\ZZ$ and an
infinite-order $p\in N$. The quotient $Q/\ZZ p$ is finite.
The image of $N$ in this finite group is the whole group: it
generates the group, and a submonoid of a finite group is a subgroup.
Choose $n_r\in N$ in each coset. Every sufficiently high element
of that coset is $n_r+kp$ with $k\geq0$, so belongs to $N$.
Thus
\[
F=\{x\in Q:\ell(x)\geq0\}\setminus N
\]
is finite, since height fibers in a rank-one finitely generated
group are finite.

A nonempty proper invariant subset $S$ cannot be unbounded below
in height. Otherwise one of the finitely many $\ZZ p$-cosets
contains points of $S$ arbitrarily far below; $+p$ invariance
fills that coset, and addition by the $n_r$'s fills all cosets.
Translate a point of minimum height in $S$ to zero. The normalized
carrier satisfies
\[
N\subseteq S\subseteq\{x:\ell(x)\geq0\}.
\]
It is $N\cup E$ for some $E\subseteq F$, leaving only finitely
many possibilities. Include the full carrier as one further type.
\end{proof}

This proof gives a finite method for a fixed rank-one quotient:
enumerate subsets of $F$, retain the generator-invariant ones,
and identify translations. The global infinity in low rank can
come from countably many stabilizers instead of complicated
boundaries inside a single quotient.

\begin{corollary}[Rigid-component dichotomy]\label{nee:ci:cor:rigid-general}
There are finitely many rigid connected action types when
$\rank(G/U)\leq1$, and continuum many when $\rank(G/U)\geq2$.
\end{corollary}
\srcs{source~04, Corollary~4.2.}
\begin{proof}
Rigidity of a carrier in $G/H$ forces $U\subseteq H$, because
unit translations preserve the carrier. If $G/U$ is finite,
the action is transitive with automorphism group $G/H$, so
only the singleton is rigid.
If $G/U$ has rank one, either $H/U$ is finite or it has rank one.
There are finitely many finite subgroups, all in the finite torsion
subgroup; each contributes finitely many types by \cref{nee:ci:thm:fixed}.
In the other case $G/H$ is finite, again leaving only the singleton.
The higher-rank conclusion is \cref{nee:ci:thm:cantor}.
\end{proof}

\section{A decision procedure from the relations}\label{nee:aa:sec:effective}
\label{nee:ci:sec:effective}

Suppose the input is a finite commutative presentation
\[
M=\langle x_1,\ldots,x_r\mid x^{u_j}=x^{v_j},\ 1\leq j\leq s\rangle,
\qquad u_j,v_j\in\NN^r.
\]
Let $B$ be the integer matrix with rows $u_j-v_j$. Then
$G=\ZZ^r/\langle u_j-v_j\rangle_\ZZ=\ZZ^r/\operatorname{row}_\ZZ(B)$ and its free rank is
$g=r-\rank_\QQ(B)$; $P=\im(\NN^r\to G)$. Define the rational polyhedral cone
\[
\mathcal W_B=\{w\in\RR^r:Bw=0,\ w_i\geq0\text{ for all }i\}.
\]
Commutativity is part of the input; this is not an algorithm for
arbitrary noncommutative presentations (source~04). Following source~04, put
\[
\mathcal W_B^{\QQ}=\mathcal W_B\cap\QQ^r,\qquad
J=\{j:\exists w\in\mathcal W_B^{\QQ},\ w_j>0\},\qquad
K=\{1,\ldots,r\}\setminus J,
\]
and write $B_J$ for the submatrix retaining the columns in $J$.

\begin{theorem}[An effective relation invariant; exact presentation test]\label{nee:aa:thm:effective}
\label{nee:ci:thm:algorithm}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The integer $d$ of \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} is
\[
\boxed{\ d=\dim\spanR(\mathcal W_B).\ }
\]
\item The effective unit group $U$ is generated by the images of the
$K$-generators, and
\[
G/U\cong\ZZ^J/\operatorname{row}_\ZZ(B_J).
\]
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ci:eq:algorithm}
\boxed{\quad d=|J|-\rank_\QQ(B_J)
       =\dim_\QQ\operatorname{span}_\QQ \mathcal W_B^{\QQ}.\quad}
\end{equation}
\item $G$ is finite exactly when $r-\rank_\QQ(B)=0$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently a finite presentation decides whether its connected action types
are finite, countably infinite, or continuum many. No enumeration of infinite
components is required.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~8.1 (``An effective relation invariant'': (i) and the decision); source~04, Theorem~5.1 (``Exact presentation test'': (ii), (iii), the decision, and a second proof of~(i)) and its equation~(5.1).}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source~03)]
The dual of $V=\RR^r/\operatorname{rowspan}(B)$ consists of the vectors
$w$ with $Bw=0$. Such a functional is nonnegative on the image cone of the
generators exactly when all $w_i\geq0$. Thus $\mathcal W_B$ is its dual cone.
For a full-spanning closed polyhedral cone $C\subseteq V$, the annihilator of
the span of its dual is $C\cap(-C)$. Indeed, every dual functional vanishes
on that lineality space; conversely, if all dual functionals vanish on $v$,
both $v$ and $-v$ belong to $C$ by polyhedral separation. Taking dimensions
and applying \cref{nee:aa:lem:cone} gives the formula.

The group rank and finite torsion invariants are computable by rational
elimination and Smith normal form. The span of a rational polyhedral cone is
computable by finite exact linear methods. If $g=0$, $G$ is finite; otherwise
compare $d$ with one. The trichotomy then supplies the answer.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source~04); a second proof of~(i)]
Each $w\in\mathcal W_B^{\QQ}$ defines a homomorphism on $G$, nonnegative on
$P$, which vanishes on units. Thus a generator in $J$ is
not a unit. Conversely, the rational-cone argument of
\cref{nee:aa:lem:cone} identifies the generators outside $J$ with
the lineality generators and makes them units.

There is a useful exact certificate. Infeasibility of
\[
Bw=0,\qquad w\geq0,\qquad w_j=1
\]
gives by rational linear separation a vector $\lambda$ with
$B^{\mathsf T}\lambda\geq e_j$. Clearing denominators gives
an integer $z$ and $v\in\NN^r$ with
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ci:eq:unitcertificate}
B^{\mathsf T}z=e_j+v.
\end{equation}
This certifies $[e_j]+[v]=0$ in $G$. Conversely, pairing
such an identity with $w\in\mathcal W_B^{\QQ}$ forces $w_j=0$.
The separation used here is the finite rational
linear-inequality alternative, equivalently the dual cone
description of lineality in \cref{nee:aa:lem:cone}.

If a unit $u=\sum n_j[e_j]$ had a representation using an
index $j\in J$, a nonnegative witness with $w_j>0$ would
make $w(u)>0$. Thus units use only the $K$-generators.
Killing those generators gives the stated presentation and rank.

For the span identity, add one witness for each index in $J$
to obtain $w^*\in\mathcal W_B^{\QQ}$ positive exactly on $J$. For any
$z\in\ker_\QQ(B_J)$, sufficiently small positive rational
$\varepsilon$ makes $w^*\pm\varepsilon z$ nonnegative.
Their difference spans $z$, proving the identity.
\end{proof}

Smith normal form can recover the torsion in $G/U$.
The algorithm decides the algebraic branch of the foundational
classification; it does not compute the continuum or decide an
arbitrary set-theoretic cofinality inequality. (Source~04.)

\begin{writenote}[Question~23.5, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
\Cref{nee:aa:thm:effective} decides, for a finite commutative \emph{monoid} presentation, which of the three cases of \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} holds for $\tau(M)$. This is the commutative-monoid analogue of \cref{nee:ca:q:decidability}, which asks the same about $\tau(G)$ for finitely presented groups. It is not the commutative case of that question: for finitely generated abelian groups given by commutative presentations the answer was already immediate (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}). Source~04's abstract and README call its result ``a commutative answer'' to Question~23.5; read as the monoid analogue, the claim is correct, and source~04's Section~1.3 itself makes ``no decidability claim for arbitrary groups''. Question~23.5 stays open beyond abelian groups.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{A small exact implementation}

The accompanying program \texttt{verify\_presentations.py} implements one
elementary finite procedure. For each nonempty support
$I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}$, it computes the rational nullspace of the column
submatrix $B_I$. If this nullspace is one-dimensional and has a generator
strictly positive on $I$, the program records its primitive integer vector,
extended by zeros outside $I$. These are exactly the extreme rays of
$\mathcal W_B$; their rank is $d$.

For completeness, an extreme nonnegative null vector must have a
one-dimensional nullspace on its support. Otherwise an independent kernel
direction permits small positive and negative perturbations while preserving
nonnegativity, contradicting extremality. Conversely a vector with a
one-dimensional support nullspace cannot split into nonproportional
nonnegative kernel vectors. The cone lies in the nonnegative orthant and is
pointed; its extreme rays span it. Alternatively, successive perturbations
reducing support give a direct finite decomposition proof. Thus the support
search is exact. It is exponential in the number of generators and is intended
for small, independently inspectable certificates. More efficient polyhedral
algorithms may replace it without changing the theorem.

\emph{Source~04's finite exact algorithm} (its Section~5.2).
For each nonempty support $S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}$, compute
the rational kernel of $B_S$. Retain $S$ if that kernel
is one-dimensional and contains a vector strictly positive on
all of $S$. Extend this vector by zero outside $S$.
These are the positive circuits of $B$.

\begin{lemma}[Completeness of circuit enumeration]\label{nee:ci:lem:circuits}
The union of retained supports is $J$. It suffices to examine
supports of size at most $\rank(B)+1$.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~04, Lemma~5.2. Source~03's completeness argument above (extreme rays) is the same decomposition; the size bound is source~04's.}
\begin{proof}
Take nonzero $w\in\mathcal W_B^{\QQ}$ with support $S$. If the restricted
kernel has dimension one, its support is retained. Otherwise,
take an independent rational kernel vector and subtract a
suitable multiple of $w$ to obtain a vector $z$ with both
signs on $S$. Moving maximally in both directions gives
\[
w^+=w+\alpha z\geq0,\qquad w^-=w-\beta z\geq0
\]
with strictly smaller supports and
\[
w=\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}w^+
  +\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}w^-.
\]
Induction on support size decomposes $w$ into retained
nonnegative vectors, so every index in $J$ occurs.
For a retained support,
$|S|=\rank(B_S)+1\leq\rank(B)+1$.
\end{proof}

Clear denominators and divide by the gcd on each retained
vector. Their sum is an integer grading positive exactly
on $J$, with zero set exactly the effective units.
A certificate consists of this grading and one identity
\eqref{nee:ci:eq:unitcertificate} for each index outside $J$.
The accompanying implementation (source~04's \texttt{verify\_results.py}) uses rational arithmetic
throughout and includes singleton supports of zero columns.

\subsection{Examples}

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{L{0.36\textwidth}cccL{0.27\textwidth}}
\toprule
Commutative presentation & Source & $g=\rank G$ & $d$ & Connected types \\
\midrule
One unrestricted injection & 03 & 1 & 1 & Countably infinite \\
Two unrestricted injections (no relation on $f,g$) & 03, 04 & 2 & 2 & Continuum \\
One permutation and one injection & 03 & 2 & 1 & Countably infinite \\
$f^2=g^3$ & 03, 04 & 1 & 1 & Countably infinite \\
$f^2=g^4$ & 03 & 1 & 1 & Countably infinite; torsion $C_2$ \\
$f^2=g^2$ & 04 & 1 & 1 & Countably infinite; $2$-torsion survives \\
$f^2=g^3h$ & 03 & 2 & 2 & Continuum \\
$fg=\id$ & 03, 04 & 1 & 0 & Countably infinite \\
$f^2=g^2$, $fg=\id$ & 03 & 0 & 0 & Finite; completion $C_4$ \\
$fh=gh$ & 03 & 2 & 2 & Continuum; cancellation forces $f=g$ \\
$h^2=h$ (source~04: $f^2=f$) & 03, 04 & 0 & 0 & One type; injectivity forces $h=\id$ \\
$fg=h$ & 04 & 2 & 2 & Continuum \\
$f^2g=g^2f$ & 04 & 1 & 1 & Countably infinite \\
$f^2=g^3$, $h^5=f^7$ & 04 & 1 & 1 & Countably infinite \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Selected exactly checked relation matrices. All maps commute, and
all are injective. The code of source~03 contains fourteen presentations in total; source~04's code checks its eight rows (\cref{nee:aa:sec:verification}). [write] Source~03's Table~2 (ten rows) and source~04's Table~1 (eight rows, ``Exact tests distinguish the number of symbols from the number of noninvertible directions'', with a column $\rank G$, which is source~03's $g$) merged; the column ``Source'' names the tables in which a row appears.}
\label{nee:aa:tab:presentations}
\label{nee:ci:tab:presentation}
\end{table}

For $f^2=g^3h$, the relation row is $(2,-3,-1)$. The two primitive
nonnegative kernel rays are $(1,0,2)$ and $(3,2,0)$, proving $d=2$ directly.
For $fg=\id$, the relation row is $(1,1)$, and the nonnegative kernel is zero:
the group still has rank one, but there is no irreversible direction. These
examples show why counting equations or subtracting their number from the
number of generators is insufficient.

Source~04's examples: for $f^2=g^3$, the nonnegative kernel of $(2,-3)$
is generated by $(3,2)$. For $fg=1$, the kernel cone
of $(1,1)$ is zero: both generators are units although
$G\cong\ZZ$ is infinite. For $fg=h$, the kernel cone
of $(1,1,-1)$ has dimension two. In the final row
$(15,10,21)$ is a positive integer grading satisfying
both relations.

\section{Complete enumeration for a power relation}\label{nee:aa:sec:power}
\label{nee:ci:sec:enumeration}

Fix positive integers $m,n$ and require that $f,g$ be commuting injections
with $f^m=g^n$. The two generators remain distinguished. Let $g_0=\gcd(m,n)$ and write $\operatorname{div}(g_0)$ for
the number of positive divisors of $g_0$, and $\sigma_1(s)$ for the sum of the positive divisors of $s$. For positive $a,b$, define
\begin{equation}\label{nee:aa:eq:necklace}
\Neck(a,b)=\frac{1}{a+b}
\sum_{q\mid\gcd(a,b)}\varphi(q)
\binom{(a+b)/q}{a/q},
\end{equation}
where $\varphi$ is Euler's totient function. This is the number of cyclic weak
compositions of $b$ into $a$ parts, as proved below; by the symmetry of the formula it is also the number of cyclic weak compositions of $a$ into $b$ parts, and source~04 reads it as the number of fixed-content binary necklaces with
$a$ symbols of one kind and $b$ of the other.

\begin{theorem}[All connected types for $f^m=g^n$; exact common-power spectrum]\label{nee:aa:thm:power}
\label{nee:ci:thm:enumeration}
For the fixed labelled operations $f,g$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The number of finite connected types of cardinality $k\geq1$ is
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ci:eq:finitecount}
C_k(m,n)=\sum_{q\mid\gcd(k,g_0)}q=\sigma_1(\gcd(g_0,k)).
\end{equation}
\item There are $\operatorname{div}(g_0)$ bilateral infinite types, meaning
whole infinite localized coset groups with both operations bijective.
\item The number of proper infinite types is
\[
\sum_{e\mid g_0}\Neck(m/e,n/e),
\]
and in every proper type both $f$ and $g$ are proper (non-surjective) injections.
\end{enumerate}
Thus the exact finite number of infinite types is
\begin{equation}\label{nee:aa:eq:infinitecount}
\boxed{\quad
I(m,n)=C_\infty(m,n)=\operatorname{div}(g_0)+
\sum_{e\mid g_0}\Neck(m/e,n/e).
\quad}
\end{equation}
For fixed $e$, the proper types are explicitly indexed by weak compositions
of $n/e$ into $m/e$ parts, modulo cyclic rotation.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~9.1 and its equations~(4)--(5); source~04, Theorem~6.1 (``Exact common-power spectrum'') and its equations~(6.1)--(6.3), which write $C_k(m,n)$ and $C_\infty(m,n)$ and add the last clause of~(iii) (``the first term counts components where both maps are permutations; in every component in the second term both are proper injections'').}

\subsection{Localization stabilizers and finite components}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the finite and bilateral assertions (source~03)]
The group completion is
\[
G=\ZZ^2/\langle(m,-n)\rangle\cong\ZZ\oplus C_{g_0}.
\]
Both generator images have positive degree under the homomorphism with
$\deg(f)=n/g_0$, $\deg(g)=m/g_0$. Hence $U=0$, $d=1$, in agreement with the
countably infinite total type count from \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}.

A finite component is a whole coset group $G/H$ of index $k$. Write
$T=C_{g_0}$ for the torsion subgroup. If $T/(H\cap T)$ has order $q$, then
$q\mid g_0$. The image of $H$ in the free factor is $a\ZZ$ for some $a>0$,
and $k=aq$. Conversely, for each $q\mid\gcd(k,g_0)$, the torsion intersection
is uniquely determined, while a lift of $a=k/q$ has $q$ possible torsion
coordinates modulo that intersection. These choices give distinct subgroups
and all index-$k$ subgroups. This proves (i).

An infinite quotient requires $H$ to have rank zero, so $H$ is a subgroup
of $C_{g_0}$. There is exactly one such subgroup $H_e$ of each order
$e\mid g_0$. Its inverse image in $\ZZ^2$ is generated by
$(m/e,-n/e)$, and therefore
\[
G/H_e\cong\ZZ^2/\langle(a,-b)\rangle,
\qquad a=m/e,\ b=n/e.
\]
For each $e$, the whole quotient is one bilateral type. Stabilizers are
invariants of labelled isomorphism, so these types do not merge.
\end{proof}

\emph{Source~04's notation for the same group.} Write the group generators of $f,g$ as $u,v$:
\[
G_{m,n}=\ZZ u\oplus\ZZ v\big/\langle mu-nv\rangle
       \cong\ZZ\oplus C_{g_0}.
\]
Its effective positive rank is one. So $G_{m,n}$ is $G$ above, and $G_{a,b}$, for $a=m/e$, $b=n/e$, is the quotient $G/H_e$, called $Q_e$ below.

\begin{proof}[Second proof of~(i) (source~04, ``Finite components: subgroups with a twist'')]
On a finite set both maps are permutations, so connected actions
correspond to index-$k$ subgroups of $\ZZ\oplus C_{g_0}$.
For such a subgroup $H$, set
\[
c=[C_{g_0}:H\cap C_{g_0}].
\]
If its projection to $\ZZ$ is $r\ZZ$, the index is
$rc=k$. Thus $c\mid\gcd(g_0,k)$ and $r=k/c$.
The torsion intersection is uniquely determined by $c$.
A lift of the positive projection generator $r$ may have
any of $c$ torsion residues modulo that intersection.
These give precisely $c$ subgroups. Sum over $c$.
\end{proof}

The finite connected generating function and cumulative count are (source~04)
\begin{align}
\sum_{k\geq1}C_k(m,n)z^k
 &=\sum_{c\mid g_0}\frac{c z^c}{1-z^c},\label{nee:ci:eq:finitegf}\\
\sum_{k=1}^N C_k(m,n)
 &=\sum_{c\mid g_0}c\left\lfloor\frac Nc\right\rfloor
  =\operatorname{div}(g_0)N-\sum_{c\mid g_0}c\left\{\frac Nc\right\}.
\label{nee:ci:eq:finitecumulative}
\end{align}
The sequence is periodic with period dividing $g_0$,
and its mean over a period is exactly $\operatorname{div}(g_0)$.

\subsection{Boundary functions and cyclic compositions}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the proper-component assertion (source~03)]
Fix $e$, and let $S$ be a nonempty proper subset of
$Q_e=\ZZ^2/\langle(a,-b)\rangle$, invariant under both positive coordinate
translations. Its full inverse image $T\subseteq\ZZ^2$ is upward closed in
the coordinate order and invariant under both signs of the period $(a,-b)$.

Every vertical column has a point in $T$. Starting from $(i_0,j_0)\in T$,
translate by a sufficiently large negative multiple of $(a,-b)$ to reach a
column left of the desired one, then move right. Every column also has a
point outside $T$: start outside, translate by a sufficiently large positive
multiple of $(a,-b)$, and use downward closure of the complement to move left.
Consequently there is a unique integer boundary function $h$ with
\[
T=\{(i,j):j\geq h(i)\},\qquad
h(i+1)\leq h(i),\qquad h(i+a)=h(i)-b.
\]
Its drops
\[
c_i=h(i)-h(i+1)
\]
are nonnegative, periodic with period $a$, and satisfy
$c_0+\cdots+c_{a-1}=b$. Conversely any such weak composition defines $h$
after choosing $h(0)=0$, and hence defines a proper component.

Translation by $(u,v)$ sends $h(i)$ to $h(i-u)+v$. Thus it cyclically shifts
the drop sequence. Conversely, once a horizontal translation aligns the
drops, a vertical translation aligns the boundary values. By
\cref{nee:aa:thm:localization}, these are all labelled isomorphisms. The proper
types for $e$ are therefore exactly the rotation classes of weak
compositions of $b$ into $a$ parts.

Apply the orbit-counting lemma to rotation by the cyclic group $C_a$.
A rotation of order $q\mid a$ fixes a composition only if $q\mid b$, in
which case it is $q$ repetitions of a weak composition of $b/q$ into
