$a/q$ parts. The number fixed is
\[
\binom{(a+b)/q-1}{a/q-1}.
\]
There are $\varphi(q)$ rotations of order $q$. Averaging the fixed counts
gives
\[
\frac1a\sum_{q\mid\gcd(a,b)}\varphi(q)
\binom{(a+b)/q-1}{a/q-1}=\Neck(a,b),
\]
using the elementary binomial identity converting this to
\eqref{nee:aa:eq:necklace}. Summing over $e\mid g_0$ proves (iii) and
\eqref{nee:aa:eq:infinitecount}.
\end{proof}

In this proof $(u,v)$ is a translation vector, not source~04's pair of generators.

\emph{Source~04's route through gap words} (its Section~6.3).
For an infinite component the localization stabilizer $H$
is finite and lies in $C_{g_0}$. There is one such subgroup
of each order $e\mid g_0$; its quotient is $G_{a,b}$
with $a=m/e,\ b=n/e$, retaining the distinguished generators.

\begin{lemma}[Threshold and gap normal forms]\label{nee:ci:lem:gaps}
Every nonempty proper invariant subset of $G_{a,b}$ has
the unique form
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ci:eq:thresholds}
S=\bigcup_{j=0}^{b-1}\{ku+jv:k\geq t_j\},
\quad t_0\geq t_1\geq\cdots\geq t_{b-1}\geq t_0-a.
\end{equation}
Its translation class is the cyclic rotation class of the
weak composition
\begin{equation}\label{nee:ci:eq:gaps}
(c_0,\ldots,c_{b-1})
=(t_0-t_1,\ldots,t_{b-2}-t_{b-1},a+t_{b-1}-t_0),
\qquad \sum_jc_j=a.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~04, Lemma~6.2 and its equations~(6.6)--(6.7). The gap word of length $b$ and sum $a$ is the dual description of source~03's drop sequence of length $a$ and sum $b$.}
\begin{proof}
The quotient modulo $\ZZ u$ is $C_b$.
Closure under $+u$ makes each coset intersection a ray,
empty, or full. Addition by $v$ cycles through the cosets,
with wraparound $bv=au$. An empty coset forces all empty,
and a full coset forces all full. Thus a nonempty proper
subset has a ray in every coset.

Closure under $+v$ gives exactly the displayed inequalities.
Their gaps are nonnegative and sum to $a$, and conversely
the gaps and $t_0$ reconstruct the thresholds.
Translation by $u$ shifts every threshold equally.
Translation by $v$ gives
$(t_{b-1}+a,t_0,\ldots,t_{b-2})$, rotating the gaps.
Since $u,v$ generate the group, these are exactly the
translation identifications.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The gap-word count]\label{nee:ci:lem:burnside}
Weak compositions of $a$ into $b$ parts modulo cyclic
rotation are counted by $\Neck(a,b)$.
\end{lemma}
\srcs{source~04, Lemma~6.3; the same orbit count as in source~03's proof above, for the dual composition.}
\begin{proof}
A rotation of order $q$ fixes only compositions that repeat
$q$ times, requiring $q\mid a$. If $q\mid\gcd(a,b)$,
the fixed count is $\binom{(a+b)/q-1}{b/q-1}$.
There are $\varphi(q)$ such rotations. Counting fixed
word--rotation pairs and dividing by $b$ gives
\[
\frac1b\sum_{q\mid\gcd(a,b)}
\varphi(q)\binom{(a+b)/q-1}{b/q-1}.
\]
Use
$\binom{(a+b)/q-1}{b/q-1}
=\frac{b}{a+b}\binom{(a+b)/q}{a/q}$
to get \eqref{nee:aa:eq:necklace}.
Equivalently, replace each gap entry by that many symbols
followed by one separator, reading the resulting binary
word cyclically.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of~(ii) and~(iii), by source~04]
Each $e\mid g_0$ contributes its full infinite quotient
and $\Neck(m/e,n/e)$ proper types. Different $e$'s
have different localization stabilizers.
If $f$ is surjective, so is $f^m=g^n$, hence $g$
is surjective. Both generators are then invertible on
the carrier, which must be the full quotient. The same
argument starts with $g$. Thus the proper types have
both maps non-surjective.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact local symmetry of each boundary}

\begin{theorem}[Boundary periods]\label{nee:aa:thm:boundaryperiods}
For a proper component associated with $e$, put $a=m/e$, $b=n/e$.
Let $p$ be the least positive period of its length-$a$ drop sequence and
let $\delta=a/p$. Then $\delta\mid\gcd(a,b)$ and
\[
\Aut(S)\cong C_\delta.
\]
The local automorphism group of a bilateral infinite type is countably
infinite. Consequently, at $\kappa=\omega$, a proper infinite type of
multiplicity $\mu$ contributes to the definable atom kernel exactly when
$\mu$ is finite; an individual atom in an untouched proper type is definable
from pure reals exactly when the residual multiplicity and $\delta$ are both
one. Bilateral infinite types contribute no atoms to the pure-parameter
finite kernel.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~9.2; source~04, the first paragraph of the proof of its Theorem~6.4 (\cref{nee:ci:thm:automorphisms}), a second proof of $\Aut(S)\cong C_\delta$. ``The definable atom kernel'' here is the pure-parameter one of \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel}.}
\begin{proof}
The drop sequence consists of $\delta$ repetitions of its primitive block,
whose sum is $b/\delta$. Thus $\delta$ divides $b$. Any translation preserving
$T$ has horizontal coordinate a multiple of $p$, and the boundary recurrence
then forces its vertical coordinate. Precisely,
\[
\Per(T)=\ZZ(p,-b/\delta).
\]
The relation vector killed in $Q_e$ is
$(a,-b)=\delta(p,-b/\delta)$. Therefore
\[
\Per(S)=\Per(T)/\ZZ(a,-b)\cong C_\delta.
\]
For a whole infinite quotient, all its translations are periods, giving
a countably infinite group. Apply \cref{nee:aa:cor:finitekernel,nee:aa:thm:atoms}.
\end{proof}

Here $e$ and $\delta$ have different meanings. The first is the order of the
subgroup identified with zero by localization; the second counts nontrivial
translations of an already formed component that preserve its boundary.
A shorter boundary period is an automorphism, not an additional identification
of points. Confusing the two would undercount types.

\emph{Automorphism groups and exact rigidity counts} (source~04, Section~6.4).
With $\mu_{\text{M\"ob}}$ the number-theoretic M\"obius function, put
\[
\operatorname{Prim}(a,b)=\frac1{a+b}
\sum_{q\mid\gcd(a,b)}
\mu_{\text{M\"ob}}(q)\binom{(a+b)/q}{a/q}.
\]
M\"obius inversion on repetition number counts primitive
cyclic gap words by this expression.

\begin{theorem}[Automorphism spectrum]\label{nee:ci:thm:automorphisms}
For each $\delta\mid g_0$, exactly $\Neck(m/\delta,n/\delta)$
proper infinite connected types have equivariant
automorphism group $C_\delta$. Thus there are
$\Neck(m,n)$ rigid infinite types and
$1+\Neck(m,n)$ rigid connected types in total.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~04, Theorem~6.4 (its $e$ is $\delta$ here and its $h$ is $e$).}
\begin{proof}
In $G_{a,b}$, a gap word repeating exactly $\delta$ times
has period $b/\delta$ and period sum $a/\delta$.
The translation
\[
\eta=(b/\delta)v-(a/\delta)u
\]
preserves the carrier and has exact order $\delta$.
Every preserving translation induces a preserving rotation
modulo $\ZZ u$; each rotation has at most one compensating
$u$-translation, since a proper ray has no nonzero
$u$-period. The automorphism group is exactly $C_\delta$.

For the quotient indexed by $e\mid g_0/\delta$, the count
with exact repetition $\delta$ is
$\operatorname{Prim}(m/(e\delta),n/(e\delta))$. Summing gives
\[
\sum_{e\mid g_0/\delta}
\operatorname{Prim}(m/(e\delta),n/(e\delta))=\Neck(m/\delta,n/\delta).
\]
This is the decomposition of a necklace into its unique
primitive repeated word; algebraically it uses
$\sum_{e\mid k}e\,\mu_{\text{M\"ob}}(k/e)=\varphi(k)$.
The full infinite quotients have infinite automorphism
groups. A finite transitive abelian action has automorphism
group its carrier group, so only the singleton is rigid.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[On the indices of \cref{nee:ci:thm:automorphisms}, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
The theorem fixes the automorphism order $\delta$ and sums over the localization quotients $e$ in which a gap word can repeat exactly $\delta$ times; a component of $Q_e$ with automorphism group $C_\delta$ has $\delta\mid\gcd(m/e,n/e)$, that is, $e\delta\mid g_0$, which is the range $e\mid g_0/\delta$ of the sum. It is a statement about the distribution of automorphism orders over \emph{all} proper types, whereas \cref{nee:aa:thm:boundaryperiods} computes the group of each one; together they give source~03's per-component statement a census. The placement's independent check confirmed the census for all $m,n\leq8$ (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:provenance}).
\end{writenote}

\begin{proposition}[Defect profile]\label{nee:ci:prop:defects}
For a proper infinite component with localization quotient
$G_{m/e,n/e}$,
\[
|S\setminus f(S)|=n/e,\qquad |S\setminus g(S)|=m/e.
\]
Thus its two defect sizes determine the divisor $e$,
although they do not determine the cyclic gap word.
\end{proposition}
\srcs{source~04, Proposition~6.5.}
\begin{proof}
Translation by $u$ misses exactly the initial point
of each of the $b=n/e$ ray fibers.
Translation by $v$ misses the successive gaps in
\eqref{nee:ci:eq:gaps}, whose total is $a=m/e$.
\end{proof}

This refines the general identity
$m|S\setminus f(S)|=n|S\setminus g(S)|$ whenever
the two defects are finite. For any injection $T$,
the complement of $T^r(S)$ is the disjoint union
of $T^j(S\setminus T(S))$, $0\leq j<r$;
apply this to $f^m=g^n$.

\subsection{Coprime powers and rational Catalan numbers}

When $\gcd(m,n)=1$, only $e=q=1$ occur, and
\[
I(m,n)=1+\frac1{m+n}\binom{m+n}{m}.
\]
Identify $G$ with $\ZZ$ by $f(x)=x+n$, $g(x)=x+m$. There is exactly one finite
type on $\ZZ/k\ZZ$ for each $k\geq1$, one bilateral type on $\ZZ$, and one
proper type for each normalized subset $\Delta\subseteq\NN$ containing zero
and closed under $+m,+n$. These are the normalized semimodules of the numerical
semigroup $\langle m,n\rangle$. Their rational Catalan enumeration is classical,
with the related simultaneous-core enumeration due to Anderson \cite{Anderson}.
The boundary proof above derives the count directly and also treats the
noncoprime case.

To see the normalization explicitly, $\langle m,n\rangle$ contains every
sufficiently large integer. A nonempty stable subset of $\ZZ$ contains a
translate of it. If unbounded below, it is all of $\ZZ$; otherwise translate
its minimum to zero. Distinct normalized subsets cannot be translates. Thus
this familiar semigroup enumeration describes actual connected action types.

For $f^2=g^3$, the proper normalized subsets are
$\NN$ and $\NN\setminus\{1\}$. The three infinite types therefore have domains
\[
\ZZ,\qquad\NN,\qquad\NN\setminus\{1\},
\]
with $f=+3$, $g=+2$ in every case. This is a complete classification of the
infinite components, not merely three examples.

\emph{Source~04's account of the coprime case} (its Section~6.5).
For $\gcd(m,n)=1$,
\[
C_k(m,n)=1,\qquad
C_\infty(m,n)=1+\frac1{m+n}\binom{m+n}{m}.
\]
All proper infinite components are rigid.
Identifying the completion with $\ZZ$, a normalized
proper carrier is a subset of $\NN$ containing zero
and closed under $+m,+n$. These are classical
numerical-semigroup semimodules, counted by rational
Catalan numbers. Their Dyck-path and core-partition
connections are established combinatorics
\cite{GorskyMazin,Johnson}; the present application
puts them inside the full injection-action classification.

For a direct coprime count, a binary word has $m$
steps of weight $n$ and $n$ of weight $-m$.
Partial sums before the final step are distinct: an
equality would give a proper subword with symbol counts
in ratio $m:n$, impossible by coprimality.
Exactly one cyclic rotation starts after the minimum
and has nonnegative partial sums. Each rotation orbit
has length $m+n$, yielding the rational Catalan count.

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
$(m,n)$ & Bilateral infinite types & Proper infinite types & Total infinite types \\
\midrule
$(2,3)$ & 1 & 2 & 3 \\
$(2,2)$ & 2 & 3 & 5 \\
$(2,4)$ & 2 & 4 & 6 \\
$(3,3)$ & 2 & 5 & 7 \\
$(3,6)$ & 2 & 11 & 13 \\
$(4,4)$ & 3 & 13 & 16 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Exact consequences of \cref{nee:aa:thm:power}, also checked by finite
composition enumeration. Generator labels are fixed throughout. (Source~03, Table~3.)}
\label{nee:aa:tab:power}
\end{table}

For $(m,n)=(2,4)$, the trivial localization stabilizer gives drop sequences
$(4,0)$, $(3,1)$, and $(2,2)$ up to rotation. Their automorphism groups have
orders $1,1,2$. The order-two localization stabilizer adds one proper ray
type; two bilateral types bring the total to six. The example exhibits the
localization and boundary-symmetry distinction concretely.

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
$(m,n)$&Infinite types&Rigid infinite types&Finite count at size $k$\\
\midrule
$(1,1)$&2&1&1\\
$(2,3)$&3&2&1\\
$(3,4)$&6&5&1\\
$(2,2)$&5&2&$1$ odd; $3$ even\\
$(2,4)$&6&3&$1$ odd; $3$ even\\
$(3,3)$&7&4&$1$ if $3\nmid k$; $4$ otherwise\\
$(4,4)$&16&10&$\sigma_1(\gcd(4,k))$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Exact component counts with both maps distinguished.
The infinite count includes the full quotient components. (Source~04, Table~2.)}
\label{nee:ci:tab:counts}
\end{table}

\subsection{All finite actions, with and without labelled vertices}

Let $L_N(m,n)$ count the ordered pairs of commuting
permutations on the fixed set $\{1,\ldots,N\}$ satisfying
$f^m=g^n$, without requiring connectedness.

\begin{corollary}[Disconnected finite actions]\label{nee:ci:cor:allfinite}
As formal power series,
\begin{align}
\sum_{N\geq0}\frac{L_N(m,n)}{N!}z^N
 &=\prod_{c\mid g_0}(1-z^c)^{-1},\label{nee:ci:eq:labelled}\\
\sum_{N\geq0}U_N(m,n)z^N
 &=\prod_{k\geq1}(1-z^k)^{-\sigma_1(\gcd(g_0,k))},
\label{nee:ci:eq:unlabelled}
\end{align}
where $U_N(m,n)$ counts isomorphism classes of arbitrary
finite actions.
\end{corollary}
\srcs{source~04, Corollary~6.6 and its equations~(6.8)--(6.9).}
\begin{proof}
A connected type on $k$ points has $k$ automorphisms,
so has $(k-1)!$ realizations on a fixed labelled set.
Decomposition into unordered connected components gives
\[
\sum_{N\geq0}\frac{L_N}{N!}z^N
=\exp\!\left(\sum_{k\geq1}C_k\frac{z^k}{k}\right).
\]
But
\[
\sum_{k\geq1}C_k\frac{z^k}{k}
=\sum_{c\mid g_0}\sum_{j\geq1}\frac{z^{cj}}j
=-\sum_{c\mid g_0}\log(1-z^c).
\]
For unlabelled actions each connected type may occur
with arbitrary finite multiplicity, giving the second
product of geometric series.
\end{proof}

An independent count uses $G_{m,n}\cong\ZZ\times C_{g_0}$:
choose $t$ with $t^{g_0}=1$, then any permutation
commuting with $t$. Each conjugacy class of $t$
contributes exactly $N!$ pairs. Its cycle lengths
divide $g_0$, producing the same partition product.
Thus finite labelled counts depend only on the gcd,
although the infinite counts depend on both exponents.

\section{Replacement, Collection, and reflection}\label{nee:aa:sec:replacement}
\label{nee:ci:sec:profile}
\label{nee:ci:sec:thresholds}
\label{nee:ci:sec:collection}

\subsection{The fixed-action criterion}

The following criterion is inherited from the repository's component-profile
analysis \cite{RepoNamingIII}. We give a short proof using the new rooted codes,
so the application does not depend on an unproved coding assertion.
For a fixed finite injective signature let
\[
D_\kappa=\bigcup\{A_t:|A_t|<\kappa\},
\]
where $A_t$ is the entire unpointed component-type block.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-action Replacement; established criterion]\label{nee:aa:thm:profile}
\label{nee:ci:thm:profile}
For $\kappa>\omega$, the expansion satisfies Replacement exactly when
$|D_\kappa|<\kappa$. At $\kappa=\omega$, it satisfies Replacement exactly when
every connected component is finite and $D_\omega$ is finite.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~10.1; source~04, Theorem~8.1 (``Component-block criterion''). Both credit it as prior work: it is \cref{nee:thm:profile} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} and \cref{nee:ca:thm:profile} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}. Source~03's proof goes through the rooted codes of Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:codes}, source~04's through Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s enumeration graphs.}
\begin{proof}
For a pure rooted code $c$, the formula
\[
\operatorname{Type}(c,a)\ \Longleftrightarrow\
\exists b\,[\Code(c,b)\land b\text{ is connected to }a]
\]
recognizes the full unpointed type block represented by $c$, or the empty
class if the code is not realized. Connectedness uses finite signed paths.
Define a total set-valued selector on $\mathscr R$ by taking that exact atom
class when there is an internal set containing exactly it, and taking
$\varnothing$ otherwise. Extensionality gives uniqueness, and the existence
test is a first-order quantifier over model sets. This selector is definable
without presuming its range exists internally. Its range kernels have union
exactly $D_\kappa$. Replacement would make the range an internal set, proving
$|D_\kappa|<\kappa$.

At $\kappa=\omega$, an infinite connected component also gives the
atom-valued word-evaluation counterexample described after
\cref{nee:aa:thm:atoms}; so finiteness of every component is necessary.

For sufficiency, let a formula define unique outputs on an internal domain
$w$, with parameter tuple $\bar a$. Put
$B=\kerat(w)\cup\bigcup_i\kerat(a_i)$. If $\kappa>\omega$, then
$|\Sat(B)|\leq\max(\aleph_0,|B|)<\kappa$; the harmless empty case is
immediate. By \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel,nee:aa:cor:finitekernel}, all output kernels lie in
$\Sat(B)\cup D_\kappa$, which is small. At the finite cutoff, $B$ is finite,
all components are finite, and $D_\omega$ is finite, giving the same conclusion.
The external range set consequently has a small atom kernel and is internal.
This proves each Replacement instance.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[On the finite-cutoff step of the proof above, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
At $\kappa=\omega$, \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel} puts the output kernels inside $\Sat(B)\cup D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega$, and \cref{nee:aa:cor:finitekernel} identifies $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega$ with $D_\omega$ only for $\kappa>\omega$. When every component is finite, the two agree at $\kappa=\omega$ as well: then $\delta_t\leq|X_t|<\omega$, so $\mu_t\delta_t<\omega$ iff $\mu_t<\omega$ iff the block $A_t$ is finite. So the step ``giving the same conclusion'' is correct under the hypothesis that all components are finite, which is the hypothesis in force there. (The write's reading of the proof; source~03 leaves this step to the reader.)
\end{writenote}

\emph{Source~04's setting} (its Section~8).
Every component of the named atom graph is countable:
from one atom there are finitely many forward and partial
inverse generator steps at each finite distance. Code
connected types by actions on a positive finite ordinal
or on $\NN$, quotienting the pure set of codes by
generator-preserving isomorphism. Let $\mathcal T_r$ be the
resulting pure set of types.
For $t\in\mathcal T_r$, define externally
\[
B_t=\{a\in A:\text{the component of }a\text{ has type }t\},
\qquad
D_\kappa=\bigcup_{\substack{t\in\mathcal T_r\\|B_t|<\kappa}}B_t.
\]
The cutoff is applied to the entire block of all components
of one type, not to a single component.
The following criterion and its basic symmetry mechanism
are prior work in ProveIt \cite[Parts I--II]{RepoNamingIII}.
We include a complete proof to make the new threshold
corollaries independent of an unchecked import.

\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
\emph{Uniform recognition of a type.}
Given a pure type code $t$ and an atom $a$, quantify
over a representative code and an injection of its domain
into the atoms whose range contains $a$. Require
equivariance for every named generator and closure of the
range under incoming edges:
\[
\forall b\bigl(\At(b)\land J_i(b)\in\operatorname{ran}(h)
  \ \Longrightarrow\ b\in\operatorname{ran}(h)\bigr).
\]
The range is connected and closed under both directions,
so is the whole component. Conversely, an actual component
enumeration witnesses these conditions.
Its graph has countably many atoms and is therefore
internal for $\kappa>\omega$. For finite types it is
internal even at $\kappa=\omega$. This is a uniform
first-order definition; no instance of expanded
Replacement is used to create the enumeration.

\emph{Necessity at an uncountable cutoff.}
On the internal pure set $\mathcal T_r$, define a functional
relation that sends $t$ to the set of all atoms of its
type if that whole set exists internally, and to the
empty set otherwise. Extensionality gives uniqueness.
The outputs are $B_t$ for the small blocks, and
empty sets for the others. Replacement followed by Union
would collect $D_\kappa$ internally, forcing the bound.

\emph{Necessity at the finite cutoff.}
Fix an atom $a$. On the pure set of finite words in
forward and formal inverse generator symbols, evaluate
the endpoint from $a$ when the path exists, and return
$a$ otherwise. A successful path has a finite
witnessing graph; injectivity makes inverse steps unique.
This is an internally definable total function whose
range is the component of $a$. Replacement would make
it an internal, hence finite, atom set. With all components
now finite, the type-block selector on finite type codes
also forces $D_\omega$ finite.

\emph{Sufficiency.}
Suppose a fixed expanded formula with parameter tuple
$\bar a$ specifies a unique $y_x$ for each $x$ in
an internal domain $w$. Let $\operatorname{cl}(B)$
be the union of components meeting an atom set $B$, and put
\[
H=\operatorname{cl}\!\left(
\kerat(w)\cup\bigcup_j\kerat(a_j)\right)\cup D_\kappa.
\]
At an uncountable cutoff, closing a set of size
$\lambda<\kappa$ under countable components has size at
most $\max(\lambda,\aleph_0)<\kappa$.
At the finite cutoff, closing finitely many atoms is
finite because their components are finite. Thus $H$
is small in the relevant sense and is component-closed.

Suppose $b\in\kerat(y_x)\setminus H$.
Its block has size at least $\kappa$.
At an uncountable cutoff it therefore has at least
$\kappa$ components, since each component is countable.
At the finite cutoff it has infinitely many components
of the same finite size. Choose a component of the same
type disjoint from $H\cup\kerat(y_x)$.
Swap it with the component containing $b$, through an
isomorphism and its inverse, fixing all other components.
This permutation commutes with every named generator.
Its recursive lift is an automorphism of the expanded
model fixing $x,\bar a$, but changing the kernel of
$y_x$. This contradicts uniqueness.

Every output kernel is therefore contained in $H$.
Ambient Replacement collects the range of the fixed
formula; that range has atom kernel inside $H$, so is
internal. This proves the entire expanded scheme.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why singular cardinals cause no hidden step]\label{nee:ci:rem:singular}
Component closure uses a \emph{uniform countable} bound.
It does not assume that every union of fewer than
$\kappa$ sets of size below $\kappa$ is small.
That false assumption would erase precisely the
cofinality obstruction appearing next.
\end{remark}
\srcs{source~04, Remark~8.2.}

\subsection{Sharp universal thresholds from the presentation}

The preceding criterion translates a supply of component
types into exact thresholds. This supply argument is
already present in the free-signature repository setting;
the new type classification determines it for all the
commutative relations considered here. (Source~04, Section~9.)

\begin{theorem}[Universal Replacement classification]\label{nee:aa:thm:universal}
\label{nee:ci:thm:summary-replacement}
\label{nee:ci:thm:threshold}
Fix a finitely generated commutative monoid $M$ and an infinite cardinal
$\kappa$. The canonical expansions preserve Replacement for \emph{every}
action by injective maps on every atom set $|A|\geq\kappa$, after the recursive lifts of a finite generating tuple are named, exactly as follows:
\begin{equation}\label{nee:aa:eq:universal}
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Algebraic case}&\text{Condition on }\kappa\\\hline
G\text{ finite}&\text{every infinite }\kappa\\
G\text{ infinite},\ d\leq1&\cf(\kappa)>\aleph_0\\
d\geq2&\cf(\kappa)>\cont
\end{array}}
\end{equation}
Failure of the stated condition is witnessed on any prescribed atom set of
cardinality at least $\kappa$. Every negative case admits an action faithful on the
effective monoid $P$, in which an element acts
bijectively precisely when it is a unit of $P$.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~03, Theorem~10.2 and its equation~(6); source~04, Theorem~1.2 (``Universal Replacement, summarized'') and Theorem~9.1 (``Uniform preservation and sharp witnesses'', which adds the last sentence).}
\begin{proof}
If $G$ is finite, there are finitely many finite connected types. A finite
union of small blocks is small at every infinite cutoff, so
\cref{nee:aa:thm:profile} applies, including at $\omega$.

Suppose $G$ is infinite. At $\kappa=\omega$, its translation action on $G$
is an infinite connected permitted action and violates Replacement. It can
be installed on countably many atoms with fixed-point filler on the rest.

Now let $\kappa>\omega$. By \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}, the number $\tau$ of possible
types is $\aleph_0$ or $\cont$. If $\cf(\kappa)>\tau$, any union of at most
$\tau$ sets individually of size below $\kappa$ has size below $\kappa$.
Hence $D_\kappa$ is small for every action.

For sharpness assume $\eta=\cf(\kappa)\leq\tau$. Choose $\eta$ distinct
nontrivial connected types, and infinite cardinals $\lambda_i<\kappa$,
$i<\eta$, with cardinal sum $\kappa$. At a regular uncountable cutoff,
take $\kappa$ many indices and $\lambda_i=\aleph_0$; at a singular cutoff,
take a cofinal sequence of infinite cardinals. Give type $i$ exactly
$\lambda_i$ copies. Its block has size $\lambda_i<\kappa$, since each
component is countable. Their union has size $\kappa$. Add at least $\kappa$
fixed atoms, using a separate part of the available atom set, and put any
remaining atoms there. This is possible even when $|A|=\kappa$ by splitting
an infinite set into two equicardinal parts. The fixed type was not selected,
so it does not alter the small blocks. Now $|D_\kappa|\geq\kappa$, and the
selector in \cref{nee:aa:thm:profile} is a concrete failed instance.
(Source~03's proof does not address the last sentence of the theorem; source~04's does.)
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
If $P$ is finite, it is the finite group $G$.
There are finitely many component types, all finite.
The small-block union is a finite union of small sets,
so \cref{nee:aa:thm:profile} applies at every infinite cutoff.

Let $P$ be infinite and write $\tau=\tau(M)$.
If $\cf(\kappa)>\tau$, the cutoff is uncountable.
The union of at most $\tau$ blocks, each of size
below $\kappa$, has size below $\kappa$.
The criterion proves universal preservation.

For the converse suppose first that
$\kappa>\omega$ and $\delta=\cf(\kappa)\leq\tau$.
Choose $\delta$ distinct connected types $t_\alpha$,
omitting the singleton and regular-$P$ types.
Choose infinite cardinals $\lambda_\alpha<\kappa$
with $\sum_{\alpha<\delta}\lambda_\alpha=\kappa$.
At a regular uncountable cutoff take
$\lambda_\alpha=\aleph_0$; at a singular cutoff use
a cofinal sequence of infinite cardinals.
Realize exactly $\lambda_\alpha$ copies of $t_\alpha$.
Each whole block has size $\lambda_\alpha<\kappa$,
but their union has size $\kappa$.
On unused atoms use the trivial action.
The selector in the necessity proof of
\cref{nee:aa:thm:profile} is an explicit failed Replacement instance.

Reserve additionally one countable component carrying
the regular action of $P$ on itself. It is faithful.
Every unit acts bijectively, whereas a nonunit translation
misses $0$ in this component. This enforces exactly
the stated invertibility behavior without altering the
bad blocks, because its type was excluded from them.

At $\kappa=\omega$, the regular component is infinite;
the finite-word endpoint function already witnesses
failure. Finally substitute $\tau=\aleph_0$ or
$\cont$ from \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}.
\end{proof}

For two free commuting injections, $d=2$. At $\kappa=\aleph_1$, some actions
therefore destroy Replacement. Requiring $f^2=g^3$ changes $d$ to one, and
\emph{every} permitted action then preserves Replacement at that same cutoff.
An additional independent injection restores $d=2$. In contrast, at
$\kappa=\aleph_\omega$ the countable-type case can already fail, since its
cofinality is countable. These are exact consequences of the relations and
the cutoff, not consequences of the individual lifts ceasing to be elementary.

\emph{What the common-power relation changes} (source~04, Section~9.1).
For every positive $m,n$, the relation $f^m=g^n$
places the effective monoid in the rank-one case.
Thus every expansion by those two commuting injections
preserves Replacement exactly when
\[
\cf(\kappa)>\aleph_0.
\]
This includes the case where both maps are proper
injections, as the sharp witness construction permits.
Two unconstrained commuting injections instead have
rank two and require
\[
\cf(\kappa)>\cont.
\]
For instance, at $\kappa=\aleph_1$ every common-power
action preserves Replacement, while some actions of
two free commuting injections fail it. The latter
claim uses only $\aleph_1\leq\cont$, not the
continuum hypothesis.

\emph{Individual preservation is more flexible} (source~04, Section~9.2).
Failure of the universal condition does not mean that
every action fails. A trivial action preserves
Replacement at every cutoff.
More structurally, an action with every realized
type block of size at least $\kappa$ has
$D_\kappa=\varnothing$, hence preserves Replacement
at every uncountable cutoff. The obstruction lies
in having too many individually small recognizable
blocks, rather than in the mere existence of many
types.

The answer is independent of finite generating lists.
Every generator in one list is a finite word in another,
and the recursive lifts obey the same word identities.
Changing the finite list is a definitional expansion
or reduct and leaves the full scheme unchanged.

\begin{writenote}[Consistency with Parts~\ref{nee:part:one} and~\ref{nee:ca:part}, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
\Cref{nee:aa:thm:universal} contains the earlier universal thresholds of this report as special cases, and agrees with each. One injection ($M=\NN$, $d=1$): $\cf(\kappa)>\aleph_0$, the case $r=1$ of \cref{nee:thm:robust}. Commuting $s$ permutations and $t$ injections ($M=\ZZ^s\times\NN^t$, presented with a generator for the inverse of each permutation, whose lift is definable from the permutation's lift, so that naming it changes no scheme; $G=\ZZ^{s+t}$, $U=\ZZ^s$, $d=t$): $\cf(\kappa)>\omega$ for $t\leq1$ and $\cf(\kappa)>\cont$ for $t\geq2$, which is \cref{nee:ca:thm:mixed} (with \cref{nee:ca:thm:commuting} for $t=0$). A finite abelian group named by generators (their inverses are positive powers): every infinite $\kappa$, \cref{nee:ca:cor:finite-group}. An infinite finitely generated abelian group named by generators (with their inverses, as before): $\cf(\kappa)>\aleph_0$, the abelian case of \cref{nee:ca:grp:robust}, where $\tau(G)=\aleph_0$ and $G$ is infinite. Two non-commuting permutations, the case $r\geq2$ of \cref{nee:thm:robust}, lie outside \cref{nee:aa:thm:universal}, which assumes commutativity.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Collection and reflection: the exact universal table}

Here reflection means the following finite-family scheme. Given finitely many
expanded formulas and an internal set of prescribed objects, there is an
internal transitive set containing those objects, closed under the named
functions, on which those formulas are absolute for parameter tuples from it.
This is the full finite-family reflection convention used in
\cite{RepoNamingIII}; it is stronger than merely finding a set that agrees on one
selected sentence with no parameters.

\emph{Source~04's formulation} (its Section~10).
To connect directly with Glazer and Yao's subject,
we distinguish Replacement from Collection and specify
the reflection principle being used.
Full expanded reflection here means: for every finite
family of expanded formulas and every internal set of
parameters, there is an internal transitive set $t$,
closed under all named lifts, containing the prescribed
parameters, such that all those formulas agree between
the full structure and $t$ on all tuples from $t$.
This is an external scheme of finite-formula assertions.
The reservoir argument below is the existing kernel-model
mechanism of \cite[Part I, \S7]{RepoNamingIII}. The classification
for general monoid relations results from the type
theorems proved in this article.

\begin{proposition}[Established fixed-action Collection criterion]\label{nee:aa:prop:collection}
\label{nee:ci:thm:collection-fixed}
Let $\theta=|I(\boldsymbol f)|$ be the number of component types actually realized by a fixed
finite tuple of injections. Expanded Collection and the stated reflection
scheme are equivalent. They hold exactly when
\[
\kappa=\nu^+\text{ is an uncountable successor cardinal}
\quad\text{and}\quad\theta<\kappa.
\]
\end{proposition}
\srcs{source~03, Proposition~10.3; source~04, Theorem~10.1 (``A fixed-action Collection criterion'', with the condition written $\kappa=\nu^+>\omega$ and $|I|\leq\nu$). Both credit it as prior: it is \cref{nee:thm:collection,nee:thm:reflection} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}.}
\begin{proof}
At a limit cutoff, including $\omega$, base-language Collection already fails.
On a pure domain of cardinals unbounded below $\kappa$, ask for a graph
injecting each cardinal into the atoms. Each individual witness is internal;
a collecting set would require a kernel of size at least every cardinal
below $\kappa$, an impossibility.

If $\theta\geq\kappa$, choose pure representative rooted codes for at least
$\kappa$ distinct realized unpointed types. The relation asking for one atom
of the specified type is total on that pure set of codes. Any collecting
set requires at least one different atom per type and cannot be internal.

Conversely let $\kappa=\nu^+$, with $\nu$ infinite and $\theta\leq\nu$.
Saturate the input and parameter kernels by components, using at most $\nu$
atoms. Include every whole type block of size at most $\nu$. For each remaining
type choose $\nu$ fresh components outside this set. Since a large block has
at least $\nu^+$ components, these choices are possible. The resulting
component-closed reservoir has size at most $\nu$.

Any internal witness involves at most $\nu$ components. Leave the already
included small type blocks fixed. For each large type, move the components
the witness uses outside the fixed parameter saturation into the fresh
reservoir copies. Such partial matches extend to permutations of the complete
component families, since fewer than $\nu^+$ components are constrained.
Choose the corresponding labelled component isomorphisms. The lifted
automorphism fixes the inputs and parameters and moves the witness into the
common reservoir. External Collection now supplies witnesses whose collected
kernel lies in that one reservoir. This proves expanded Collection.

For reflection, carry out the reservoir construction repeatedly. Use full
hierarchy stages $V_{\alpha_n}(B_n)$ over increasing component-closed reservoirs
$B_n$ of size at most $\nu$, with increasing ordinal bounds $\alpha_n$.
Choose the first stage to contain the prescribed objects. At each successive
stage include witnesses for the relevant existential subformulas over all
tuples from the current stage, enlarge the atom reservoir by fresh components,
and increase the rank bound. There are only set-many such tuples, so external
Collection supplies a common rank bound for the compressed witnesses.
The union of the countably many full hierarchy stages is transitive and
closed under the named operations, which preserve rank and each $B_n$.
Its atom kernel has size at most $\nu$. Induction on the finite family of
formulas and their subformulas proves absoluteness. The atom reservoir must
grow at each stage: an automorphism fixing a reservoir pointwise cannot move
outside atoms into that same fixed reservoir.

Finally reflection implies Collection by reflecting both a totality statement
on a given domain and its witnessing formula, with the domain and parameters
among the prescribed objects. Transitivity then puts every input in the
reflecting set, which supplies genuine witnesses and is itself a collecting
set.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
\emph{Limit cutoffs fail Collection in the base language.}
If $\kappa$ is a limit cardinal, choose a pure set of
ordinals below $\kappa$ whose cardinalities are
unbounded below $\kappa$. For $\kappa=\omega$ use
the finite ordinals. Each ordinal in the domain has an
internal injection into the atoms. A collecting set of
such injection graphs would have a kernel containing
at least that many atoms for every size below $\kappa$,
contradicting the cutoff.

\emph{Too many types fail Collection at a successor.}
At $\kappa=\nu^+>\omega$, the recognition formula in
the second proof of \cref{nee:aa:thm:profile} gives, for every $t\in I=I(\boldsymbol f)$, an atom
of that type. Collection would gather at least one atom
of each type in an internal set. Distinct types use
distinct atoms, so $|I|\leq\nu$ is necessary.

\emph{Reservoir compression.}
Assume this bound and protect a component-closed set
$P_0$ of at most $\nu$ atoms containing the kernels
of the inputs and parameters of a Collection instance.
Add every whole type block of size at most $\nu$.
There are at most $\nu$ types, so the enlarged protected
set still has size at most $\nu$.
For each remaining type, reserve $\nu$ components
outside the protected set. Their union with it is a
fixed reservoir $R$ of at most $\nu$ atoms.

Every internal witness meets at most $\nu$ components.
For each large type, a permutation of its component
family can move the witness components into the reserved
ones while fixing the protected set. Such a partial
matching extends to a permutation: the full family
has cardinality at least $\nu^+$.
Choose component isomorphisms to lift these permutations.
The resulting expanded automorphism fixes every input
and parameter and carries the witness into the reservoir.
Ambient Choice and Collection now choose witnesses for
the set-sized domain, all supported on $R$; their
collection is internal.

\emph{Growing reservoirs give reflection.}
First express universal quantifiers through negation and existential
quantification, then close the finite formula family under subformulas.
Build externally increasing stages
\[
X_n=V_{\alpha_n}(H_n)\qquad(n<\omega),
\]
with $H_n$ component-closed and of size at most $\nu$.
Start with all prescribed parameters included.
For every true existential subformula and every tuple
in $X_n$, use the reservoir construction fixing $H_n$
to place a witness over a common $H_{n+1}$.
There are only set-many such tuples; choose a rank
$\alpha_{n+1}$ containing all the chosen witnesses.
Increase ranks further if necessary for nesting and
closure under the finitely many named lifts.
Then $t=\bigcup_nX_n$ is transitive and internally
supported on at most $\nu$ atoms. Induction on
subformulas proves the required absoluteness.

\emph{Reflection implies Collection.}
Reflect both the matrix of a total relation and its
totality assertion. Include the domain $w$ itself
and the parameters in $t$, so transitivity places
all elements of $w$ in $t$. Totality in $t$
provides witnesses there, and absoluteness of the
matrix makes them genuine. Thus $t$ is a collecting
set.
\end{proof}

The countable union of reservoirs is harmless even
when $\nu$ is singular: every reservoir has the
\emph{same} upper bound $\nu$, and
$\nu\cdot\aleph_0=\nu$. The reservoir must grow;
one fixed atom supply could itself occur as a
parameter in a later stage and fail to supply
fresh witnesses. (Source~04.)

\begin{corollary}[Universal Collection and reflection]\label{nee:aa:cor:univcollection}
\label{nee:ci:cor:collection-uniform}
For all actions of a fixed finitely generated commutative monoid, the exact
conditions for Collection and the stated full reflection are:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
G\text{ finite, or }G\text{ infinite with }d\leq1
 & \kappa\text{ an uncountable successor},\\
d\geq2
 & \kappa\text{ an uncountable successor and }\kappa>\cont.
\end{array}
\]
\end{corollary}
\srcs{source~03, Corollary~10.4 (its second row reads ``$\kappa$ a successor and $\kappa>2^{\aleph_0}$''; such $\kappa$ is uncountable); source~04, Corollary~10.2 (``Uniform Collection and reflection''). It generalizes \cref{nee:ca:thm:commuting-collection} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}.}
\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{nee:aa:prop:collection} with \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}. If the possible type
count reaches the cutoff, an action realizing that many types supplies
the necessary counterexample. All its components are countable, so it fits
on an atom set of size at least $\kappa$ after adding filler.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
At $\kappa=\nu^+$, every action realizes at most
$\tau(M)$ types, so $\tau(M)\leq\nu$ is
sufficient. If $\tau(M)>\nu$, then
$\tau(M)\geq\kappa$; realize $\kappa$
different countable types on a subset of $A$.
The fixed-action theorem gives failure.
All limit cutoffs already fail in the base language.
Substitute the exact type count.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Replacement without Collection at a successor]\label{nee:aa:ex:replwithoutcoll}
\label{nee:ci:ex:replwithoutcoll}
Take the free two-generator monoid and
$\kappa=|A|=\aleph_1$. Choose $\aleph_1$
distinct connected types from the Cantor family
and give each exactly $\aleph_1$ copies.
There are still only $\aleph_1$ atoms in total.
All realized blocks have size $\kappa$, so
$D_\kappa=\varnothing$ and Replacement holds.
But $\aleph_1$ types are realized, exceeding
the reservoir bound $\nu=\aleph_0$, so Collection
and full reflection fail. This is an explicit
distinction between the two criteria within the
same successor-cutoff construction.
\end{example}
\srcs{source~04, Example~10.3. It is \cref{nee:ex:jointcoll} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} with commuting injections in place of the two permutations $f,g$ there, which do not commute.}

The new input to these axiom tables is the classification by relations. The
underlying Collection/reflection equivalence is an established result for
this externally choice-based family. This does not purport to settle any
additional choiceless separation problem from Glazer and Yao's paper.

\section{Finite codes, forcing, and new connected types}\label{nee:ci:sec:boundaries}

\subsection{When forcing adds new connected types}

The proof gives more than a cardinal count: it distinguishes
finite descriptions from genuine real parameters. The following
statement concerns a fixed finite commutative presentation
belonging to a ground model of ZFC. Types in an extension are
compared with interpretations of the ground-model carriers.

\begin{theorem}[Type absoluteness and new reals]\label{nee:ci:thm:forcing}
If $d\leq1$, every connected type in a forcing extension is
isomorphic to a ground-model type. If $d\geq2$, an extension
has a connected type absent from the ground model if and only
if it has a new real.
\end{theorem}
\srcs{source~04, Theorem~11.1.}
\begin{proof}
The finitely generated abelian group $G$, its elements and
its finite tuples are unchanged. Every subgroup is finitely
generated, so there are no new subgroups of $G$.
For $d\leq1$, the proof of \cref{nee:ci:prop:countable} describes
each carrier by its subgroup and finitely many integer
thresholds in a finite or virtually cyclic quotient.
These finite data are already in the ground model.
The invariance conditions on them are absolute.

For any $d$, a carrier in a countable quotient is coded
by a real. With no new reals, the normal form introduces
no new types.
If $d\geq2$ and $R$ is a new real, use the fixed
ground-model height and vector in \cref{nee:ci:thm:cantor}
to construct $S_R$. Were it isomorphic to a
ground-model type, that type could be put into a
ground-model carrier $S'\subseteq G/U$; normal
form would give $S_R=S'+t$ for a ground-model
group element $t$. Then $S_R$ would be a
ground-model set, and its height-zero trace would
recover $R$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The theorem does not assert that arbitrary individual
expanded kernel models retain their schemes under every
forcing. The cutoff's cardinality, its cofinality, and
the sizes of the realized blocks can change. It
identifies exactly when forcing can introduce
new \emph{types} for a fixed finite commutative
presentation. The algebraic integer $d$ itself is
absolute.

\begin{writenote}[Relation to Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}'s forcing theorem, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
\Cref{nee:ca:thm:forcing} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} concerns a \emph{fixed} ground action: if $\kappa$ stays a cardinal, the expanded-Replacement verdict is unchanged, because component types and $D_\kappa$ are absolute. \Cref{nee:ci:thm:forcing} concerns the \emph{supply} of types for a fixed presentation: in rank $d\leq1$ no new types appear, and in rank $d\geq2$ new types appear exactly with new reals. The two complement each other: by the second, for $d\geq2$ a forcing extension adding reals has new actions, built from new types, which is how the universal thresholds of \cref{nee:aa:thm:universal} can change while every ground action keeps its verdict; this is the distinction \cref{nee:ca:q:forcing} and \cref{nee:aa:q:forcing} ask to make precise. (Source~04 does not cite \cref{nee:ca:thm:forcing}.)
\end{writenote}

\section{Verification, formalization, and the trust boundary}\label{nee:aa:sec:verification}
\label{nee:ci:sec:verification}

\subsection{What has actually been checked}

The delivered package includes the complete LaTeX source, the compiled PDF,
the exact-arithmetic program, its JSON results, and a reproducible vector
figure. Separate ChatGPT agents reviewed the written mathematical arguments
along three routes: rooted-component coding and kernels; monoid localization and
the type trichotomy; and the power-relation enumeration, including its torsion
and period groups. The reviews found no remaining mathematical counterexample
within the hypotheses stated here. This is an audit of written proofs, not a
substitute for publication review or proof-assistant verification. (Source~03.)

The executed program performs the following distinct checks:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Fourteen relation matrices are evaluated by exact rational support-ray
enumeration and Smith normal form. The predicted group ranks, cone dimensions,
and finite torsion factors are asserted.
\item For all $64$ pairs $1\leq a,b\leq8$, weak compositions are enumerated
directly and grouped by canonical cyclic rotation. The program compares this
count with the divisor formula \eqref{nee:aa:eq:necklace} and with a separately
computed average of fixed-word counts under every rotation.
\item Every enumerated cyclic class has its local stabilizer order computed.
The code verifies orbit--stabilizer and divisibility of the stabilizer order
by $\gcd(a,b)$.
\item Five coprime numerical semigroups are checked by enumerating subsets
of their finite gap sets and imposing closure under both generators directly.
The counts for $(2,3)$, $(3,4)$, $(3,5)$, $(4,5)$, $(4,7)$ are
$2$, $5$, $7$, $14$, $30$, respectively.
\end{enumerate}
The cyclic-composition grid contains $24{,}301$ weak compositions and
$3{,}521$ rotation classes. For $(a,b)=(8,8)$, it finds $810$ classes:
$800$ with trivial stabilizer, $8$ with stabilizer order $2$, and one each
with orders $4$ and $8$. These checks probe torsion and periodicity cases
that a coprime-only experiment would miss.

The general proofs of infinite cardinalities and axiom schemes do not come
from these finite enumerations. The JSON's cardinality labels are applications
of \cref{nee:aa:thm:types}, while its rays and finite counts are directly computed.
This division of responsibilities is part of the artifact's documented
verification boundary.

\emph{Mathematical review} (source~04, Section~13.1).
Separate internal proof reviews checked cancellativization,
localization and translation equivalence, the torsion
issue, positive heights, the rigid Cantor family,
the fixed-stabilizer finite case, the common-power
necklace and subgroup counts, automorphism groups,
and the transfer to Replacement and reflection.
The main conceptual corrections preserved in the
final statements are:
\begin{itemize}
\item units belong to the cancellativization, not
necessarily the original presented monoid;
\item $G/U$ can retain finite torsion;
\item quotient stabilizers require recomputing
the effective unit group;
\item generator-preserving types differ from
actions on labelled vertices;
\item small entire type blocks differ from
small individual components;
\item reflection requires growing atom reservoirs
and reflection of the totality assertion.
\end{itemize}

\emph{Exact computational companion} (source~04, Section~13.2).
The included \texttt{verify\_results.py} uses only
Python's standard library and exact integer or
\texttt{Fraction} arithmetic. Its \texttt{verification.json}
records inputs, witnesses, individual outcomes,
and totals. All \textbf{148 cases} passed:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{Yrr}
\toprule
Independent check&Cases&Objects examined\\
\midrule
Rank and unit certificates&8&8 circuits; 3 unit identities\\
Compositions versus necklace formula&36&1,709 compositions\\
Finite subgroups versus divisor sum&24&3,759 candidate subsets\\
Permutation pairs versus \eqref{nee:ci:eq:labelled}&80&9,888 pair/exponent checks\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The composition cases use $1\leq m,n\leq6$.
The subgroup cases use $C_k\times C_D$, with
$1\leq D\leq4$, $1\leq k\leq6$, and enumerate
subgroups of order $D$. The permutation cases
use $0\leq N\leq4$ and $1\leq m,n\leq4$.
These checks exercise independent finite
interpretations of the formulas; they do not
replace proofs of the infinite or foundational
theorems.

Run with Python 3.10 or later:
\begin{quote}\ttfamily
python verify\_results.py
\end{quote}
To build the document, run \texttt{pdflatex} twice
on \texttt{commuting\_injections.tex}, or use
\texttt{latexmk -pdf}. The bibliography and figure
are contained in the single source file.

\begin{writenote}[Shipped layout and replay, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
In ProveIt the programs and records of this Part are \nolinkurl{code/03-atom-actions-verify_presentations.py} (source~03's \texttt{verify\_presentations.py}, SymPy), \nolinkurl{code/03-atom-actions-make_figure.py} (its \texttt{figures/make\_figure.py}, Matplotlib), \nolinkurl{data/03-atom-actions-verification_results.json} (its \texttt{verification\_results.json}) and \nolinkurl{figures/03-atom-actions-antichain_upsets.pdf} with the preview \nolinkurl{figures/03-atom-actions-antichain_upsets.png} (its \texttt{figures/antichain\_upsets.*}, \cref{nee:aa:fig:antichain}); and \nolinkurl{code/04-commuting-injections-verify_results.py} with \nolinkurl{data/04-commuting-injections-verification.json} (source~04's \texttt{verify\_results.py} and \texttt{verification.json}). Source~04's figure is drawn by TikZ in this article (\cref{nee:ci:fig:upset}). The text above names the delivered files; both programs, run without arguments, write their record \emph{beside themselves}, under the delivered name, and the figure script writes \texttt{antichain\_upsets.pdf} and \texttt{.png} beside itself, so all three must be run on copies or with \texttt{--output}; the commands ``\texttt{latexmk -pdf atom\_actions.tex}'' and ``\texttt{pdflatex} \dots\ \texttt{commuting\_injections.tex}'' refer to the delivered articles, which are not shipped (this article is the merged text). At this write, on scratch copies: source~04's program passed all 148 cases in under a second (Python~3.14.4) and its record is byte-identical to the shipped one; source~03's program, run with SymPy~1.14.0 (Python~3.13.5) and \texttt{--output}, passed its assertions and printed the counts quoted above, and its record equals the shipped one except for line endings and the field \texttt{python} (3.13.5 against the delivered 3.12.14); the figure script, run with Matplotlib~3.10.8, reproduced the PDF except for its creation date (TrueType fonts, no Type~3) and the PNG pixel for pixel. The report's README gives the commands.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{A concrete route into ProveIt}

The repository already distinguishes exploratory computations from results
connected to formal semantics by proved checkers \cite{RepoRoot}. This article
fits that approach. A useful formalization order is (source~03):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Finite signed paths.} Define the partial evaluation relation
for a finite family of injections; prove endpoint uniqueness and the
component equivalence relation. This portion is ordinary graph theory and
does not need a universe of sets with atoms.
\item \textbf{Rooted codes.} Formalize the equality-diagram code, construct
the unique pointed isomorphism from equality of codes, and prove the
multiplicity formula. The key output is a theorem connecting a pure code
to exactly one commuting-automorphism orbit.
\item \textbf{Algebraic localization.} Construct the quotient of pairs,
prove the embedding and group action, and establish the coset-subset
classification with label-preserving isomorphisms.
\item \textbf{Finite cone certificates.} Make the relation matrix and
primitive rays executable data. Prove a checker for the ray-span claim
and connect its output to $d$. The supplied exact script can generate
candidate certificates; it need not be trusted as the checker.
\item \textbf{Power-relation boundaries.} Formalize the integer cutoff
recurrence, cyclic weak compositions, and the finite group action on them.
The period quotient is a particularly concrete algebraic target.
\item \textbf{Set-theoretic interpretation.} Add a universe-level or
first-order-model presentation of atoms, actual kernels, and fixed-formula
relativization. Prove the range-kernel lemmas before connecting the component
classification to Replacement and reflection.
\end{enumerate}

This ordering separates elementary finite certificates from the ambient
set-theory assumptions. A cardinal-valued trichotomy should be stated in an
explicit universe containing the type codes; a schema theorem should be
stated over formula syntax and a defined satisfaction relation for the chosen
model representation. Introducing an unrestricted truth predicate for the
proper-class universe would change the foundations and is unnecessary.

\begin{writenote}[Formal status, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
None of these six steps exists in ProveIt. As recorded in Section~\ref{nee:sec:provenance}, the formula type shared by \nolinkurl{SetTheory/ZF} and \nolinkurl{SetTheory/BoundedConsistency} has no atom predicate and no function symbols, and no urelement theory is formalized; no monoid localization, cone certificate or necklace enumeration of this Part is formalized either.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Scope of the resolution}

The paper gives a complete answer to the specified
commutative-monoid cardinality question in the
inspected repository. It also gives exact common-power
enumeration and effective certificates that go
beyond a cardinality answer. The older component
and reservoir mechanisms are explicitly credited.
No claim is made to settle Glazer and Yao's
broader choiceless program, to resolve unrelated
Glazer questions, or to have produced a
kernel-checked formalization.

The finite certificates, the ordinary mathematical
proofs, and a future proof-assistant development
are distinct verification layers. The evidence
provided here supports the stated theorems and
their precise hypotheses; historical priority
would require a broader specialist literature
review. (Source~04, Section~13.3.)

\section{Further questions and research}\label{nee:aa:sec:future}
\label{nee:ci:sec:future}

The following questions concern extensions beyond the proved statements.
They are proposed research tasks, not assertions that every wording is a
longstanding published open problem. In particular, neither the noncoprime
power-relation enumeration nor the finite-presentation trichotomy is left
as an open problem here. (Source~03.)

The questions below are directions produced by the proved
classification. They are not all asserted to be longstanding
open problems in the published literature. ProveIt's monoid
cardinality question is answered under the hypotheses of
this article and is not repeated as unresolved. (Source~04.)

\begin{writenote}[How the questions were merged, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
Source~03 asks twelve questions (unnumbered by section, Questions~1--12 there, each followed by a paragraph of commentary), source~04 twelve (Questions~12.1--12.12). Eight of source~03's questions coincide with or contain one of source~04's; each such pair is printed once, with both wordings, and credited to both. The result is sixteen questions, in source~03's order with source~04's four unmatched questions after them, followed by one question added at the write under ProveIt's standing rule for unproved claims (\nolinkurl{docs/incoming/README.md}, Section~4, item~11). Where a question continues one of Parts~\ref{nee:part:one}--\ref{nee:ca:part} or of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/docs/foundations-and-computation/birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (labels \texttt{hset:ns:}), the credit line says so; those questions are not reprinted here. None of the seventeen is answered in ProveIt.
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[Several positive relations and exact inventories; efficient finite boundary enumeration]\label{nee:aa:q:inventories}
\label{nee:ci:q:boundaryenumeration}
For a finitely presented commutative monoid with rank-one group completion,
give an efficient enumeration of all infinite connected types, including
their local automorphism groups. The two-generator power relation admits
the necklace formula above. With three or more generators, identify finite
boundary data that replace the single cyclic composition.

Source~04's wording: for a rank-one effective monoid with three or more
generators and a specified finite isotropy subgroup,
compute the number of proper connected types efficiently
from its conductor and gap poset. Determine which
presentation families admit product formulas or rational
generating functions comparable to the two-generator
necklace formula.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~1; source~04, Question~12.2. For a fixed isotropy subgroup the number is finite and there is a finite enumeration method (\cref{nee:ci:thm:fixed}); efficiency and closed forms are what is asked.}
\noindent
The next concrete deliverable would be a certified enumerator for normalized
ideals of a rank-one monoid with finite torsion, together with a proof of
completeness. A type-count decision alone is already supplied by
\cref{nee:aa:thm:effective}.

\begin{question}[Prescribed surjectivity]\label{nee:aa:q:surjectivity}
Add requirements that selected named injections be surjective or
non-surjective. Classify when the remaining connected-type set is empty,
finite, countably infinite, or continuum many, and determine the corresponding
universal axiom thresholds.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~2.}
\noindent
For connected actions, non-surjectivity is a genuine extra condition: in
the one-injection example it excludes every finite cycle and the bilateral
chain, leaving just a ray. Global non-surjectivity of a disconnected action
does not exclude finite-cycle components. Consequently
the exact cardinality statement cannot simply be reused without modification.
For the power relation, a first step is to read these restrictions from the
drop sequence. (For $f^m=g^n$, \cref{nee:aa:thm:power}~(iii) already shows that in a connected proper type both maps are non-surjective, and in a bilateral type both are bijective.)

\begin{question}[Noncommutative monoids with common multiples; beyond commutativity]\label{nee:aa:q:noncommutative}
\label{nee:ci:q:beyond}
Extend the relation-sensitive trichotomy to specified finitely presented
noncommutative monoids whose actions admit a useful group-of-fractions
localization. Determine which group and order invariants control the number
of invariant subsets of coset actions.

Source~04's wording: for finitely generated cancellative monoids with an
Ore localization, identify hypotheses under which
connected actions still admit a useful subset
normal form. Determine a first family where
nonabelian isotropy and boundary geometry can
both be classified explicitly, before seeking
an analogue of the general rank theorem.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~3; source~04, Question~12.9. Related: \cref{nee:ca:q:decidability} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} for groups.}
\noindent
The rooted-code and kernel theorems already apply to finite noncommuting
injective signatures. The missing part is a usable classification of
components. Group rank and an abelian cone no longer suffice.

\begin{question}[Ordinary definability of rooted codes; definable atoms at the finite cutoff]\label{nee:aa:q:ordinary}
\label{nee:ci:q:finitecutoff}
For a fixed union of labelled components, characterize the pure reals
definable from specified internal parameters. Combine that characterization
with \cref{nee:aa:prop:ordinary} to obtain a concrete description of ordinary
finite-kernel definable closure.

Source~04's wording: develop an exact description of atom-valued definable
closure at $\kappa=\omega$ for common-power actions
and for higher-rank rigid components. Infinite type
enumerations are not internal at this cutoff, so the
finite boundary features detectable by a first-order
formula must be identified explicitly.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~4; source~04, Question~12.5. With pure real parameters, source~04's question is answered by source~03: \cref{nee:aa:thm:atoms} (individually definable atoms), with \cref{nee:aa:thm:boundaryperiods} for common-power types (a proper type contributes exactly when its residual multiplicity and $\delta$ are both one) and \cref{nee:aa:thm:types} for higher-rank rigid components ($\delta_t=1$, so exactly the types occurring once). What remains is ordinary definability, which is this question. It is also what remains of \cref{nee:ca:q:injections} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} and of \cref{nee:q:dcl} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}; Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered}.}
\noindent
This is the unresolved step between the exact reduction in
\cref{nee:aa:prop:ordinary} and an effective classification without arbitrary pure
parameters. It should be studied first for finite unions and for
arithmetically presented families of power-relation components.

\begin{question}[Full fixed-object definability at larger cutoffs]\label{nee:aa:q:fixedobjects}
Extend \cref{nee:aa:thm:fixedobjects} to arbitrary infinite $\kappa$, with a complete
internal description of the orbit of a small support enumeration. Determine
the minimal additional pure data needed beyond its word-equality diagram.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~5. It contains what remains of \cref{nee:ca:q:nonabelian} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} for arbitrary set parameters at $\kappa>\omega$, inside the saturation of their kernels.}
\noindent
For infinite supports, equality of the generated-component diagrams can
miss whether all copies of a type have been exhausted. A promising refinement
records residual component multiplicities exactly below $\kappa$ and records
larger multiplicities by the failure of an internal transversal to exist.
This is a route to a proof, not a theorem claimed in this manuscript.

\begin{question}[Atom ideals beyond cardinal bounds; general atom ideals]\label{nee:aa:q:ideals}
\label{nee:ci:q:ideals}
Replace $|\kerat(x)|<\kappa$ by $\kerat(x)\in\mathcal I$ for an atom ideal.
Find hypotheses under which the rooted-code formula gives an exact
definable-kernel theorem and an exact Replacement criterion.

Source~04's wording: replace the cardinal bound on $\kerat(x)$ by membership
in a specified atom ideal. Separate the roles of closure
under component saturation, unions of recognizable
small blocks, and compression into reservoirs.
Find a necessary and sufficient criterion for a
substantial family of ideals beyond cardinal cutoffs.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~6; source~04, Question~12.6. The same question for named lifts is Question~10.7 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}, for commuting and group actions \cref{nee:ca:q:ideals} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}, and for group actions at the finite cutoff Question~65.1 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:q:ideals}). What is new here is the rooted-code and reservoir form.}
\noindent
Cardinal cutoffs automatically handle deletion of small families of copies.
A general ideal need not. Closure under signed-path images, behavior under
component swaps, and closure under definable unions of pointed fibers should
be separated before attempting an equivalence.

\begin{question}[Weak ambient choice; choice strength]\label{nee:aa:q:choice}
\label{nee:ci:q:choice}
Determine the weakest ambient choice principles sufficient for the type
trichotomy, the kernel formula, and the universal axiom tables, separately.

Source~04's wording: audit the exact choice requirements of the algebraic
normal forms, the type-cardinality statements, the
atom-permutation extensions, and the reservoir
construction. The explicit Cantor family and the
finite certificates should be separated from the
selection of families of component isomorphisms.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~7; source~04, Question~12.10. For named lifts this is Question~10.8 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}, for commuting actions \cref{nee:ca:q:choice} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}.}
\noindent
The explicit continuum family needs no choice of translation representatives.
The global extension of component isomorphisms and the common witness
reservoirs do use ambient Choice. Isolating these uses would bring the
construction closer to the choiceless setting of Glazer and Yao.

\begin{question}[Syntactic complexity of failed instances]\label{nee:aa:q:complexity}
Bound the formula complexity of the type selector and the failed Replacement
instances in terms of the presentation. For a power relation, replace the
general rooted-code predicate by direct boundary recognition and determine
whether the resulting complexity bound is optimal.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~8. The same question for the earlier negative examples is Question~10.9 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}, \cref{nee:ca:q:complexity} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} and Question~65.6 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:q:complexity}).}
\noindent
The problem should distinguish fixed numerical exponents, a pure code
parameter, and a variable family of relation matrices. These different inputs
permit different amounts of uniformity in a single formula.

\begin{question}[Infinitely generated actions; countably generated monoids]\label{nee:aa:q:infinitelygenerated}
\label{nee:ci:q:countablygenerated}
Find additional hypotheses under which an infinitely generated commutative
monoid has countably many connected action types. Clarify the effect of
separate operation names versus one uniform evaluation symbol.

Source~04's wording: find a replacement for the rank criterion for
selected countably generated commutative monoids.
The example $\QQ_{\geq0}$ suggests controlling
the index of positive cyclic subgroups or the
cut structure of the induced order. Keep the
algebraic classification separate from the
choice of naming language in the model theory.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~9; source~04, Question~12.8. The language distinction is the one of Section~\ref{nee:ca:uni:section} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} (separate names versus the evaluator $E$).}
\noindent
The rational-cut counterexample shows that finite rational rank is inadequate.
A plausible starting class consists of unions of rank-one discrete monoids
with controlled index growth; the language must be specified before an
axiom-scheme assertion is made.

\begin{question}[Noninjective maps and branching]\label{nee:aa:q:noninjective}
Determine which parts of the rooted-code theorem extend when inverse images
can have more than one element. Identify a replacement for the freeness of
local automorphisms on a component.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~10. Compare Question~10.5 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} (other bounded-degree signatures) and Question~65.3 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:q:relational}).}
\noindent
With branching, one endpoint no longer determines an isomorphism. Codes
based on finite partial substructures, rather than deterministic signed
words, may require substantially more descriptive complexity.

\begin{question}[Boundary statistics and finite certificates; refined common-power statistics]\label{nee:aa:q:statistics}
\label{nee:ci:q:statistics}
Refine the necklace enumeration by missing-predecessor counts, least period,
and automorphism order. Seek generating functions and compact certificates
for the resulting distributions, including several-relation cases.

Source~04's wording: refine the necklace count by the size of the intersection
$(S\setminus fS)\cap(S\setminus gS)$, by distributions
of consecutive boundary gaps, and by automorphism order
simultaneously. Seek bijections and generating functions
that distinguish these action statistics from classical
area statistics on numerical-semigroup ideals.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~11; source~04, Question~12.3. The distribution by automorphism order alone is \cref{nee:ci:thm:automorphisms}; the missing-predecessor counts $|S\setminus f(S)|$, $|S\setminus g(S)|$ alone are \cref{nee:ci:prop:defects}. The joint refinements are open.}
\noindent
The period computation already identifies the symmetry order of every
individual cyclic composition. The further task is a systematic refined
enumeration, with a proved connection to finite atom-fiber sizes in the
corresponding model.

\begin{question}[Machine-checked synthesis; formalization in ProveIt]\label{nee:aa:q:formal}
\label{nee:ci:q:formal}
Formalize the chain from a presentation certificate through localization and
the type count to the exact Replacement verdict, without importing the final
cardinality or model-theoretic conclusion as an axiom.

Source~04's wording: implement the localization and finite certificates
first, then prove the common-power normal form
and finite enumeration. A later layer should
formalize the first-order model and its expanded
schemes, with a separate theorem connecting
semantic type codes to the internal type-recognition
formula. No semantic shortcut should identify
ambient Replacement with the scheme under study.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~03, Question~12; source~04, Question~12.12. For the earlier results this is Question~10.12 of Part~\ref{nee:part:one}, \cref{nee:ca:q:formal} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part} and Question~65.12 of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\texttt{hset:ns:q:formalization}).}
\noindent
A useful first milestone is the complete $f^2=g^3$ classification and its
three infinite components, followed by the general boundary-necklace theorem.
The finite computation is small; the important work is connecting it to the
semantics of arbitrary components.

\begin{question}[Complexity of the presentation test]\label{nee:ci:q:presentationcomplexity}
Determine the bit complexity of deciding $d$, producing
the grading, and certifying every unit generator. Rational
linear programming avoids exhaustive circuit enumeration,
but the costs of compact exact certificates and Smith
normal forms should be tracked separately. A first concrete
goal is a verified implementation with explicit size bounds.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~04, Question~12.1. Compare \cref{nee:ca:q:decidability} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}, which asks the corresponding complexity for finitely presented groups.}

\begin{question}[Prescribed automorphism groups]\label{nee:ci:q:automorphismgroups}
For a general finite commutative presentation, a subgroup
$H$, and a subgroup $K\leq G/H$, characterize when an
invariant carrier $S$ has exactly $K$ as its translation
automorphism group. The unit image must lie in $K$.
Determine when that necessary condition and a quotient
cone condition are sufficient.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~04, Question~12.4. For $f^m=g^n$ the answer is \cref{nee:aa:thm:boundaryperiods,nee:ci:thm:automorphisms}.}

\begin{question}[Descriptive complexity of the type relation]\label{nee:ci:q:descriptive}
On the natural coded space of invariant carriers,
determine which monoids give smooth translation
classification on particular definable subfamilies.
The existence of a Cantor family of inequivalent
rigid carriers does not by itself determine this
descriptive complexity. Boundary restrictions,
not cardinality alone, should be part of the question.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~04, Question~12.7. Compare \cref{nee:ca:q:subgroupspaces} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}, on subgroup spaces.}

\begin{question}[Forcing and individual expansions]\label{nee:aa:q:forcing}
\label{nee:ci:q:forcing}
Combine \cref{nee:ci:thm:forcing} with precise preservation
hypotheses on the cutoff and block cardinalities.
Distinguish a new action using new real-coded
types from a changed scheme in one fixed
ground-model action. Investigate exact examples
when the cutoff is collapsed or its cofinality changes.
\end{question}
\srcs{source~04, Question~12.11. It continues \cref{nee:q:forcing} of Part~\ref{nee:part:one} and \cref{nee:ca:q:forcing} of Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}; the fixed-action half with the cutoff preserved is \cref{nee:ca:thm:forcing} (the note after \cref{nee:ci:thm:forcing}).}

\begin{writenote}[A question added at the write, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
ProveIt's standing rule for unproved claims moves the following claim of source~03 here; it is kept in place, with a dated note, in Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:pureparams}.
\end{writenote}

\begin{question}[The parameter-free definable kernel of a rigid continuum family]\label{nee:aa:q:parameterfree}
\emph{Source:} source~03, Section~4.3 (Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:pureparams} here). \emph{Claim:} for two commuting injections with $\cont$ pairwise nonisomorphic rigid countable components, one copy of each, at $\kappa=\omega$, every atom is definable from its pure real rooted code, while ``the parameter-free definable kernel is at most countable''; so the pure-parameter qualification in \cref{nee:aa:thm:kernel,nee:aa:thm:atoms} cannot be dropped. \emph{Argument given:} there are countably many parameter-free formulas and each defines at most one object, with a finite kernel; source~03 calls this ``external semantic reasoning, for example after placing the construction in a set-sized ambient model whose atom set is uncountable in a larger metatheory with access to its satisfaction relation''. \emph{Missing:} a framework in which the claim is a theorem, and its proof. In the scheme reading of \cref{nee:aa:rem:semantics} the union over all formulas is not an object of the ambient theory (the note in Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:pureparams}). In a set-sized ambient model $W$ inside a universe that has its satisfaction relation, the counting argument is immediate, but the contrast with $D^{\mathrm{pt}}_\omega=A$ needs $A$ to be uncountable in the outer universe (so $W$ cannot be countable) and needs \cref{nee:aa:thm:types,nee:aa:thm:rootcode} to hold in $W$; what hypotheses on $W$ suffice, and can they be met without assuming a set model of the whole ambient theory? Is there a statement \emph{inside} the ambient theory, one formula at a time, that expresses the same necessity, for instance that no single formula with a fixed finite tuple of parameters defines, as its parameter varies over a set of size below $\cont$, every atom of the family?
\end{question}
\srcs{the write, from source~03, Section~4.3.}

\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions, 4 October 2026, batch~96]
None of these seventeen questions is answered in ProveIt. The ones that continue questions of Parts~\ref{nee:part:one}--\ref{nee:ca:part} or of Part~VIII of \nolinkurl{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} say so in their credit lines: \cref{nee:aa:q:ordinary} is what remains of \cref{nee:q:dcl,nee:ca:q:injections}, \cref{nee:aa:q:fixedobjects} of \cref{nee:ca:q:nonabelian}; \cref{nee:aa:q:ideals,nee:aa:q:choice,nee:aa:q:complexity,nee:aa:q:formal,nee:aa:q:forcing} are Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s Questions~10.7, 10.8, 10.9, 10.12 and~10.10 and Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}'s \cref{nee:ca:q:ideals,nee:ca:q:choice,nee:ca:q:complexity,nee:ca:q:formal,nee:ca:q:forcing} asked again for monoid actions (with Part~VIII's Questions~65.1, 65.6 and~65.12 for the first, third and fourth); \cref{nee:aa:q:noninjective} meets Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s Question~10.5. Conversely this Part answers Part~\ref{nee:ca:part}'s \cref{nee:ca:q:monoids} and, with pure real parameters only, its \cref{nee:ca:q:nonabelian,nee:ca:q:injections} and Part~\ref{nee:part:one}'s \cref{nee:q:dcl}; dated notes after those four questions record it, and Section~\ref{nee:aa:sec:questions-answered} gives the details.
\end{writenote}
